## INTRODUCTION {#cuaderno.1.0}

The Catechism of Christian Doctrine covers briefly the mission of man on earth: he has been created --- one of its concise points teaches --- to know, love and serve God in this life, and to enjoy him eternally in heaven. This is what divine Revelation has established, what the Church, since its foundation by Jesus Christ, teaches to all her children. It is a real joy, then, to discover again --- after a profound theological study --- this simple and precise formulation of the truths of the faith. Nothing has changed. We appreciate better that the doctrine we have piously learnt is reasonable --- *intellectus quaerens fidem*. We can penetrate with rigour into the immense treasure of the faith now, in a way which is explicit and clear --- *fides quaerens intellectum*. But the truth is the same.

This divine Truth --- unique and simple in itself, complex and varied in our knowledge which is so limited[^c00-1] --- is proposed by the Magisterium of the Church, in the name of and with the authority of Jesus Christ, the only-begotten Son of God. To this divine Revelation *man has to submit with faith*.[^c00-2] *Through faith man gives himself entirely and freely to God, he offers him the total "homage of his understanding and his will"*,[^c00-3] *assenting freely to what God reveals. In order to give this response of faith, the grace of God is necessary, the grace of God which acts first and helps us, along with the help of the Holy Spirit, who moves the heart, directs it to God, opens the eyes of the spirit and concedes to "all joy in accepting and believing the truth"*.[^c00-4] [^c00-5]

In all ages, men have been tempted to accommodate the faith and Christian life to the not-always-upright desires of their hearts. Also ***there are many people who are dedicated to obscuring the intelligences of people, to muddying their consciences. They present themselves*** --- our Father reminds us --- ***as the devil has always presented himself: in disguise. They come with manifestations of piety, which are like gold, and underneath they have venom. Heresies that are not novelties, because you can see that the devil is exhausted and repeats himself. But we have to be vigilant***.

On many occasions the Roman Pontiff has denounced these errors, asking Christians to be strong in their faith. *While the sense of religion declines amongst the men of our times* --- Paul VI said on the occasion of the nineteenth centenary of the martyrdom of the Apostles Sts Peter and Paul --- *depriving the faith of its natural foundation, exegetical and theological opinions, taken many times from the most audacious but blind profane philosophies, penetrate here and there in the field of Catholic doctrine, placing in doubt or deforming the objective sense of the truths authoritatively taught by the Church.*

*On the pretext of adapting religious ideas to the mentality of the modern world, they dispense with the guide of the ecclesiastical magisterium, theological speculation is given a radically historicist bent, the testimony of Sacred Scripture is brazenly stripped of its historical and sacred character and they try to introduce into the People of God a mentality which they call "postconciliar", that leaves to one side the firm cohesion of the broad and magnificent doctrinal and legislative developments of the Council, with its treasure of ideas and practical norms of the Church, in order to strip them of their spirit of traditional fidelity and in order to spread the illusion of giving to Christianity a new interpretation, which is both arbitrary and sterile. What would be left of the content of our faith and of the theological virtues which we profess in it, if these attempts, totally condemned by the ecclesiastical Magisterium, were to prevail?*[^c00-6] **

All these tendencies become a serious danger for many people, whose Christian sense of life can vacillate before a truly universal campaign that is carried out with all sorts of means. Human life has God as its centre: this is the truth that our vocation leads us to spread, the only truth capable of orienting the life of men to their supernatural destiny. ***My daughters and sons, we Christians, those of us who have received the gift of faith from God, have the mission of speaking to other men in God**'**s name:*** **They should everywhere on earth bear witness to Christ and give an answer to everyone who asks a reason for the hope of an eternal life which is theirs *(cf. 1 Pet 3:15) (Second Vatican Council, Dogmatic Constitution,*** **Lumen gentium*, 10).***

***With our witness and our word, by our example and our teaching, we have to make Christ known and loved; and, through Christ and in the Holy Spirit, God the Father***: **Him we proclaim, warning every man** --- ***by our witness*** --- **and teaching every man in all wisdom** --- ***by our doctrine*** --- **that we may present every man mature in Christ. For this I toil, striving with all the energy which he mightily inspires within me** ***(Col 1:28- 29).***[^c00-7]

These considerations on our Catholic faith do not pretend to be a complete and exhaustive treatise: only some points of Christian doctrine, which are especially current, are gathered here --- as the Magisterium of the Church presents them. Their study can give us the necessary doctrinal fundamentals to be able to speak of our faith simply and rigorously, demonstrating without equivocation (that can never lead to peace or to union) what the Christian sense of life consists of.

Men have in the depth of their souls a longing for eternal life, and they expect from Christians the clear message of our faith. We will show, with our word and with our life, what the true meaning of human existence is: that which points us in the direction of heaven and which gives at the same time the highest value and the greatest relief to the good things of the earth. We want souls to be saved: to believe what God has revealed --- exactly as the Church, the depository of this divine revelation, teaches it to us --- and that, believing, they can act accordingly.

[^c00-1]: Cf. S. Th., II-II, q.1, a.2

[^c00-2]: Cf. *Rom* 16:26; 2 *Cor* 10:5-6

[^c00-3]: First Vatican Council, Dogmatic Constitution, *Dei Filius*, ch. 3, Dz 3008 (1789)

[^c00-4]: Second Council of Orange, can. 7, (Dz 377)

[^c00-5]: Second Vatican Council, Dogmatic Constitution, *Dei Verbum*, 5

[^c00-6]: Paul VI, Apostolic Exhortation, *Petrum et Paulum*, 22 February 1967

[^c00-7]: From our Father, *Letter*, 24 October 1965, 19

## THE HOLY TRINITY {#cuaderno.1.1}

I am the Alpha and the Omega, the beginning and the end, says the Lord God, Who is and Who was and Who is to come, the Almighty.[^c01-1] We have already seen a few of the mysteries of the Catholic faith which speak of God’s mercy and immense love for man. And in these times when there are growing numbers of people who are abandoning the practice of religion,[^c01-2] and who completely misunderstand this intimate and vital link with God, or who have explicitly rejected it,[^c01-3] we focus our attention on the central mystery of Christianity which reveals the intimate life of God Himself.

The mystery of the Holy Trinity, an ineffable truth which infinitely surpasses the limits of creation, demands a disposition of humble and reverent faith before God’s majesty. From the burning bush the Lord said to Moses: Do not come near; take off your shoes from your feet, for the place on which you are standing is holy ground... and Moses hid his face, for he was afraid to look at God.[^c01-4] Our Father told us: My children, put yourselves before the Lord, and say to him slowly, with all the zeal of your heart, Credo, Domine, Lord, I believe! Adiuva incredulitatem meam (Mark 9:23). But help me, Lord, to believe more and better.[^c01-5]

Revelation of the mystery

The mystery of the Most Holy Trinity was revealed by Christ himself. The Apostle St John says that no one has ever seen God; only the Son, who is in the bosom of the Father, he has made him known,[^c01-6] because no one knows the Son except the Father, and no one knows the Father except the Son and those to whom the Son chooses to reveal Him.[^c01-7] Little by little, with his life and teaching, our Lord revealed this truth to his disciples. For the first time the existence of the three Divine Persons had been shown in the angel’s announcement to Mary: The Holy Spirit will come upon you, and the power of the Most High will overshadow you; therefore, the Child to be born will be called holy, the Son of God.[^c01-8] Later, the Trinity was shown in a visible manner at Jesus’ baptism. St Matthew tells us that immediately he went up from the water, behold the heavens were opened, and he saw the Spirit of God descending like a dove, and alighting on him; and lo, a voice from heaven said “This is my beloved Son, with whom I am well pleased’’.[^c01-9] Then, for three years our Lord taught his disciples to have faith in their divine filiation and in the existence of the Holy Spirit.[^c01-10] His last words, prior to his Ascension into heaven, are a definitive affirmation: Go and teach all nations, baptising them in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit.[^c01-11]

The Apostles preached this truth throughout the world,[^c01-12] and the Church, entrusted with this revelation, proclaimed it firmly in all the Creeds and Councils as the first and last subject, the beginning and the end of faith: We believe in one true God, Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, Creator of all, visible and invisible, by whom are made all things in heaven and on earth. This is the one and only God and this is the one and only Trinity of Divine Substance. The Father is not the same as the Son, but He has a Son who is not the Father. The Son is not the Father, but He is of the Nature of the Father. The Holy Spirit also exists, who is neither the Father nor the Son, but proceeds from the Father and the Son.[^c01-13]

Unity of Essence and Trinity of Persons; one divine Nature and three really distinct Persons: this is the fundamental truth of the Catholic Faith, a truth which is so sublime that it would never have been known to human reason by its own powers, and which even after it has been revealed supernaturally, cannot be fully comprehended or rationally demonstrated.[^c01-14]

The Church, from the very beginning, under the interior guidance of the Holy Spirit, began to reflect on its trinitarian faith. What do we really mean when we say that we believe that God is One and Three? How is God a Trinity and at the same time Unity? The starting point is found in the first words of the Gospel of St John: In the beginning was the Word and the Word was with God, and the Word was God.[^c01-15] God himself said that his Son was his Word, the interior Word which expresses himself completely. The uncreated and eternal Wisdom of God, of which the Old Testament speaks at length,[^c01-16] finally found its fullest meaning. With this Revelation as a basis, the Fathers of the Church spoke of an intellectual generation of the Son of God, who alone is by essence — I AM WHO AM,[^c01-17] Scripture says — and who alone knows himself eternally and completely. The terminus of this most perfect act of knowledge is a most perfect Idea. In our knowledge, the idea is distinct from the knowing intellect and from the object known. In God, who is absolutely simple, however, the eternal Idea which is the product of this eternal act of knowing is not really distinct from the divine nature. Nevertheless, this concept, the substantial Image of God, is — and herein lies the mystery — a subsistent Idea, a divine Person who is really distinct from the knowing Person, precisely because it is known. The divine Person, who in knowing himself intellectually conceives the Word, is God the Father. The Person begotten or conceived by the Father, the eternal Word of God, the perfect Image of the Father is the Son.[^c01-18]

The Church has always preserved this faith. When at the beginning of the fourth century, Arius tried to rationalise the mystery by placing the Son in an inferior position with respect to the Father, the first ecumenical council, the Council of Nicaea, solemnly pronounced what had to be believed: We believe... in one Lord, Jesus Christ the Son of God, the only begotten of the Father, that is, of the substance of the Father, God from God, Light from Light, true God from true God, begotten not made, consubstantial with the Father.[^c01-19] And similarly, years later, when Macedonius and the semi-Arians denied the divinity of the Holy Spirit, the Church again re-affirmed as revealed truth: We believe... in the Holy Spirit, the Lord, the Giver of Life, who proceeds from the Father and the Son, with the Father and the Son He is adored and glorified.[^c01-20]

The three Divine Persons

The first Person of the Holy Trinity is the Father, to whom corresponds unbegottenness and paternity: unbegottenness because He is not born nor created but is unbegotten; for He has origin from no one,[^c01-21] in such a way that whatever He is or what He has He has not from another but from Himself: He is Principle without any principle.[^c01-22] And paternity corresponds to the Father since He alone begets the Son, without the least detriment to His substance: For the Father, in eternally generating the Son, gave him His own substance as the Son himself testifies: “What My Father has given Me is more precious than all else.’’[^c01-23] But it cannot be said that He gave him part of His substance, and retained part of it for Himself, because the substance of the Father is indivisible, because it is altogether simple. Nor can one say that the Father, in engendering the Son, so transferred His own substance, as to give it to the Son in a way that He did not retain it for himself, because He would then have ceased to be a substance.[^c01-24] God the Father, by generating the Son, gives him His nature totally without losing it himself. This is how perfect divine generation is: All that the Father is, the Father himself gave to his only-begotten Son in generating Him, except for being Father.[^c01-25]

In order to express the proper names of each Person, which cannot be attributed to the others, Catholic theology uses the term “notion’’. Thus, the first Person of the Blessed Trinity is called Father, Unbegotten, Principle without principle. The Church also affirms that the Father, besides begetting the Son, together with the Son, breathes forth, spirates the Holy Spirit.[^c01-26] This spiration is called a “notion’’ because it permits the Holy Spirit to be known.

The second Person of the Blessed Trinity is the Son, true God who proceeds from the Father by generation: non factus, nec creatus, sed genitus.[^c01-27] Consubstantial with the Father, for we must believe that the same Son is begotten or born, he is not from nothing nor from any other substance but He is from the bosom of the Father, which is to say, of his very substance.[^c01-28] Being a distinct Person, the Son is completely equal to God the Father because He was not born in time nor has He ever ceased to be... nor, because of the fact that the Son of God was born of the Father, do we call him a small part of a divided nature; rather, we maintain that the Perfect Father begot a Perfect Son but without any diminution and without any division.[^c01-29] All that the Son is, and all that he has, he has from the Father; he is a Principle from a principle.[^c01-30] He is, moreover, the Son of God by nature, not by adoption; and we must believe that God the Father begot Him not through his Will and not of necessity, for there is no necessity in God nor does the Will precede Wisdom.[^c01-31]

Filiation is exclusively a property of the Son. He is begotten by the Father, and together with the Father is the single principle of spiration of the Holy Spirit.[^c01-32] His proper names are Son, Word, and Image: Son, inasmuch as he is begotten by the Father; Word, because his generation is intellectual;[^c01-33] and Image, because the Son alone reflects the glory of God and bears the very stamp of his nature.[^c01-34]

Just as the Father from whom the Son proceeds is eternal, so the procession of the Son is eternal: if the Father is eternal, then the Son is eternal. Consequently, if he was always Father, he always had a Son of whom He was the Father.[^c01-35] This truth is revealed in Sacred Scripture in the words of the Psalm: Filius meus es tu — this is said by God the Father — ego hodie genui te.[^c01-36] This is developed even more fully when speaking of the eternity of the Wisdom of God: From everlasting I was firmly set, from the beginning, before earth came into being. The deep was not, when I was born, there were no springs to gush with water. Before the mountains were settled, before the hills, I came to birth, before he made the earth, the countryside or the first grains of the earth’s dust. When he fixed the heavens firm, I was there, when he drew a ring on the surface of the deep, when he thickened the clouds above, when he fixed fast the springs of the deep, when he assigned the sea its boundaries — and the waters will not invade the shore — when he laid the foundations of the earth, I was by his side, a master craftsman, delighting him day after day, ever at play in his presence.[^c01-37]

Our Catholic Faith also speaks of the mission of the Son: when the fullness of time came, God sent his Son;[^c01-38] and the Word was made flesh and dwelt among us.[^c01-39] He alone took flesh from Mary, admirably uniting human nature with the divinity. Therefore we believe in two births of the divine Word: the first before time, not in body, from the Father; the other, in this last age, when the Word descended from heaven and took flesh of the holy and glorious Mother of God and ever Virgin Mary and He was born of her.[^c01-40] Thus He who in the divinity was Son of God the Father, true God from the Father, was in the humanity, son of man, true man of a mother, with true flesh from his mother’s womb and with a rational human soul, the one nature being united to the other: that is, God and man, a single person, a single Son, a single Christ, a single God with the Father and the Holy Spirit... He ate and drank, slept and, tired from walking, he rested. He suffered with true suffering in his flesh, he died a true death of his Body, and he rose again with the true return of his Soul to his Body; and in that flesh, after having eaten and taken drink, he ascended into heaven and there he is seated at the right hand of the Father, and in that same flesh he will come to judge the living and the dead.[^c01-41]

The Church continues her profession: We believe in the Holy Spirit, who spoke in the Law and announced in the prophets and descended on the Jordan, who speaks in the Apostles and dwells in the saints; and thus we believe in him who is the Holy Spirit, the Spirit of God, the perfect Spirit, the consoling and uncreated Spirit.[^c01-42]

Jesus told his disciples about the Third Person of the Blessed Trinity, the Spirit of Truth who proceeds from the Father, whom I shall send to you.[^c01-43] The soul that lives on faith might ask: But how does this procession of the Holy Spirit take place? What does God say to us having made us share in this truth? Making use of analogy, Catholic doctrine has been developed by considering the respective operations of our intellect and of our will. Just as the study of the internal operation of divine knowledge shed light on the generation of the Word, so a study of the operations of the Will, can somehow shed light on the mystery of the Holy Spirit. God, in knowing Himself eternally, loves himself eternally. The object of his love is also His own Essence, which is known perfectly in the Word. And since it pertains to love to give itself to the beloved, God the Father gives himself completely to the Word with an infinite love. In the same way, the Word, knowing himself as conceived by the Father, gives himself to the Father with the same infinite love which is his and the Father’s. The terminus of this single procession of love — and in this lies the mystery — is a subsistent divine Person, the Holy Spirit, the substantial Love of the Father and of the Son.[^c01-44] We believe that the Holy Spirit, the third Person of the Trinity is God, and that he is one with and equal to God the Father and to God the Son. However, he is not begotten nor created, but he proceeds from both and is the Spirit of both. We believe that the Holy Spirit is neither unbegotten nor begotten; lest, if we say unbegotten we should appear to be asserting two Fathers; and if we say begotten, we would appear to be preaching two Sons. He is called the Spirit, not only of the Father nor only of the Son, but equally of the Father and of the Son.[^c01-45] The joint procession of the Holy Spirit from the Father and from the Son as from a single principle is a dogma of faith solemnly defined in the Council of Florence: In the name of the Holy Trinity, of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit, with the approval of this holy universal Council of Florence, we define that all Christians should believe and receive this truth of faith, and all should confess that the Holy Spirit proceeds eternally from the Father and the Son, and equally from the Father and from the Son he has his Essence and his subsistent being, and from one and the other he proceeds eternally as from a single principle and by a single spiration.[^c01-46]

What is exclusively the property of the Holy Spirit is therefore the procession which makes him a Person. And together with the name of Holy Spirit which Scripture gives in many places, he also receives the names of Love and Gift: Love inasmuch as the terminus of the procession is love; Gift, because in the Holy Spirit the Father gives himself completely to the Son, and the Son gives himself completely to the Father and furthermore, because he was sent to us visibly as a gift from the Father and the Son when the work which the Father gave the Son to do on earth[^c01-47] was accomplished, the Holy Spirit was sent on the day of Pentecost in order that he might continually sanctify the Church, and thus, all those who believe would have access to the Father through Christ in one Spirit.[^c01-48] In this manner the promise of Jesus to his Apostles was fulfilled: if I do not go away, the Counsellor will not come to you; but if I go, I will send Him to you.[^c01-49]

Processions and Relations

The faith of the Church, then, speaks of three divine Persons who are really distinct, and of two processions: the first is properly called generation; the second does not take place by way of generation, but in another ineffable way which the Magisterium simply calls procession.

The existence of processions in God — operations which have a principle and a terminus — is a truth of Catholic and divine faith, revealed by Christ in the Gospels. He tells the Jews: I proceeded and came forth from God.[^c01-50] The Magisterium of the Church and the theologians in their desire to delve into the words of Scripture have frequently used analogy[^c01-51] and have studied the divine processions in the light of operations proper to rational beings.

The two divine processions are immanent processions, that is, they reach their terminus and perfection in the bosom of God without being really distinct from the divine nature, since God is absolutely simple. When something proceeds from a principle of the same nature, that which proceeds and the principle from which it proceeds necessarily agree in the same order and consequently they have between themselves real relations. Therefore, as the processions in God have identity of nature, these relations which ensue are necessarily real relations.[^c01-52] For each procession there ought necessarily to be two mutually opposed relations: one, from that which proceeds to its principle; the other from the principle to that which proceeds. The two opposed relations which follow from generation are called paternity and filiation. Similarly, two opposed relations arise from the second divine procession. But since the procession of love does not have its own name, so neither do the ensuing relations have a proper name of their own. The relation of the principle of this procession is called spiration; and that of the terminus is called procession.[^c01-53]

The Council of Florence teaches that all things are one in God where relative opposition does not prevent this,[^c01-54] that is only mutually opposed relations constitute the Persons. St Thomas, witness to Catholic tradition, explains this statement: two opposed relations must refer to two distinct persons and if any relations are not opposed, they must belong to the same person.[^c01-55] Although there are four real relations predicated of God, only three are mutually opposed: Paternity is opposed to filiation, filiation to paternity and procession is simultaneously opposed to the first two;[^c01-56] thus, paternity corresponds to the Father only, filiation to the Son only and procession to the Holy Spirit only. The Father is not made by anyone, nor created by anyone, nor generated by anyone. The Son is not made, nor created, but is generated by the Father alone. The Holy Spirit is not made nor created nor generated, but proceeds from the Father and the Son.[^c01-57]

Unity of Nature. Circumincession and operations “Ad extra”.

The Trinity of Persons, the duality of processions and the quaternity of real relations exist in God without diminishing the unity of nature. The Catholic Faith teaches: The loving Trinity is a single divinity, the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit are a single principle, a single substance, a single strength, a single power. The Father is God, the Son is God, and the Holy Spirit is God. We do not say that there are three gods, but, with all our piety, we confess that there is but one God. Because, in naming three Persons, we profess with Catholic and apostolic voice that They are but one substance.[^c01-58]

The only thing proper to the Father is to be Father, the only thing proper to the Son is to be Son, and the only thing proper to the Holy Spirit is to proceed from the Father and from the Son, for the Father is not Father with respect to himself, but in relation to the Son; and the Son is not Son with respect to himself, but in relation to the Father. The Holy Spirit is Spirit not with respect to himself but in relation to the Father and the Son.[^c01-59] Therefore, in God all things are one except where relative opposition prevents this.[^c01-60] Consequently, the Catholic Faith affirms that what the Father is the Son is, and the Holy Spirit is. The Father is uncreated, the Son is uncreated, and the Holy Spirit is uncreated. The Father has immensity, the Son has immensity, and the Holy Spirit has immensity. The Father is eternal, the Son is eternal, and the Holy Spirit is eternal. Nevertheless there are not three eternal beings, but one eternal being. Thus there are not three uncreated beings, nor three beings having immensity, but one uncreated being and one being that has immensity.[^c01-61] This admirable Unity in the Trinity is expressed in theology with the word “circumincession’’: The Father is entirely in the Son and entirely in the only Spirit; the Son is entirely in the Father and entirely in the Holy Spirit; the Holy Spirit is entirely in the Father and entirely in the Son.[^c01-62] This presence of each of the Persons in one another without confusion of identity was revealed by Christ to the Apostles at the Last Supper: He who has seen me has seen the Father... Do you not believe that I am in the Father and the Father is in me? The words that I say to you I do not speak on my own authority; but the Father who dwells in me continually achieves his works in me.[^c01-63]

The operations ad extra of God, that is, operations which have their terminus outside the Divinity, are common to the whole Trinity: The works of the Trinity are inseparable.[^c01-64] However, even though the operations are common, they are attributed to each Person in a particular manner as is indicated by Sacred Scripture.

In the mystery of Creation, the Church believes that the Father, the Son and the Holy Spirit, the one God of whom we are speaking, is the Creator, maker and ruler of all things, spiritual and corporeal, visible and invisible,[^c01-65] but the Church attributes a special mode of acting in the work of creation to each Person according to the words of St Paul: there is one God, the Father, from whom are all things... and one Lord, Jesus Christ, through whom are all things have been made.[^c01-66] From him, and through him, and in him all things have their existence.[^c01-67] Everything proceeds from God the Father through the Son in the love of the Holy Spirit.

In considering the mystery of the Redemption, the Church teaches that only the Second Person of the Trinity took flesh from Mary’s womb. But it also states that the Incarnation of this Son of God was the work of the whole Trinity:[^c01-68] a work of the Father because in this the love of God has been made manifest among us, in that God sent his only-begotten Son into the world, so that we might live through him.[^c01-69] And it is a work of the Son who offered himself voluntarily to the Father: upon entering the world he said, Sacrifices and offerings Thou hast not desired, but a Body Thou hast prepared for Me; in burnt offerings and sin offerings Thou hast taken no pleasure. Then I said, “Lo, I have come to do Thy Will, O God’’.[^c01-70] It is also a work of the Holy Spirit because, as we say in the Creed, the Son, was made flesh by the Holy Spirit, of the Virgin Mary.[^c01-71]

The mystery of sanctification, through which God has man participate in his own Life, is attributed in Sacred Scripture to the activity of the Holy Spirit. St Paul indicates this to the Corinthians and in many of his other letters: You were washed clean, you were sanctified, you were justified in the name of our Lord Jesus Christ by the Spirit of our God.[^c01-72] Our Father also told us: Our sanctification, and the sanctification of the souls we deal with, and that of the whole Church, is the work of the Holy Spirit. Love the Third Person of the Most Holy Trinity very much.[^c01-73] The Holy Spirit was sent visibly by the Father and the Son at Pentecost in order to sanctify the Church and to dwell in it eternally;[^c01-74] and for the sanctification of men he is sent invisibly at baptism and in the Sacrament of Penance.[^c01-75] In this way we attribute to the Holy Spirit: the saving power of the Sacraments,[^c01-76] the inspiration of Holy Writ,[^c01-77] the assistance to the Magisterium of the Church,[^c01-78] the sanctification of the entire People of God. The Spirit dwells in the Church and in the hearts of the faithful as in a temple.[^c01-79] In them he prays and bears witness to their adoption as sons.[^c01-80] The Spirit guides the Church into the fullness of truth[^c01-81] and gives her a unity of fellowship and service. He furnishes and directs her with various gifts, both hierarchical and charismatic, and adorns her with the fruits of his grace.[^c01-82] By the power of the gospel he makes the Church grow, perpetually renews her, and leads her to perfect union with her Spouse.[^c01-83] The Spirit and the Bride say to the Lord Jesus, “Come!’’[^c01-84] Thus the whole Church shines forth as a people made one by virtue of the unity of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit.[^c01-85]-[^c01-86]

The mystery of the Most Holy Trinity is the infinitely transcendent centre of the life of men, of history, of the entire creation. And the Church does nothing without first invoking the Most Blessed Trinity. Learn to praise the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit. Learn to have special devotion to the Holy Trinity: I believe in God the Father, I believe in God the Son, I believe in God the Holy Spirit; I hope in God the Father, I hope in God the Son, I hope in God the Holy Spirit; I love God the Father, I love God the Son, I love God the Holy Spirit. I believe, I hope, and I love the Most Holy Trinity. We need this devotion as a supernatural exercise which is translated into acts of the heart, if not always into words. The praise and adoration will become eternal in heaven: To him who sits upon the throne and to the Lamb be blessing and honour and glory and might for ever and ever! And the four living creatures said, “Amen!’’ and the elders fell down and adored him Who lives forever and ever.[^c01-87]

## KNOWLEDGE OF GOD {#cuaderno.1.2}

The Catholic Faith teaches us that the one divine nature subsists in three distinct Persons: the Father, the Son and the Holy Spirit. Following the teachings of the magisterium of the Church, the only authorised interpreter of Revelation, we have considered, in so far as human reason can with the light of Faith, the intimacy of this mystery hidden for ages and generations but made manifest to his saints.[^c02-1] It is a sublime mystery which, after it has illuminated our life on earth with its splendour, we will be able to see clearly in heaven. They shall see his face, the Book of Revelation says of the blessed, and his name shall be on their foreheads. And night shall be no more; they need no light of lamp or sun, for the Lord God will be their light, and they shall reign for ever and ever.[^c02-2] In the beatific vision we shall contemplate in a perfect way the One and Triune God, known here in a veiled and imperfect way, as St Paul told the Corinthians: Now we are looking at confused reflection in a mirror, but then we shall see face to face. Now I know in part; then I shall understand fully, even as I have been fully understood.[^c02-3]

The beatific vision

It is a dogma of Faith, repeatedly taught by the Church, that the blessed see the divine essence with an intuitive vision, face-to-face, without the interposition of any creature which becomes what is actually seen. They see the divine essence in a way which is immediate, plain, clear and open; and seeing it in this way, they rejoice in the divine essence itself, in such a way that by this vision, this gratification, the souls of those who leave this world are truly blessed and partake of eternal life and eternal rest.[^c02-4] The Church teaches that this blessedness does not consist of a substantial transformation into God,[^c02-5] but of the supernatural elevation of the soul and its powers by means of a special light, the lumen gloriae,[^c02-6] which God infuses into the blessed making them capable of contemplating Him face-to-face.[^c02-7] In lumine tuo videbimus lumen – by your light we see the light,[^c02-8] we read in one of the psalms. And the prophet Isaiah adds, No more will the sun give you daylight, nor moonlight shine on you, but Yahweh will be your everlasting light. Your God will be your splendour. Your sun will set no more nor your moon wane, because Yahweh will be your eternal light.[^c02-9]

It is also a truth of Faith that the beatific vision begins as soon as the soul is free from all stain of sin and all punishment, without waiting for the universal resurrection: those souls of those who have not committed any sin at all after they received holy Baptism, and the souls of those who have committed sin, but have been cleansed, either while living or separated from their bodies, are promptly taken up into heaven.[^c02-10] Once it has begun, the beatific vision itself and its enjoyment endures without interruption until the final judgement and from then unto all eternity.[^c02-11]

In the glory of heaven there is no faith or hope, since these are virtues of the wayfarer on earth. The souls that contemplate God face to face already enjoy full possession of Him. The beatific vision is the fullness of charity,[^c02-12] even though this fullness allows of different degrees. The Church teaches that all the blessed see clearly the One and Triune God himself, just as he is, some more perfectly than others according to their respective merits.[^c02-13] This is why St Paul loved suffering and gloried in his tribulations,[^c02-14] which have the power of producing the eternal weight of a sublime and incomparable glory.[^c02-15] Hence, our Father gives us this advice: Let us drink to the last drop the chalice of pain in this poor present life. What does it matter to suffer for ten years, twenty, fifty... if afterwards there is heaven for ever, for ever... for ever?[^c02-16] And he adds: Let us think what heaven will be like: No eye has seen nor ear heard, nor has the mind of man conceived, all those things God has prepared for those who love Him (1 Cor 2:9). Can you imagine what it will be like to arrive there, and find ourselves with God, and see that beauty, that love which overflows into our hearts, that fills us continuously yet never satiates?[^c02-17] I ask myself many times a day: what will it be like when all that beauty, all that goodness, all the infinite wonders of God overflow into this poor vessel of clay that I am, that we all are? Then I can well understand those words of the Apostle: no eye has seen nor ear heard... It is worth while, my sons, it is worth while!

Knowledge of God by means of faith: Revelation

On this journey toward eternity, which human life is, man can enjoy a foretaste of the divine vision by means of Faith, supernatural knowledge of God, which at the same time is a light that guides him along the way. God has intervened from the beginning of time, speaking to the heart of man, calling him in a thousand ways, making him feel a need for what is divine.[^c02-18] The Church has defined as a truth of Faith the existence of a supernatural and gratuitous revelation in which God completely took the initiative. It was the good pleasure of his Wisdom and Goodness, the First Vatican Council teaches, to reveal himself and the eternal decrees of his Will to the human race in another way, a supernatural way.[^c02-19] We impart a secret and hidden wisdom of God, which God decreed before the ages for our glorification. None of the rulers of this age understood this; for if they had, they would not have crucified the Lord of Glory... God has revealed to us through his Spirit. For the Spirit searches everything, even the depths of God.[^c02-20]

Revelation was brought about in a progressive way. After having made a covenant with Noah, God promised a numerous posterity to Abraham, who was already advanced in years. And as a way of showing him He could fulfil his promise, He revealed to him his name: I am El Shaddai,[^c02-21] the Omnipotent God. Later He renewed this covenant with Isaac and Jacob; and when He chose Moses as a guide for Israel, He revealed to him his eternal subsistence, the fullness of his Being: I  AM WHO I AM.[^c02-22] The prophets added more and more to this revelation. God is all-merciful and kind, God is holiness itself, God is eternal and all-knowing, God the Creator of heaven and earth, the Powerful One, the Only One, the Lord of all, the Supreme Judge whose glory is praised by all creatures.

All these things, however, our Mother the Church teaches, transpired by way of preparation and as a type of that new and perfect covenant which was to be ratified in Christ, and of that fuller revelation which was to be given through the Word of God himself made flesh. “Behold the days shall come,” says the Lord, “and I will make a new covenant with the house of Israel, and with the House of Judah... I will place my law in their bowels, and I will write it in their heart, and I will be their God, and they shall be my people... For all of them shall know me, from the least of them even to the greatest,” says the Lord.[^c02-23] [^c02-24]

God’s revelation reached its fullness in Jesus Christ, as St Paul told the Hebrews: At various times in the past and in various different ways, God spoke to our ancestors through the prophets, but in our own time, the last days, He has spoken to us through his Son.[^c02-25] The Word of God, which is the power of God for the salvation of all who believe.[^c02-26] And our Lord continued to teach men: He gathered around him a small group of unlearned men, and with infinite patience he formed them. Little by little, with divine pedagogy, he revealed the most sublime mysteries to them. At the same time, with maternal tenderness and the strength of one who has authority, according to the needs of the moment, he went on polishing and purifying their rough spirits which were insensitive to heavenly realities.[^c02-27] God himself, making himself present and manifesting himself: through his words and deeds, his signs and wonders, but especially through his Death and glorious Resurrection from the dead and the sending of the Spirit of Truth, brought to fullness all revelation and confirmed with divine testimony: God is with us to free us from the darkness of sin and death, and to raise us up to eternal life.[^c02-28]

Revelation, culminating in Christ, is closed with the death of the last Apostle, St John.[^c02-29] The Christian dispensation, therefore, as the new and definitive covenant, will never pass away and we now await no further new public revelation before the glorious manifestation of Our Lord Jesus Christ.[^c02-30] Until then, the Church, columna et fundamentum veritatis (1 Tim 3:15), pillar and foundation of truth, continues among men throughout the centuries and until the end of time, that work of formation and teaching which Jesus entrusted to the first twelve.[^c02-31]

Revelation, through which we know in a supernatural way the existence of God and the mysteries of his life, has not been presented to man as a philosophical system to be perfected by human ingenuity; it was presented as a divine trust given to the Bride of Christ to be kept faithfully and interpreted infallibly.[^c02-32] It is not the work of man nor is it a gradual discovery of his. It is a divine work, a manifestation of the Creator’s benevolence toward his creatures.[^c02-33] Neither is it, as the Modernists thought, man’s awareness of his relationship to God,[^c02-34] as if Faith, which is the basis and foundation of all religion, were to consist in a certain interior sentiment springing from a need of the divine.[^c02-35] If such were the case, God would vanish and no one would take his place. Theocentric and supernatural Faith would be destroyed. In its place we would have a natural and anthropocentric faith, a substitute for Faith, which could save no one. On the contrary, Catholic doctrine teaches that Revelation is a divine teaching taught in a supernatural way and that it constitutes a body of doctrine applicable to all times and to all men.[^c02-36] It is a collection of truths, a Deposit entrusted to the Church by God himself. St Paul reminds his disciple Timothy of this: Guard the deposit that has been entrusted to you. Avoid the godless novelties of expression and contradictions of what is falsely called learning, for by professing it some have come to lose the Faith.[^c02-37]

Content of Revelation

This supernatural Revelation, according to the Faith of the universal Church declared by the holy Council of Trent[^c02-38] is contained in written books and in the unwritten traditions which, being received by the Apostles orally from Christ himself, or by the same Apostles under the inspiration of the Holy Spirit, have been handed down to us.[^c02-39] Through Revelation human reason has enriched its treasury of truths to the point of having an insight into mysteries, the existence of which it would not have been able even to suspect, had it been left to its own devices.[^c02-40] Reason has also been able to reach certainty about other natural truths which could have been known without the help of grace. It is certain, absolutely speaking, that human reason, by its own natural efforts and light, can arrive at true and certain knowledge of the one personal God, who by his providence watches over and governs the world. The same holds true for the Natural Law which the Creator has written in our hearts. Nevertheless, there are many obstacles that prevent reason from making effective and fruitful use of its natural ability.[^c02-41] In the first place: It is certain that the light of reason has been dimmed because of original sin which is communicated to all the descendants of Adam.[^c02-42]

The human mind has only managed to penetrate natural phenomena after a great deal of time and effort. Furthermore: truths which refer to God or the relationship between God and man, are truths which wholly transcend the visible order of things.  Truths of that kind, if they are to be translated into human action or to influence it, call for self-surrender and self-sacrifice.[^c02-43] Human understanding, subject in some way to sense knowledge, is hampered in the attainment of such truths, not only by the impulse of the senses and the imagination, but also by the disordered appetites resulting from original sin. As a result, man can easily persuade himself that which he would not like to be true is false, or at least doubtful.[^c02-44] For this reason, revelation of those truths which are not strictly supernatural is morally necessary, so that even in the present condition of the human race... they may readily be known by all men with firm certainty and with freedom from all error.[^c02-45]

Revelation also gives us other mysteries that are hidden in God and which, unless divinely revealed, would never have been known.[^c02-46] This is its main purpose. We are dealing here with a revelation which is absolutely necessary, because God, in his infinite goodness, has destined man to a supernatural end, that is, to a participation in the riches of God – and this altogether exceeds the power of the human intellect.[^c02-47] Truths such as the existence of three persons in the unity of the Divine Essence, the mystery of the Redemption and of sin, the doctrine of grace and the sacraments etc., are so sublime that they cannot be understood or demonstrated by the scientific method. The Church has asserted this many times, condemning Rationalism and Semi-rationalism.[^c02-48] The Church also had to determine with precision the true Catholic doctrine on Revelation when faced with the errors of Fideism and Traditionalism, which denied that reason was capable of going deeper into faith.[^c02-49] The First Vatican Council taught that it is nevertheless true that, if human reason, with Faith as its guiding light, inquires earnestly, devoutly, and circumspectly, it does reach, by God’s generosity, some understanding of the mysteries, and a most profitable one. It achieves this by th similarity with truths it knows naturally, such as the relationship of mysteries with one another and with the final end of man. Reason, however, never becomes capable of understanding them in the way it does with truths which are its own proper object. For divine mysteries of their very nature so excel the created intellect that even when they have been given in Revelation and accepted by Faith, that very Faith still keeps them veiled in a sort of obscurity, as long as we are exiled from the Lord in this mortal life, “for we walk by Faith and not by sight”.[^c02-50] [^c02-51]

Immutability of dogmas

In the religious field, my sons and daughters, there is no progress; there is no possibility of advancing. We have already reached the high point of progress. It is Christ, alpha and omega, the beginning and the end (cf Rev 21:6).[^c02-52] Revelation is not perfectible: the works of God are without blemish.[^c02-53] For this reason the Church has emphatically rejected the opinions of those who hold that the origin of all religion is found in a religious sense, which through the agency of vital immanence emerges from the lurking-places of the subconscious.[^c02-54] Such people go so far as to say that the Catholic religion was engendered, by the process of vital immanence, and by no other way, in the consciousness of Christ, who was a man of the choicest nature, whose like has never been, nor will be.[^c02-55] These formulas of faith – dogmas – are not the product of an awareness that has come from a rational development of an obscure religious feeling. They are objective pronouncements of absolute truths which God has wished to reveal to us. For this reason, once our Holy Mother the Church has declared the meaning of sacred dogmas, they must be adhered to forever, in that same sense; and there may never be any deviation from that meaning on the specious grounds of a more profound understanding.[^c02-56]

The content of Faith is unchangeable because it comes from God himself, with whom there is no change, nor shadow of alteration.[^c02-57] Truth is perennial; the word of Christ does not change. The Christian ought to be faithful to this truth even though it might prove uncomfortable, even though at times it may be the cause of friction and lack of understanding.[^c02-58]

From the first centuries onwards, the Church has affirmed that everything which has been declared through apostolic hands, with the consent of the universal Church...; that which sincerely and clearly comes forth from the pure fount of the Scriptures cannot be tarnished by false and deceptive arguments.[^c02-59] Thus – and the Church recognises this – what theologians call homogeneous evolution of dogma, reason illuminated by faith, can obtain a deeper understanding of the mysteries but only within proper limits, that is, with the same doctrine, the same meaning, and the same purport.[^c02-60] In this way the Church defends the integrity of the deposit of faith, which is something divine, immutable. She also defends the need for progress in our knowledge of it.[^c02-61]

We frequently hear the word aggiornamento these days, and of the need to be up-to-date in the field of theology. But at times people forget that revealed doctrine is perennial, that it is not subject to the changing circumstances of time and culture. Pope Paul VI referred to this danger: It is said that the Council has initiated and authorised such a handling of traditional teaching. Nothing could be further from the truth, if we refer to the teaching word of Our venerable Predecessor, Pope John, the discoverer, if We may express it so, of that aggiornamento in whose name some dare to harm Catholic dogma with dangerous and even unfounded interpretations and deformations. In his famous opening address of the Second Vatican Council, Pope John XXIII proclaimed that the Council itself was to confirm the whole Catholic doctrine “nulla parte inde detracta”, without detracting from it in any why, even though it call for a search for a better way in accord with the wisdom of modern scholarship, to give that doctrine a new, more adequate and deeper expression.[^c02-62] With this fidelity to the Council he exhorts us, on the one hand, to a new and deep study of the truths of faith, and on the other hand, he guides us to that univocal, perennial and consoling testimony of Peter, whose infallible voice Jesus wanted in the bosom of the Church, as a guarantee of the firmness of the Faith and as a challenge to the arbitrary and volatile weakness of time.[^c02-63]

Therefore there is no contradiction between what we can come to know by reason and what we know with certainty through Revelation. Nevertheless, although Faith is above reason, there can never be any real disagreement between Faith and reason because it is the same God who reveals mysteries and infuses Faith and who has put the light of reason into the human soul. Now God can no more contradict himself than truth can ever contradict truth... not only can Faith and reason never disagree; but rather, they are mutually supportive. For right reason demonstrates the foundations of Faith and, enlightened by the light of Faith, it pursues the science of divine things. Faith, on the other hand, sets reason free, guards it from error and furnishes it with extensive knowledge.[^c02-64]

Natural knowledge of God

God’s plan for salvation has no limits. He wishes all men to be saved and to come to the knowledge of the truth.[^c02-65] But for this, it is indispensable for man to know him. To believe in the existence of God and in the fact that he rewards good and punishes evil is indispensable to be able to attain salvation. And without faith it is impossible to please God. For he who comes to God must believe that God exists and that he rewards those who seek him.[^c02-66]

Our Lord wanted all his creatures to bear his mark imprinted on them and to give testimony to his existence. For since the creation of this world his invisible nature, namely his eternal power and deity, has been clearly perceived in the  things that have been made.[^c02-67] And thus, our Holy Mother the Church herself holds and teaches that God, the origin and end of all things, can be known with certainty by the natural light of human reason from the things that he created.[^c02-68] But despite the possibility of knowing the Creator naturally, still, many of our contemporaries feign ignorance of this intimate and vital link with God, or have explicitly rejected it. Thus atheism must be accounted among the most serious problems of this age.[^c02-69]

Look around you, our Father tells us, at those peoples, who have reached an almost incredible growth in culture and progress. In just a few years they have brought about a marvellous technical evolution which has given them a high material standard of living. Their research should have induced them to draw closer to God – it is wonderful how God aids the human intelligence – because in so far as these things are true and good, they come from God and lead to him.

Nonetheless, this is not the case: in spite of all their progress, they are not any more human. Nor can they be, because, if the divine dimension is lacking in the life of man – no matter how much material perfection is achieved – it is animal life. Only when he opens himself to the religious horizon does man achieve his desire to distinguish himself from beasts. Religion, from a certain point of view, is the greatest rebellion of man who does not want to be a beast.[^c02-70]

The fool says in his heart: there is no God.[^c02-71] In spite of our natural ability to arrive at the knowledge of the existence of God, there have always been atheists in the world. Today however, for a number of very different reasons, the phenomenon of atheism is being felt in a systematic way.[^c02-72] For all men were by nature foolish who were in ignorance of God and who from the good things seen did not succeed in knowing him who is... Or if they were impressed by their own might and energy, let them from these things realise how much more powerful is he who made them... For if they so far succeeded in knowledge that they could speculate about the world, how did they not more quickly find its Lord.[^c02-73] St Paul, in his epistle to the Romans, uses stronger words and arrives at a point where he says that the pagans are without excuse, seeing that, although they knew God, they did not glorify him as God or give thanks, but became vain in their reasoning and their senseless minds have been darkened. For while professing to be wise, they have become fools... Therefore, he concludes, God has given them up to the lustful desires of their hearts and to uncleanliness, so that they dishonour their own bodies; they who exchanged the truth of God for a lie and worshipped and served the creature rather than the Creator.[^c02-74]

The most important thing on earth, above everything else, is to know and love God, for whom we have been created. The principal object of faith is the first Truth, God himself. This is the ultimate end of man, that which gives him his real dimension and meaning. The highest reason for human life, the Second Vatican Council states, lies in man’s call to union with God. From the very circumstances of his origin man is already invited to converse with God. For man would not exist were he not created by God’s love and constantly preserved by God’s love. And he cannot live fully in accord with truth unless he freely acknowledges that love and devotes himself completely to his Creator.[^c02-75] Man can only find the truth he is looking for and the good his heart longs for in God, in knowing him and loving Him. Truth, in a certain sense, is always something sacred. It is a gift from God, a divine light which leads us to the One who is light itself. This is the case especially with truth in the supernatural order. We must treat it then with respect and love.[^c02-76] Love is the key which opens all knowledge. It is the light which dispels all darkness. The natural knowledge of God will not be reached very well without a right conscience, humility to be able to find traces of the divine in creatures and goodness to accept the consequences which follow from the existence of God. In the same way, love – supernatural charity – is indispensable to be able to abide perfectly by divine Revelation. Without piety faith is always in danger. Faith, if it is alive, works through charity.[^c02-77] Supernatural love is required to the highest degree to be able to contemplate God in heaven, face-to-face, in the fullness of the lumen gloriae, which only those who reach the perfection of charity will attain.

The natural knowledge of God, which human reason in some way reaches on its own, helps us to receive that other type of knowledge which is higher, the knowledge of faith. And faith, the whole-hearted and loving acceptance of divine Revelation, makes man taste the Joy which will fill him in heaven, when he will know the One and Triune God in the fullness of union; and it leads him to that eternal vision to which he has been destined: Lord, You have made us for yourself and our hearts are restless until they rest in You.[^c02-78]
