## CREATION {#cuaderno.1.3}

The history of man\'s life on earth constantly testifies to the truth of the words of St Paul: *God desires all men to be saved and to come to the knowledge of the truth*.[^c03-1] At the dawn of history, after having created all things from nothing, God formed man, and by a free decree of his Will, raised his nature to the supernatural order. Man corresponded with original sin. To this generous and paternal love of the Creator, Man answered with original sin, a defiant rejection of the divine demands. But *the fatherly and holy conversation between God and man, interrupted by original sin, has been marvellously resumed in the course of history. The history of salvation narrates exactly this long and changing dialogue which begins with God and weaves with man an admirable and multi-faceted conversation*.[^c03-2] When the moment determined by God from all eternity arrived, *the Word was made Flesh and dwelt among us*.[^c03-3] Through the life and death of his Son, God established us in the original dignity once again. *Thus,* St Paul says, *you are no longer strangers and sojourners, but you are fellow citizens with the saints and members of the household of God*.[^c03-4]

Not content with this, and knowing that men would rebel repeatedly against his love, our Lord in his mercy left us with the Church on earth so that through the sacraments, the grace of salvation earned by his death on the Cross might come to heal, as often as necessary, the wounds in the soul caused by sin.

*God in human history*

To each of man\'s infidelities, God has responded with a fresh outpouring of mercy and forgiveness. Human history is the history of the salvation of man wrought by God, whom --- according to the teaching of the Church --- we know through supernatural Revelation. Certainly it is true that *the same holy Mother Church holds and teaches that God, the origin and end of all things, can be known with certainty by the natural light of human reason from the things that he created: "for since the creation of the world, his invisible perfections are clearly seen, through the things that he has given to us his creatures"*.[^c03-5] *Nevertheless, it pleased his wisdom and goodness to reveal to mankind in another way, a supernatural way, himself and the eternal decrees of his will*.[^c03-6] According to the faith of the universal Church, solemnly pronounced in the Council of Trent and repeated by the two Vatican Councils, this supernatural revelation, made freely by God,[^c03-7] *is contained in written books and in the unwritten traditions that have been handed on, as it were, from hand to hand and have come down to us from the Apostles, who received them either under the inspiration of the Holy Spirit or directly from the lips of Christ*.[^c03-8] *Sacred Tradition and Sacred Scripture form one sacred Deposit of the Word of God committed to the Church, to which the entire holy people united with their shepherds adhere steadfastly\... But the task of interpreting the Word of God authentically, whether written or handed down, has been entrusted exclusively to the living teaching office of the Church, whose authority is exercised in the name of Jesus Christ*.[^c03-9]

All the faithful ought to give both internal and external assent to the decisions of the Magisterium of the Church, for it is the sole guarantee of possessing the truth. This adherence gives the Christian a *comforting assurance. The Catholic faithful know that our Lord gave to the Apostles a command and an authority to teach what he himself had taught. He entrusted to them the task of being the transmitters of his Word. They know that this Word is an integral part of the plan of salvation: that is to say that accepting that Word, Faith, is a fundamental condition for being admitted to the happiness of the kingdom of God. They know further that this transmission comes about through a mysterious and effective assistance of the Holy Spirit\..., and they know that this transmission is accompanied with that same rigorous fidelity which guarantees the univocal and stable meaning of the divine Message which we call tradition.*

*That is, the faithful know they stand before a mysterious and marvellous institution of the divine Goodness which has willed that Revelation be accepted, guarded and spread to mankind by means of this human and hierarchical structure. The general idea of God\'s design is always before us, it is that the gratuitous and supernatural communication of God with men should include these same men as collaborators, as instruments and signs of his charity. To those who have undergone spiritual trials of every kind in reaching the objective certainty of Faith, the encounter with the ecclesiastical Magisterium truly gives a sense of gratitude to God, to Christ, for having entrusted his saving message to an unequivocal and living organ, to a competent utility, to an authorised voice.*[^c03-10]

In 1967, Paul VI declared *the year commemorating the centenary of Saints Peter and Paul will be "the year of faith." He also expressed the desire that there might be special commemorative celebrations, all directed toward an interior perfecting of our holy Faith, a more profound study and religious profession of our faith, and an active witnessing to that faith without which "it is impossible to please God"*[^c03-11] *and by means of which we hope to arrive at the salvation promised to us*.[^c03-12] [^c03-13] Following the Pope\'s wishes, we contemplate the principal events of the history of salvation, which have really occurred at specific moments of man\'s history. In this doctrine we can once again savour that *joy in the faith*,[^c03-14] that genuine meaning of human life: at every step of the way we will discover the magnificent imprint of Divine Love. *Bless the Lord, O my soul, and forget not all his benefits! He forgives all your iniquity, he heals all your diseases. He redeems your life from the pit. He lavishes you with steadfast love and mercy\...*[^c03-15]

*Creation of the world and of man*

*The Holy Catholic Church, Apostolic and Roman, believes and professes that there is one true and living God, the creator and lord of heaven and earth, all powerful, eternal, unmeasurable, incomprehensible, and infinite in intellect and will and in every perfection. Since he is but one spiritual substance, entirely simple and unchangeable, he must be understood as really and essentially distinct from the world, perfectly happy in himself and by his very nature, and inexpressibly exalted above all things that exist or can be conceived other than himself*.[^c03-16] From all eternity God contains in himself the fullness of every perfection. He needs nothing outside himself, and there is nothing that can add to his Glory. Nevertheless, out of sheer goodness, he wanted to share his infinite happiness with other beings outside himself, and he decided to create the world out of nothing.

Revelation opens precisely with this stark affirmation: *In the beginning God created heaven and earth*.[^c03-17] Man could have known this through his own reason.[^c03-18] But God wanted to reveal it so as to help the weakness of the human mind which can rise to a knowledge of these exalted truths only with great effort. Throughout the ages the Church has articulated the characteristics of the creative act and has taught them as a doctrine of Faith: it was a completely free act; time began with it; nothing existed before it; it was done to show the infinite goodness of God. The First Vatican Council taught this basic truth of the Catholic Faith as follows: *In order to manifest his perfection through the benefits which he bestows on creatures --- not to intensify his happiness nor to acquire any perfection --- this one true God, by his goodness and almighty power and by a completely free decision, from the very beginning of time has created both orders of creatures in the same way, out of nothing, the spiritual or angelic world and the corporeal or visible universe. And afterwards he formed the creature man, who in a way belongs to both orders, as he is composed of spirit and body*.[^c03-19] *Furthermore, by his providence God watches over and governs all the things that he has made*,[^c03-20] making it possible for creatures to fulfil their ultimate purpose of proclaiming, by their existence, the mercy and grandeur of God: *the heavens proclaim the Glory of God, and the firmament proclaims his handiwork*.[^c03-21]

*After telling of the universality of Creation, Scripture goes on to teach the origin of the different creatures that populate the earth. When everything was done, and God saw that* no plant was yet in the earth and no herb of the field had yet sprung up --- for the Lord God had not caused it to rain upon the earth, and there was no man to till the ground,[^c03-22] *he said to himself,* Let us make man in our image, after our likeness*.*[^c03-23] **Then the Lord God formed man of dust from the ground and breathed into his nostrils the breath of life; and man became a living being*.*[^c03-24] *The biblical narrative teaches clearly and the* Catholic Faith obliges us to hold that souls are immediately created by God*.*[^c03-25] *Nevertheless, The teaching of the Church leaves the doctrine of Evolution an open question, as long as it confines its speculations to the development , from other living matter already in existence, of the human body....In the present state of scientific and theological opinion, this question may be legitimately canvassed by research, and by discussion between those who are expert in both subjects\... And there must be a readiness on both sides to accept the arbitrament of the Church, as being entrusted by Christ with the task of interpreting the Scriptures aright, and the duty of safeguarding the faith.* [^c03-26]

*Elevation to the Supernatural Order*

In Adam\'s person, God made man the crown of creation so that he would have dominion *over the fish of the sea, and over the birds of the air, and over the cattle, and over all the earth, and over every creeping thing that creeps upon the earth*.^\ ^[^c03-27] For this he received intelligence and will so that he might freely give his Creator a much more excellent glory than that which irrational creatures could offer. But God\'s goodness went even further. Led by his Love, he wanted rational creatures to participate in his own nature, to know in some way the intimate mysteries of his life which completely surpass the requirements of nature. To achieve this, God gratuitously adorned Adam[^c03-28]^\ ^ with sanctifying grace and with supernatural virtues and gifts establishing him in holiness and justice,[^c03-29]^\ ^ enabling him to act supernaturally. Besides, he perfected Adam\'s nature with those gifts of freedom from death, concupiscence and ignorance, also given gratuitously. Scripture clearly reveals how familiar man was with God in Paradise.[^c03-30] It reveals the knowledge Adam had, just after he was created, when it says that *man gave names to all cattle, and to the birds of the air, and to every beast of the field*.^\ ^[^c03-31] It speaks of his freedom from concupiscence[^c03-32]^\ ^ and the gift of immortality which God offered if he kept his commands.[^c03-33]^\ ^ The Doctors of the Church commonly teach that the integrity of human nature in the state of original justice was a consequence of the perfect and free submission of man\'s will to the Creator. This submission to God was the cause of the harmony between his soul and body, between his appetites and his reason. Strengthened with these gifts, man could not be mistaken; thus he was completely free from error. His body was endowed with immortality *not because of any intrinsic vigour, but by reason of a supernatural force given by God to the soul, whereby it was enabled to preserve the body from all corruption so long as it remained itself subject to God*.[^c03-34]^\ ^

*Original sin*

In Adam God saw the entire human race. The gift of original holiness and justice *had been given to the first man, not as an individual, but as a general principle of human nature as a whole, so that after him it would be propagated, through generation, to all successive men*.[^c03-35]^\ ^ We would have all been born in friendship with God, with soul and body adorned with the perfections bestowed by God. And when he had wanted to he would have confirmed each one in grace, taking him up from earth without suffering and without passing through the peril of death, to enjoy the beatific vision in heaven.

This was the divine plan, unbounded in its generosity. For its accomplishment God wanted man to cooperate freely with his grace. ***God is so good that he left man free. He left him with a capacity for all virtues, with his grace; and with the sad capacity for all defects, for all aberrations***. He gave Adam but one condition: *You may freely eat of every tree of the garden except that you shall not eat of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, for in the day that you eat of it surely you shall die*.[^c03-36]^\ ^ From Revelation we know the sad consequences: the devil himself, in the form of a serpent, tempted the first woman to break the divine commandment: *She took of its fruit and ate, and she also gave some to her husband, and he ate*.[^c03-37]^\ ^ Thus in Adam\'s person, *mankind sinned at the suggestion of the devil*,[^c03-38] rejecting the divine plan of salvation. Immediately his submission to the Creator was broken, the harmony that existed between the human faculties was disintegrated. Using a graphic way to relate these historic events,^\ ^[^c03-39] Scripture says that as soon as the sin of disobedience was committed, *the eyes of both were opened, and they knew that they were naked; and they sewed fig leaves together and made themselves some aprons*.^\ ^[^c03-40]^\ ^ Then, aware of their offence against God, *they heard the sound of God walking in the garden in the cool of the day, and the man and his wife hid themselves from the presence of the Lord, among the trees of the garden*.[^c03-41]^\ ^

Thus, in simple words, easily understood by all , God revealed the dogma of original sin. Throughout the course of history, the Church has maintained it firmly: in the Council of Carthage in 418; in the Second Council of Orange in 529; in numerous documents of the popes. And the Council of Trent reaffirmed it in a solemn manner: *The first man, Adam, in disobeying the command of God in the Garden of Paradise, immediately lost the justice and holiness in which he was established ; and through the offence of this sin, he incurred the wrath and the indignation of God; consequently he incurred the death with which God had previously threatened him and, together with death, the bondage in the power of him who has "dominion over death,"*[^c03-42]^\ ^ *that is, the devil. And it was the whole Adam, both body and soul, who was changed for the worse through the offence of sin*.[^c03-43]^\ ^ He was expelled from Paradise, and although human nature preserved its integrity as regards its own being, henceforth it would find serious difficulties in doing good: *In the sweat of your brow you shall eat bread till you return to the earth; for out of it you were taken; you are dust and unto dust you shall return*.[^c03-44] ***And, contrary to God\'s marvellous plan, man is*** **pronus ad peccatum** ***after the first fall - inclined towards evil***.

Some questions related to the doctrine on original sin have been left by the Magisterium to the free discussion of theologians. Among these is the theory --- as yet not shown to be certain --- of evolutionism, in the sense described in the Encyclical, Humani Generis. Paul VI spoke to a group of theologians in detail on this subject: The theory of evolutionism will not seem acceptable to you as long as it is not in clear accord with the immediate creation of each and every human soul by God, and as long as it does not maintain that the disobedience of Adam, the first parent of all men, has been of decisive importance to the fate of all mankind.[^c03-45] [^c03-46]^\ ^ However, when it comes to another hypothesis, called "polygenesis", continues the Magisterium, the Church\'s children do not enjoy the same freedom. Because the faithful cannot embrace that opinion which maintains either that after Adam there existed on this earth true men who did not take their origin through natural generation from him as from the first parent of all, or that Adam represents a certain number of first parents. It is in no way apparent how such an opinion can be reconciled with that which the sources of revealed truth and the documents of the Church\'s Magisterium propose regarding original sin, which proceeds from the sin actually committed by one individual, Adam, and which, passed on to all through generation, is in everyone as his own.[^c03-47]^\ ^[^c03-48]^\ ^

*Transmission of original sin*

In fact it is a dogma of Faith that original sin, really committed by our first parents at the beginning of history, is propagated through generation to each person who enters the world. First, the Sixteenth Council of Carthage, and later, the Second Council of Orange, the Councils of Florence and Trent, as well as numerous documents of the Roman Pontiffs, testify to the unanimous faith of the Church: *If anyone asserts that Adam\'s sin was injurious only to Adam and not to his descendants, or if he declares that it was only death --- which certainly is the penalty for sin --- which, through the death of one man, passed on to all the human race and not also the sin, which is the death of the soul, he attributes injustice to God and contradicts the words of the Apostle who says*:[^c03-49]^\ ^ *"Through one man sin entered into the world and through sin death, and thus death has passed into all men, because all have sinned."*[^c03-50]

There is a mysterious solidarity between all men and Adam, so that *all men born of Adam may be considered as one man, inasmuch as they have one common nature, which they receive from their first parents. The same thing happens precisely even in civil affairs where all who are members of the one community are regarded as one body and the whole community as one man*.[^c03-51]^\ ^[^c03-52] The solidarity of Grace, which united all men to Adam before his original disobedience, was transformed into solidarity in sin. In explaining this dogma, theologians commonly teach that *original justice, conferred by God on the first man, was a gift of grace for all human nature. But the first man lost it through the first sin. Thus, as that original justice was to have been transmitted to his posterity, so, instead, was the disorder*.[^c03-53]^\ ^[^c03-54]

The Second Vatican Council has also reaffirmed this belief of the Church and, with a pastoral purpose, refers a number of times to original sin --- in the Dogmatic Constitution on the Church, *Lumen gentium*,^\ ^[^c03-55]^\ ^ and above all, in the Pastoral Constitution (on the Church in the modern world), Gaudium et Spes, in which the Church confronted and amply developed the theme that was so long awaited and of such importance. *It is no wonder then*, Pope Paul VI told a special audience for a group of theologians, *that the introductory part of this document, when speaking of the conditions of man in the contemporary world, takes up the subject of the sad consequences of original sin. It does so in the vivid and effective terms already used by St Paul in the Epistle to the Romans. Nevertheless, the Council, following the example of St Paul himself, does not present original sin as the only source of evil in mankind. In fact, in the Constitution, we read: The truth is that the imbalances under which the modern world labours, are linked with that more basic imbalance which is rooted in the heart of man, for in the heart of man many elements wrestle with one another. Thus, on the one hand, as a creature he experiences his limitations in a multitude of ways; on the other hand, he feels himself to be boundless in his desires and summoned to a higher life. Pulled by manifold attractions he is constantly forced to choose among them and renounce some. Indeed, as a weak and sinful being, he often does what he does not want to do, and fails to do what he would. Hence he suffers from internal divisions, and from these flow so many and such great discords in society*.[^c03-56]

The Holy Father continued: *In explicit terms, the first chapter of the same Constitution, --- "De humanae Personae Dignitate", the dignity of the human person --- referring tacitly to Genesis (Chapter 3) and to the doctrine of the Council of Trent, points to the sin of the first man as the principal source of the moral disorder existing in mankind. It declares*: *"Although Created by God in a state of justice, man, at the urging of the devil, abused his freedom from the very dawn of history. Man set himself against God and sought to find fulfilment apart from God."* [^c03-57][^c03-58] Thus the unanimous teaching of Sacred Scripture, Tradition, and the Magisterium of the Church is that *the sin of the first man is transmitted to all his descendants, not by way of imitation but by propagation*. *"Inest uniquique proprium" --- it is present in each as his own. It is "mors animae" (the death of the soul), that is, the privation --- not merely the simple lack --- of sanctity and justice, even in new-born children.*[^c03-59]^\ ^[^c03-60] It is also a dogma of Faith that only one creature, *the Blessed Virgin Mary, in the first instant of her conception, was preserved free from all stain of original sin, through a singular grace and privilege of Almighty God and in consideration of the merits of Christ Jesus, the Saviour of the human race*.[^c03-61]

*The promise of Redemption*

*Comfort, comfort my people, says your God. Speak tenderly to Jerusalem, and cry to her that her warfare is ended, that her iniquity is pardoned.*^\ ^[^c03-62]^\ ^ God could have left man, fallen in his sin, eternally separated from his love and subject to the devil\'s power. But out of his infinite goodness and freedom, He wanted to restore the broken order and to draw out of the evil of Adam even greater benefits. Therefore, when He imposed on our first parents the penalty for their rebellion, He also foretold the promised Redemption, directing his words to the devil: *I will put enmity between your seed and her seed. This shall bruise your head, and you shall snap at its heel.*[^c03-63]^\ ^ Man\'s infidelity does not make God withdraw his kindness but rather accentuates even more the gratuity of his gifts. Expulsion from the earthly paradise, although signifying the end of an habitual familiarity between God and the first parents, did not mean the end of God\'s solicitous interventions, for God always seeks man\'s salvation. Human history continues being a history of divine love and mercy.

*God however does not make men holy and save them individually, separating them from one another. Rather, it pleased him to bring men together as one people, a people which was to acknowledge him in truth and to serve him in holiness. He therefore chose the race of Israel as a people unto himself. With it he set up a covenant. Step by step he taught and prepared this people, making known in its history both himself and the decree of his will and making it holy unto himself. All these things, however, were done by way of preparation and as a figure of the new and perfect covenant which was to be ratified in Christ, and of the fuller Revelation which was to be given through the Word of God himself made flesh*.[^c03-64] In fulfilment of his promise, *in the fullness of time, God sent forth his Son, born of woman, born under the Law to redeem those who were under the Law, so that we might receive adoption as sons*.^\ ^[^c03-65]^\ ^ *And he was hungry and suffered thirst, felt pain, wept and suffered all other maladies of the body. Finally he was crucified died and was buried, and he arose on the third day. Then after having conversed with his disciples, on the fortieth day he ascended into heaven*.[^c03-66]

St Paul frequently testifies that the Incarnation, death, and resurrection of Jesus Christ has reconciled us with God, freeing us from the power of the devil, from sin and death. Thanks to a mysterious solidarity between Christ and humanity burdened with sin.[^c03-67]Our Lord\'s infinite merits have satisfied divine justice for us forever. Conscious of this joyful fact, St Paul preaches it to all men: *You who in other time were far from God, have been brought near by the Blood of Christ*.^\ ^[^c03-68] *You have been purchased at a great price*.^\ ^[^c03-69] *Thus, as sin came into the world through one man and sin brought death\..., so one man\'s act of righteousness leads to acquittal and life for all men. For as by one man\'s disobedience many were made sinners, so by one man\'s obedience many will be made righteous*.^\ ^[^c03-70]

*Baptism*

According to the Church\'s teaching, righteousness or justification *is a passing from the state in which man is born as a son of the first Adam, to the state of grace and "adoption as sons of God"*^\ ^[^c03-71] *through the second Adam, Jesus Christ our Saviour. After the promulgation of the Gospel this passing cannot take place without the washing of regeneration or the desire for it, as it is written*: *"Unless a man be born again of water and the Holy Spirit, he cannot enter into the kingdom of God."* [^c03-72] [^c03-73] St Paul had already spoken of the need for baptism for sharing in the death and resurrection of Jesus Christ, through which we are reconciled with God. He writes to the Romans: *In baptism we were buried with him dying to sin, so that as Christ was raised from death to life by the glory of the Father, so too we might walk in newness of life\... our old self was crucified with him so that the sinful body might be destroyed, and we might no longer be enslaved to sin. For he who has died is freed from sin. But if we have died with Christ, we believe that we shall also live with him. For we know that Christ, being raised from the dead, will never die again; death no longer has dominion over Him\... So you also must consider yourselves dead to sin and alive to God in Christ Jesus.*^\ ^[^c03-74]

Through baptism the infinite merits of Christ are applied to each one of us. But the justification that we acquire *is not only the remission of sins, but also sanctification and the renewal of the interior man through the voluntary reception of grace and the gifts, whereby a man is changed from unjust to just and from enemy to friend.*^\ ^[^c03-75] Once more he becomes a son of God, called to contemplate him face-to-face.

Baptism totally forgives the guilt and punishment of original sin and of personal sins that might have been committed before its reception, so that *the souls of those who have not committed any sin at all after they have received holy Baptism are promptly taken up into heaven*.^\ ^[^c03-76] But it does not free from the penal effects of sin: man remains subject to error, concupiscence[^c03-77] and death; and although fallen human nature can perform some naturally good works without grace,^\ ^[^c03-78]^\ ^ it cannot avoid all venial sins without a special privilege.[^c03-79] With our souls redeemed through baptism we *groan inwardly as we wait for adoption as sons, the redemption of our bodies*.^\ ^[^c03-80] The Catholic faith teaches us that this will happen at the end of time, *when the Lord shall come in his majesty, and all the angels with Him*,^\ ^[^c03-81] *when, death being destroyed, all things are subject to Him.* [^c03-82]^-^[^c03-83] Until then, the Church, *like a stranger in a foreign land, presses forward amid the persecutions of the world and the consolations of God*,^\ ^[^c03-84]^\ ^ *announcing the Cross and Death of the Lord until he comes*.[^c03-85] *By the power of the risen Lord it is given strength so that, in patience and in love, it might overcome its sorrows and its challenges, both within itself, and from without, and that it might reveal to the world, faithfully though somewhat hidden, the mystery of Christ until, at the end of time, it will be manifested in all its splendour*. ^8^[^c03-86]^3^

## THE WORK OF OUR REDEMPTION {#cuaderno.1.4}

In the beginning of time, when God created the world from nothing and fashioned the first man in his own image and likeness[^c04-1], He placed Adam *in such an exalted condition that he was to have communicated to his descendants, together with earthly life, the supernatural life of grace*.[^c04-2] Everything was in harmony and was good in those moments. ***The world, my children, all God**'**s creatures are good. Sacred Scripture teaches us that, after finishing the wonderful work of Creation, heaven and earth with all its splendid array of creatures (cf. Gen 2:1) having been completed,*** **God looked at all that he had done and he saw that it was very good *(Gen 1:31).***

***It was Adam**'**s sin that broke this divine harmony of creation.***[^c04-3] Man rose in opposition to God's saving intention at the very threshold of history; grievously offending his Creator, he merited a just punishment for himself and his descendants. *All men had lost innocence in the sin of Adam*,[^c04-4] *"they became unclean"*[^c04-5] *and --- as the Apostle said --- "children of wrath by nature"*,[^c04-6] *so completely that they were "slaves of sin"*[^c04-7] *and under the power of the devil and of death, that neither the power of nature for the Gentiles, nor the very-,- Letter-,- of the Law of Moses for the Jews, could bring liberation from that condition*.[^c04-8] So great was the offence committed against God *that no created power was sufficient to expiate the crimes of mankind*;[^c04-9] *nothing was capable of raising us up and freeing us from such a ruinous state and eternal punishment*.[^c04-10]

As slaves of the law, of sin, of the devil and of death,[^c04-11] all men are born estranged from God and bereft of any possibility of satisfying divine justice themselves or of meriting his forgiveness. Only God could reconcile to himself mankind that had sinned in Adam. *The father of mercies and the God of all comfort*,[^c04-12] taking pity on the human race, wished to carry out the redemption of the world.[^c04-13]

*The divine decree of redemption*

The saving plan, hidden in God from the foundation of the world,[^c04-14] *flows from the source of all love, the charity of God the Father*[^c04-15] who *when the time had finally come, sent forth his Son, born of a woman, born under the Law, to redeem those under the Law*.[^c04-16]

God could have left us in the state merited by our sins. However, *God so loved the world that he gave his only Son*.[^c04-17] *The Word of the Eternal Father with the same divine love assumed human nature from the race of Adam, but innocent and spotless, so that He, as the new Adam, might be the source whence the grace of the Holy Spirit should flow unto all the children of the first parent*.[^c04-18]

Without losing his divine nature, the Son of God, *for us men and for our Salvation, came down from heaven, and was made Flesh of the Virgin Mary by the power of the Holy Spirit*.[^c04-19] In the mystery of the Incarnation, the Church solemnly professes that *the one self-same Christ, only-begotten Son and Lord, must be acknowledged in two natures without any co-mingling or change or division or separation; that the distinction between the natures is in no way removed by their union but rather that the specific character of each nature is preserved and they are united in one person and one hypostasis*.[^c04-20]

By virtue of his Incarnation, Jesus Christ *was sent into the world as true Mediator between God and men. Being God,"the whole plenitude of the deity is embodied and dwells in him"*.[^c04-21] *According to his human nature, on the other hand, he is the new Adam, made head of redeemed humanity, and "full of grace and of truth"*.[^c04-22] [^c04-23] Taking on our human condition, he established a solidarity with us, and was ready to suffer in his own Body the punishment which mankind had merited by its sins. *By his Incarnation, the Son of God has united himself in some fashion with every man. He worked with human hands, he thought with a human mind, he acted with a human will*[^c04-24] *and he loved with a human heart. Born of the Virgin Mary, he has truly been made one of us, like us in all things except sin*.[^c04-25] [^c04-26] *How else could he be wounded for our iniquities and redeem us from the slavery of sin, if he did not possess a human nature like ours? On the other hand, how could he give total satisfaction to the justice of the heavenly Father, flaunted by the human race, if he did not possess the immense and infinite dignity of a divine Person?*[^c04-27]

Christ's whole life, from his Incarnation to his Ascension into heaven, had redemptive value because his actions, being actions of God Himself, were expiatory and meritorious to an infinite degree.[^c04-28] However, out of an excess of love, *the wonderful works of God among the people of the Old Testament were but a prelude to the work of Christ the Lord in redeeming mankind and giving perfect glory to God. He achieved his task principally by the paschal mystery of his blessed Passion, resurrection from the dead, and glorious ascension*.[^c04-29]

*Sacrifice of the Cross, true and perfect Sacrifice*

It was on the Cross that the redemptive desires of Christ reached their complete fulfilment. *I have a baptism with which to be baptised*, he cried out shortly before the Passion, *and how impatient I am for its accomplishment!*[^c04-30] On Calvary, Jesus Christ --- High Priest and Victim at the same time --- offered a true and perfect Sacrifice, since he immolated his Body and *gave himself up for us, a fragrant offering and sacrifice to God*.[^c04-31]

On countless occasions Scripture attests to the fact that our Lord's death was a true immolation, thereby revealing to us the ultimate meaning of his life: *Christ has been offered once to bear the sins of many*.[^c04-32] St Paul tells the Romans that the redemption is through Jesus Christ, *whom God put forward as an expiation by his Blood*.[^c04-33] *This immolation of the Victim was carried out by means of a bloody death, voluntarily suffered*.[^c04-34]

The offering to God of the immolated Victim is the second element characteristic of every sacrifice; and it is also fully verified in the Sacrifice of Calvary. Christ *freely accepted for the salvation of the world*[^c04-35] and offered to his heavenly Father the immolation which he carried out with complete freedom. *Consequently, when Christ came into the world, he said, "Sacrifices and offerings Thou hast not desired, but a Body hast Thou prepared for Me; in burnt offerings and sin offerings Thou hast taken no pleasure." Then I said, "Lo, I have come to do Thy Will, O God."*[^c04-36] At the very threshold of the Passion when his human nature resisted pain and suffering, he offered to God the sacrifice he was about to accomplish: *Father, if you are willing, remove this cup from me; nevertheless not my will, but thine be done*.[^c04-37]

In the light of Revelation, the sacrifice of the Cross is presented to us as the perfect fulfilment of the different sacrifices offered in the Old Testament. When the people of Israel were about to leave Egypt, God gave the command for every family to sacrifice *a lamb without blemish, a male, of one year*,[^c04-38] following a prescribed ritual.[^c04-39] *And when your children shall say to you: What is the meaning of this rite? You shall say to them: It is the victim of the passage of the Lord, when he passed over the houses of the children of Israel in Egypt*.[^c04-40]

All of this, Scripture teaches, was but *a shadow of the good things to come, instead of the true form of these realities*.[^c04-41] Therefore St Paul, addressing all Christians, joyously exclaims: *Clean out the old leaven that you may be a new dough\... for Christ, our Paschal Lamb, has been sacrificed*.[^c04-42] The crossing of the Red Sea by the Israelites and their flight from the slavery of Egypt to the promised land was the prefiguringe of the new spiritual freedom won by Christ.

God was not content with having liberated Israel from the bondage of Egypt. He also established a covenant, ratified by another sacrifice, through which he chose them to be his people on earth. *Moses took the blood and sprinkled it upon the people, and he said: This is the blood of the Covenant which the Lord has made with you*.[^c04-43] This nation was repeatedly unfaithful to its promises. Consequently God announced a new and perfect covenant to replace the old one. *The days will come, says the Lord, when I will establish a new Covenant with the house of Israel and the house of Judah; not like the covenant that I made with their fathers on the day when I took them by the hand to lead them out of the land of Egypt; for they did not continue in my covenant, and so I paid no heed to them, says the Lord*.[^c04-44] This new and definitive Covenant was instituted by Christ on the very night of the Passion, when he announced to the disciples that the Eucharist was the same Sacrifice which, a few hours later, he was to offer on the Cross. *This cup which is poured out for you is the new covenant in my blood.*[^c04-45] *He is the mediator of the new Covenant*, St Paul explains, *so those who are called may receive the promised eternal inheritance since a Death has occurred which redeems them from the transgressions under the first Covenant*.[^c04-46]

To atone for offences against God, the Lord had ordained in the Old Law that sacrifices also be carried out in satisfaction for sins.[^c04-47] These sacrifices of themselves *cannot perfect the conscience of the worshipper, but deal only with food and drink and various ablutions, outward observances\... But when Christ appeared as a high priest of the good things that have come\... He entered once for all into the holy place, taking not the blood of goats and calves but his own Blood, thus securing an eternal redemption. For if the sprinkling of defiled persons with the blood of goats and bulls and with the ashes of a heifer sanctifies for the purification of the flesh, how much more shall the Blood of Christ, who through the eternal Spirit offered himself without blemish to God, purify your conscience from dead works to serve the living God?*[^c04-48]

*Characteristics of the sacrifice of the Cross*

Christ's sacrifice on the Cross --- the Pasch of Christians, the new covenant, the propitiation for all sins --- is, first of all, a manifestation of the boundless love of God the Son for his Father, and of the entire Trinity for all mankind. *By this we have known the love of God, that he laid down his life for us*.[^c04-49] Each of us can truly say with St Paul: Christ *loved me and gave himself for me*.[^c04-50]

Moreover Catholic doctrine teaches that this sacrifice had an immense value in God's eyes for atoning for sins and meriting a reward. *It was a sacrifice of atonement*, St Paul writes, because *you who were dead in trespasses and the uncircumcision of your flesh, God made alive together with him, having forgiven us all our trespasses, having canceled the bond which stood against us with its legal demands; this he set aside, nailing it to the Cross*.[^c04-51] It is a vicarious atonement, or one of substitution, by which Christ, the head of all creation[^c04-52] and in solidarity with it, taking man's place, suffered in his flesh the punishment which, in justice, belonged to sinful mankind. St Peter reminds all Christians: *Christ died for sins once and for all, the righteous for the unrighteous, that he might bring us to God*.[^c04-53] *He himself bore our sins in his Body on the tree, that we might die to sin and live to righteousness. By his wounds you have been healed*.[^c04-54] St Paul, using a more direct figure of speech, says that God *made him to be sin who knew no sin, so that in him we might become the righteousness of God*.[^c04-55] In this way *our Lord Jesus Christ\... reconciled us to God in his Blood*,[^c04-56] *having become for us justice, and sanctification, and redemption*.[^c04-57] *He merited justification for us by his own most holy Passion on the Wood of the Cross, and made satisfaction for us to God the Father*.[^c04-58]

The second characteristic of the Sacrifice of the Cross, inseparable from the aspect of atonement, concerns the merits won by Christ in fulfilling the divine Will. *As he hung upon the Cross* --- the Magisterium of the Church teaches --- *Christ Jesus not only appeased the justice of the Eternal Father which had been violated, but he also won for us, his brethren, an ineffable flow of graces*.[^c04-59] So measureless and superabundant are these merits,[^c04-60] that the Church does not hesitate to call original sin the *felix culpa*[^c04-61] since it brought our Redeemer to us. *Where sin increased, grace abounded all the more*.[^c04-62]

Finally, the Redemption wrought by Christ on the Cross is universal, it extends to the whole human race. *Just as there is not, never was, nor ever will be any man whose nature was not taken up in him, so there is not, never was, nor ever will be any man for whom Jesus Christ, our Lord, has not suffered.*[^c04-63] ***He, by his cross and his triumph over death, cancelled the decrees of condemnation of mankind (cf Col 2:14) and he redeemed us all with the immense and infinite price of his blood.*** **Empti enim estis pretio magno *(1 Cor 6:20; cf 1 Pet 1:18), we have been purchased at a great price. To all mankind, without exception, he has opened up the possibility of a new life, of being reborn in the Spirit, of beginning a life of sure victory.***[^c04-64] *Outside of him* --- St Peter and St John explained to the Jews, --- *there is salvation in no one else, for there is no other name under heaven given among men by which we must be saved*.[^c04-65]

*Effects of the sacrifice of the Cross*

By his Passion and Death Christ merited, first of all, the glorification of his own body and the exaltation of his sacred Humanity. United to God in an ineffable way by the hypostatic union, the human nature of our Lord had a right to the glory of the divinity overflowing also into his Body. However, *he humbled himself and became obedient unto death, even death on a cross. Therefore God has highly exalted him and bestowed on him the name which is above every name, that at the name of Jesus every knee should bend, in heaven and on earth and under the earth, and every tongue confess that Jesus Christ is Lord, to the glory of God the Father*.[^c04-66] Now that his sacred Humanity has been glorified and brought to heaven, he is eternally praised by the angels and Saints. *Worthy is the Lamb who was slain, to receive power and wealth and wisdom and might and honour and glory and blessing*.[^c04-67]

Having carried out the Redemption objectively, the Sacrifice of Christ healed the wounds caused by original sin, restored the order which had been destroyed and returned men to friendship with God. *Christ redeemed us from the curse of the Law, having become a curse for us; for it is written: Cursed be every one who hangs on a tree*.[^c04-68] Since the Law was a continuous occasion of sin,[^c04-69] Christ's death also freed human nature from the slavery in which it was held. *Our old self*, writes St Paul to the Romans, *was crucified with him so that the sinful body might be destroyed*.[^c04-70] To such an extent did Christ destroy sin by his death that *John* the Baptist said to his disciples, while pointing to Jesus by the Jordan: *Behold the Lamb of God, behold him who takes away the sins of the world!*[^c04-71]

Sin makes man a slave of the devil and subject to death. Therefore the deliverance from the bondage of sin which Christ won also brings victory over the devil and over death.

*The Son of God* --- exclaims St John --- *came into the world to destroy the works of the devil*.[^c04-72] His whole Life is a manifestation of this power and, at the same time, a clear sign that the Kingdom of God has come on earth.[^c04-73] However it was chiefly on Golgotha where he *disarmed the principalities and powers and made a public example of them, triumphing over them in his own Person*.[^c04-74]

Finally, Christ's sacrifice was an effective antidote against death, the penalty of sin.[^c04-75] Jesus came *so that through death he might destroy him who has the power of death, that is, the devil, and deliver all those who through fear of death were subject to lifelong bondage*.[^c04-76] Revelation teaches us that, on account of a mysterious human solidarity, the death that Adam merited as punishment for his sin, is transmitted to all his descendants. *Therefore, as sin came into the world through one man and death through sin, even so death spread to all men because in him all had sinned*.[^c04-77] Christ's Incarnation transformed that solidarity in sin and death into a solidarity in grace and life. *For as by a man came death, by a Man has come also resurrection of the dead*.[^c04-78] In Jesus Christ, truly risen by the power of God, all men have overcome death, although the definitive triumph will only take place at the end of time. *The Christian faith teaches us that bodily death\... will be vanquished; the almighty and merciful Saviour re-establishes man in the salvation lost through sin*.[^c04-79] Only one creature --- Mary most holy --- has already overcome death in her own body. The rest of us await the glorious coming of our Lord, who clothe our mortal flesh with incorruptibility. And *when this mortal body puts on immortality, then shall come to pass the saying that is written: Death has been swallowed up in victory. O death, where is your victory? O death, where is your sting?*[^c04-80]

In addition to freeing us from this four-fold slavery, the redemptive sacrifice of Christ has reconciled us with God and with the rest of men.[^c04-81] *For in him all fullness of God was pleased to dwell, and through him to reconcile to himself all things, whether on the earth or in heaven, making peace by the Blood of his Cross. And you, who were once estranged and hostile in mind, doing evil deeds, he has now reconciled in his Body of flesh*.[^c04-82]

In Christ, all mankind has merited divine grace and the promise of glory. St Paul attests to this in the Epistle to the Romans: *Thus, as one man's offence led to condemnation for all men, so one man's act of righteousness leads to acquittal and life for all men*.[^c04-83] The Church, ever guided by the Holy Spirit, teaches us that this justification does not consist in the covering over of our sins, or simply in their not beingattributed to us,[^c04-84] or in an external favour of God, or in a merely extrinsic clothing with the merits of Christ.[^c04-85] Nor can it be said to formally consist inChrist's own justice,[^c04-86] or in obedience to the commandments,[^c04-87] or in remission of sins, even though this pardon is always linked to justification.[^c04-88] Justification is, essentially, the *sanctification and renovation of the interior man by the voluntary reception of grace and gifts.*[^c04-89] *Whence in the very act of being justified, at the same time that his sins are remitted, man receives through Jesus Christ, to whom he is joined, the infused gifts of faith, hope, and charity*.[^c04-90] For this reason, it is the state in which *man becomes just instead of unjust and a friend instead of an enemy*,[^c04-91] having been truly made adoptive children of God, sharers in the divine nature[^c04-92] and *heirs with hope of eternal life*.[^c04-93]

Catholic doctrine also teaches that the only formal cause of this justification is *the justice of God, not the justice by which he himself is Just, but the justice by which he makes us just, namely the justice which we have as a gift from him and by which we are renewed in the spirit of our mind. And not only are we considered just, but we are truly said to be just and we are just, each one of us receiving within himself his own justice, according to the measure that "the Holy Spirit imparts to each one as he wishes"*[^c04-94] and *according to the disposition and co-operation of each person*.[^c04-95]

*Personal co-operation in redemption*

The work of redemption has been fully achieved by Christ on the Cross, his sacrifice is perfect. However, God counts on the co-operation of each man in his own salvation. *Although "Christ died for all men"*,[^c04-96] *not everyone benefits from his death, but only those to whom the merits of his Passion are communicated*.[^c04-97] *It can be said* --- the Magisterium of the Church points out --- *that on Calvary, Christ has built a font of purification and of salvation which he filled with his Blood that he shed; but if men do not bathe in it and in it wash away the stains of their iniquity, they can never be purified and saved. Thus, in order that sinners may be purified in the blood of the Lamb, individual co-operation of the faithful is required*.[^c04-98]

This personal co-operation, which theologians call "subjective redemption", is carried out *especially by means of the sacraments and the eucharistic Sacrifice*.[^c04-99] Faith alone therefore is not sufficient for justification;[^c04-100] the reception of the Sacraments, or at least the effective desire to receive them, is also necessary.[^c04-101] Through the sacraments we are actually *united with him in a death like his*[^c04-102] and the work of redemption, including everything contained in the sacrifice of the Cross, is accomplished in each man. *Through this active and individual participation, in the same manner that the members of the Mystical Body become day by day more like the divine Head, so the salvation that comes from the head flows to its members*.[^c04-103]

Nevertheless, ***the Lord, who offers salvation to all men without distinction of nationality, race, language or human condition (cf. Gal 3:28; Col 3:11), does not force anyone to accept it. He leaves men free. At times men are not willing, and they oblige Jesus to accept their base and selfish excuses, their refusals* *---* **habe me excusatum *(cf. Luke 14:15-24)* *---* *to the loving invitation to take part in the great banquet.***[^c04-104]

Along with faith and the reception of the sacraments, good deeds are also necessary. *Of what use is it, my brethren, if a man says he has faith if he has no works? Can his faith save him?\... Because faith apart from works is dead*.[^c04-105]

***My children, it is our upright actions, together with faith and the grace of God, that make our human life supernatural.*** This is so because *the justified\... have everything necessary to them to be regarded as having completely satisfied the divine Law in this life by their works, those which they have performed in God. And they may be regarded as having likewise truly merited the eternal life, which they will certainly attain in due time if they but die in the state of grace.*[^c04-106] This personal merit, by virtue of which salvation becomes the inheritance of every man, does not in any way diminish the complete gratuity of justification: *Christ Jesus himself always gives strength to the justified, just as the head gives strength to the members*[^c04-107] *and the vine gives strength to the branches*.[^c04-108] *This strength always precedes, accompanies, and follows the good works of the justified and without it the good works cannot be at all pleasing to God or meritorious*.[^c04-109]

The mercy of God knows no limits. It has been unsparing in granting us pardon and making us once again his children. However, ***it is painful to see that after twenty centuries there are so few in this world who call themselves Christians, and that, among those who call themselves Christians, there are so few who hold the true doctrine of Jesus Christ. I have told you on some occasion how a man, who was not bad at heart but did not have faith, said to me while looking at a map of the world:*** **Look, from north to south, from east to west. What do you want me to look at?** ***I asked. And he replied:*** **The failure of Christ. People have been trying for many centuries to implant his doctrine in man's hearts, and look at the results: there are no Christians.**

***At first I was filled with sadness, but immediatelty with love and gratitude, because our Lord has wanted us to co-operate freely in his work of redemption. Christ has not failed. His doctrine and life are always bearing fruit in the world. His redemption is sufficient, and superabundant, but he treats us as intelligent and free beings and has so ordained that in a mysterious way we should fulfil in our flesh, in our life, that which is lacking in his Passion* pro corpore eius, quod est Ecclesia --- for the sake of his body, that is, thee church *(Col 1:24).***

***The Redemption is still being wrought, and you and I are co-redeemers. It is worth risking our whole life and being willing to suffer, for love, in order to further the things of God and help him redeem the world, that is to say, to co- redeem. With this in mind, you and I should cry out in praise of God:* Laudationem Domini loquetur os meum, et benedicat omnis caro nomini sancto eius** ***(Ps 144:21): My mouth will speak the praise the Lord, and let all flesh bless his holy name.***[^c04-110]

## THE CHURCH {#cuaderno.1.5}

The Eternal Father, by an utterly free and mysterious decree of his own wisdom and goodness, created the whole world. His plan was to dignify men with a participation in his own divine life. He did not abandon men even though they had sinned in Adam, but He always held out to them the helps needed for salvation, in anticipation of Christ the Redeemer.[^c05-1] But God wanted to save all men, not merely as individuals, without any mutual bonds, but by making them into a single people, a people which would acknowledge him in truth and serve him in holiness:[^c05-2] that is to say, a supernatural society whose head would be his only-begotten Son. In order to prepare for the Incarnation of the Word, he chose Israel as his people and made a Covenant with them by way of preparation and as a figure of that new and perfect Covenant which was to be ratified in Christ.[^c05-3] When the fullness of time arrived, Christ, to carry out the Will of the Father, inaugurated the kingdom of heaven on earth, revealed to us the mystery of the Father and, by his obedience, brought about our redemption.[^c05-4] In order to make the saving work of redemption permanent, he decreed the establishment of the Holy Church, in which, as in the house of the living God, all the faithful would be united by the bond of charity and of the one faith.[^c05-5]

The founding of the Church by Jesus Christ

Sacred Scripture eloquently manifests Christ’s intention to found a Church.[^c05-6] Our Lord began preaching the Good News, that is, the coming of God’s Kingdom, which for centuries, had been promised in the Scriptures.[^c05-7] He spoke in parables to the multitudes that followed him, explaining to them the nature of this new order. The kingdom of heaven is like a grain of mustard seed which a man took and sowed in his field.[^c05-8] At other times he spoke to them about a net, or about the yeast that ferments the dough, or of a hidden treasure or of a precious stone, of a man who goes off to a distant land... The word of the Lord is like a seed sown in a field.[^c05-9] Those who hear the Word with faith and become part of the little flock of Christ,[^c05-10] have truly received the kingdom. Then, by its own power, the seed sprouts little by little and ripens until harvest time.[^c05-11] The miracles of Jesus also confirm the fact that the kingdom has already arrived on earth: “If I cast out devils by the finger of God, then the Kingdom of God has come upon you”.[^c05-12] [^c05-13]

Our Lord did not content himself with merely announcing the coming of a kingdom of faith and charity. He also wanted to make it clear that this kingdom was hierarchically structured and for this reason after praying to the Father, calling to himself those whom he wanted, he appointed twelve men who would stay in his company and whom he would send to preach the kingdom of God.[^c05-14] Then, over a period of three years, on innumerable occasions, Jesus speaks with them, answers their questions and resolves their doubts, progressively revealing to them the divine truth... Jesus pours out his love. He forms their minds, strengthens their wills, corrects their faults and sets their intentions straight, until he makes them, with the sending of the Holy Spirit, the pillars upon which the Church is built.[^c05-15] Only the Apostles, who are the foundations of the new Israel,[^c05-16] were promised by our Lord the power to govern the Church: Truly, I say to you, whatever you bind on earth shall be bound in heaven, and whatever you loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven.[^c05-17]

Even though the whole life of Christ was directed toward the foundation of the Church, Tradition and the Magisterium have seen its birth especially in the Cross: The Word of God, in order to save everyone, not only wanted to be nailed to the Cross and to die on it, but he also allowed, after he had given up his Spirit, that his side to be opened by a lance so that — at the issue of streams of water and blood — there would form the one Holy Mother Church, immaculate and virgin, the Spouse of Christ, just as Eve was formed from the side of the first man as he slept.[^c05-18] After the Resurrection, having been made Lord and Supreme Pontiff,[^c05-19] he entrusted to the twelve Apostles the power that he had promised them: As the Father has sent me, even so I send you. And when he had said this — writes St John — He breathed on them, and said to them, “Receive the Holy Spirit: for those whose sins you forgive, they are forgiven; for those whose sins you retain, they are retained.[^c05-20] A few moments before his Ascension, he commissioned them: Go out into the whole world; proclaim the Good News to all creation.[^c05-21] Go, therefore, make disciples of all the nations; baptise them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit and teach them to observe all the commands I have give to you.[^c05-22] You will be my witnesses, not only in Jerusalem, but throughout Judea and Samaria and indeed to the ends of the earth.[^c05-23]

Our Lord began to fulfil his promise at Pentecost when the Holy Spirit was sent in order that he might forever sanctify the Church.[^c05-24] Since then, the whole Church shines forth “as a people united by virtue the unity of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit”.[^c05-25] [^c05-26]

Names of the Church

Coming forth from the eternal Fathers love, founded in time by Christ the Redeemer, and made one in the Holy Spirit,27  the Church is a complex reality which is both present in the world and transcends it. It is a mystery of the love of God which only revelation can shed light on. For this reason, Sacred Scripture, speaking in a way understandable to all, explains to us the nature of the Church through the use of different figures.

The Church is compared to a sheepfold whose door is Christ,28  and to a flock whose shepherd is God29 and Jesus Christ who gave His life for His sheep.30  It is also a field tilled by God,31  a divine edifice whose cornerstone is Christ and whose foundations are the Apostles, and in which the faithful act as living stones.32  This edifice has various names to describe it: the house of God,33  in which His family dwells; the household of God in the Spirit;34  the dwelling place of God among men;35  and especially, the holy temple. This temple, symbolised by places of worship built out of stone, is praised by the holy Fathers and, not without reason, is compared in the liturgy to the Holy City, the new Jerusalem.36

The Church is called the Mystical Body of Christ because God has put all things under his feet and has made him the head over all things for the church, which is his body, the fullness of him who fills all in all.37  As all the members of the body though they are many, form one body, so also are the faithful in Christ.38  Similarly, in the building up of Christ's body there is a flourishing variety of members and functions. But there is only one Spirit who, according to His own richness and the needs of the ministries, distributes His different gifts for the welfare of the Church.39

The Church is the Kingdom of God40  which, although not being of this world,41  is already present in a mysterious way and will be completed in heaven.

But the Church, the gathering of those who believe in Christ, is above all the people of God, which He bought with His blood,42  prefigured already in the ancient people of Israel. For this reason the Magisterium states that Israel according to the flesh, which wandered as an exile in the desert, was already called the Church of God.43  Likewise, the new Israel which, while going forward in this present world, goes in search of a future and abiding city,44  is also called the Church of Christ.45

For Christ instituted a new covenant, that is to say, a new testament, in His blood,46  by calling together a people made up of Jew and Gentile, making them one, not according to the flesh but in the Spirit. This was to be a new People of God... That messianic people has for its head Christ, “who was put to death for our trespasses and rose again for our justification”,47  and who now, having won a name which is above all names, reigns in glory in heaven. The heritage of this people is the dignity and freedom of the sons of God, in whose hearts the Holy Spirit dwells as in His temple. Its law is the new commandment to love as Christ loved us.48  Its goal is the kingdom of God, which has been begun by God Himself on earth, and which is to be further extended until it is brought to perfection by Him at the end of time. Then Christ our life49  will appear, and “creation itself also will be delivered from its slavery to corruption into the freedom of the glory of the sons of God.”50 51

Properties of the Church

It can be deduced from these figures and from the consideration of the parables that Our Lord used to show His disciples the characteristics of the kingdom of heaven that the Church is both human and divine, visible and yet invisibly endowed, eager to act and yet devoted to contemplation, present in this world and yet a pilgrim.52  Like her Founder who, while being one person, has both a divine and a human nature, the Church too is both human and divine: a society divine in its origin, supernatural in its end and in the means immediately leading to its end. However, it is a human association in that it is made up of men.53  The Church is made up of an invisible and spiritual element clothed in an eternal and visible form by which she is recognised. The Second Vatican Council affirms this: Christ, the one Mediator, established and ceaselessly sustains here on earth His holy Church, the community of faith, hope, and charity as a visible structure. Through her He communicates truth and grace to all... This Church, constituted and organised in the world as a society, subsists in the Catholic Church, which is governed by the successor of Peter and by the bishops in union with Him.54

The Church is at the same time charismatic and juridical because Christ Himself has filled it with His Spirit and provided it with those means which befit it as a visible and social Unity.55  Our Lord conferred the power of jurisdiction on the Apostolic College as a whole and on Peter in particular56 before His Ascension into heaven. Because of this, the Church is a perfect society in its own order. It is independent of

civil society and endowed with the necessary means to bring its members to their end.57

Even though present in time as the seedling of the kingdom of heaven, the Church has a saving and an eschatological purpose which can be fully attained only in the future world. But she is already present in this world, and is composed of men, that is, of members of the earthly city who have a call to form the family of God’s children during the present history of the human race, and to keep increasing it until the Lord returns.58 However, she will obtain her full perfection only in the glory of heaven. Then will come the time for the restoration of all things.59  Then the human race as well as the entire world, which is intimately related to man and achieves its purpose through him, will be perfectly reestablished in Christ... However, until there is a new heaven and a new earth where justice dwells,60  the pilgrim Church in her sacraments and institutions, which pertain to this present time, takes on the appearance of this passing world. She herself dwells among creatures who groan and travail in pain until now and await the revelation of the sons of God.61

The Church is both a hierarchical and a fraternal society. It is hierarchical because for the nurturing and constant growth of the People of God, Christ the Lord instituted in His Church a variety of ministries, which work for the good of the whole body.62  Catholic doctrine affirms that this power of jurisdiction which pertains solely to the Hierarchy does not proceed from the community of the faithful but comes directly from God.63  But the Church is above all a fraternal society because all share a true equality with regard to the dignity and to the activity common to all the faithful for the building up of the Body of Christ. Just as the laity by divine condescension have Christ for their brother who, though He is the Lord of all, came not to be served but to serve,64  so too do they have for their brothers, those in the sacred ministry who by teaching, by sanctifying, and by ruling with the authority of Christ so feed the family of God that the New Commandment of charity may be fulfilled by all.65

The fact that the Church is human and supernatural, visible and invisible, juridical and charismatic, temporal and eschatological, local and universal, fraternal and at the same time hierarchical does not endanger her unity. On the contrary, it confirms and maintains it. The society furnished with hierarchical agencies and the Mystical Body of Christ are not to be considered as two realities, nor are the visible assembly and the spiritual community, nor the earthly Church and the Church enriched with heavenly riches. Together they form one interlocked reality which is comprised of a divine and a human element.66  But the mystery of the Church, warns Paul VI, is not a mere object of theological knowledge; it is something to be lived, something of which the faithful soul can have a kind of connatural experience, even before arriving at a clear notion of it.67  Only thus will the difficulties which today trouble students of ecclesiology...be overcome in practice and solved by those who, after being enlightened by sound teaching, experience the living reality of the Church herself.68

Marks of the Church

We believe...in One, Holy, Catholic and Apostolic Church.69  All

the symbols of faith profess the existence of certain characteristic marks of Christ’s Church by which it can be recognized by all as the guardian and teacher of the revealed word.70  The Magisterium teaches that the true Church of Jesus Christ is constituted by divine authority and is known by four marks. We lay down these marks as matters of faith in the Creed. And any one of the marks is so joined to the others that it cannot be separated from them. Hence, the Church that really is Catholic, and is called Catholic, must, at the same time, shine with the prerogatives of unity, sanctity, and apostolic succession.71  In these marks the Church carries within her, and spreads about her, her own defence. Whoever beholds her and studies her with truthloving eyes must admit that, independently of her members and of the practical ways in which she is presented, she carries with her a message of light that is universal and unique, liberating and necessary; in a word, divine.72

Jesus Christ founded the one Church. He did not plan and establish a Church made up of a number of organisations that were generically similar yet separate and without those bonds of unity which make the Church one and indivisible... When Jesus Christ spoke of this mystical structure, He spoke of one Church only, which He called His own: “I will build my Church”.73  Since no other Church than this was founded by Jesus Christ...all who depart from it depart from the will and command of Christ our Lord. They have left the path of salvation and are heading toward destruction.74  Don’t you see, writes the Father, that those who separate themselves from the Church, even though at the time they may be full of fruit, turn into a living mass of worms? Love the One, Holy, Apostolic and Roman Church.75

This one Church of Christ maintains its unity by the bonds of the profession of faith, of the sacraments, of ecclesiastical government and of communion.76  Only the breaking of those bonds separates the faithful from the Mystical Body. Therefore only those are really to be included as members of the Church who received the regenerating waters of Baptism and profess the true faith and who have not unhappily separated from the body’s unity or for most grave faults been excluded by the legitimate authority.77  One must not think that the Body of the Church is made up only of members conspicuous for their holiness or that she consists only of the group of those whom God has predestined to eternal happiness.78  The men who make up the Church are made of Adam’s clay. They can be sinners and they often are. The Church is holy in her structure and she can be sinful in the human members in which she is realised. She is holy in the search for sanctity. She is at the same time holy and penitent, holy in herself, sick in the men who belong to her.79   Even though she gathers sinners within her, we believe that the Church is unfailingly holy. For Christ, the Son of God, loved the Church as His Bride, delivering Himself up for her. This He did that He might sanctify her.80  He united her to Himself as His own body and crowned her with the gift of the Holy Spirit for God’s glory.81  And although her members will reach perfection only in the glory of heaven, even now on this earth the Church is marked with a genuine holiness,82  which is unceasingly manifested, as it ought to be, through those fruits of grace that the Spirit produces in the faithful. It is expressed in multiple

ways by those individuals who, in their walk of life, strive for the perfection of charity, and thereby help others to grow.83  Thus, it is evident to everyone that all the faithful of Christ of whatever rank or status are called to the fullness of the Christian life and to the perfection of charity.84

The Church of Christ is also Catholic, universal. Her embrace unifies different peoples and different cultures. The Magisterium affirms and the history of the Church confirms that all men are called to belong to the new People of God. Wherefore this People, while remaining one and unique, is to be spread throughout the whole world and must exist in all ages, so that the purpose of God’s will may be fulfilled. In the beginning God made human nature one. After His children were scattered, He decreed that they should at length be unified again. It follows that among all the nations of earth there is but one People of God, which takes its citizens from every race,  making them citizens of a kingdom which is of a heavenly and not an earthly nature... Since the kingdom of Christ is not of this world,85  the Church or People of God takes nothing away from the temporal welfare of any people by establishing that kingdom. Rather does she foster and take to herself, insofar as they are good, the ability, resources, and customs of each people. Taking them to herself, she purifies, strengthens and ennobles them.86

We profess a fourth mark in the Creed the apostolicity of the Church, which  spread throughout the world  continues the mission Jesus Christ gave to the twelve Apostles. That divine mission, entrusted by Christ to the Apostles, will last until the end of the world,87 since the Gospel which was to be handed down by them is for all time the source of all life for the Church. For this reason the Apostles took care to appoint successors in this hierarchically structured society.88  The Magisterium teaches that by divine institution the bishops have taken the place of the Apostles as shepherds of the Church,89 and that he who hears them hears Christ.90  In this way the apostolic doctrine is guarded and transmitted by the bishops in communion with their head, the Roman Pontiff to whom Christ in the person of Peter, the prince of the Apostles gave full power over His Church.

One, Holy, Catholic and Apostolic. Four marks which define the Church founded by Jesus Christ and which can only be found integrally in Catholicism. This is the only Church of Jesus Christ, which we confess in the Creed to be one, holy, Catholic and Apostolic.91  After his Resurrection our Saviour handed her over to Peter to be shepherded,92 commissioning him and the other apostles to propagate and govern her.93  He erected her for all ages as “the pillar and mainstay of the truth”.94  This Church, constituted and organised in the world as a society subsists in the Catholic Church, which is governed by the successor of Peter and by the bishops in union with that successor, although many elements of sanctification and of truth can be found outside of her visible structure. Since these gifts belong to the Church of Christ, they are forces impelling towards Catholic unity.95  Though parts of the truth can be found in nonCatholic Christian religions, it is through Christ’s Catholic Church alone, which is the allembracing means of salvation, that the fullness of the means of salvation can be obtained.96

My sons, the Father tells us, I love all men very much, Catholics and nonCatholics, those who believe in something, and those who don’t  they make me feel pity. But Christ founded only one Church, Christ has only one Spouse. Christian unity? Yes, and what is more, the unity of all those who believe in God. But there is only one Christian Church. To achieve the unity of Christians, Mother Church never ceases to pray, hope, and work that they may gain this blessing. She exhorts her children to purify and renew themselves so that the mark of Christ may shine more clearly on the face of the Church.97  And moved by the Spirit of God dwelling within her, the Church prays and labors that the fullness of the whole  world should pass over to join the People of God, the Body of the Lord and the Temple of the Holy Spirit, and that all honour and glory be paid in Christ, the Head of all, to the Creator and Father of all.98
