## THE SACRAMENTS OF THE CHURCH {#cuaderno.1.6}

The whole life of Christ had redemptive value. Nevertheless, the Redemption was *achieved principally by the paschal mystery of his blessed Passion, Resurrection from the dead, and glorious Ascension, whereby "dying, he destroyed our death and, rising, he restored our life".*[^c06-1] [^c06-2] In order to perpetuate in time his saving death and resurrection so that all men might obtain the effects of his sacrifice, our Lord wanted to entrust to his Church certain specific means of sanctification which are the sacraments.

*Institution of the sacraments by Jesus Christ*

That Christ himself directly instituted some of the sacraments is clearly shown in Sacred Scripture. Christ revealed the sacrament of baptism to Nicodemus at the beginning of his public life[^c06-3]^\ ^and he solemnly instituted it before his Ascension into heaven.[^c06-4]^\ ^Christ promised the Eucharist, the sacramental sacrifice of his Body and Blood, in the synagogue of Capharnaum.[^c06-5] He instituted it, together with the sacrament of Holy Orders, during the Last Supper: *For I received from the Lord*, St Paul writes to the Corinthians, *what I also have taught to you, that the Lord Jesus, on the night on which he was betrayed, took bread and, giving thanks, he broke it and said, This is my Body which shall be given up for you: do this in commemoration of me. In like manner also the cup, after he had supped, saying, This cup is the new covenant in my Blood: do this, as often as you drink it, in remembrance of me*.[^c06-6]

On another occasion Jesus promised the Twelve the supernatural power of forgiving sins, which he himself frequently exercised during his lifetime.[^c06-7] *Whatever you bind on earth*, he told them, *will also be bound in heaven*.[^c06-8] This power to bind and to loose, which includes the power to forgive sins, was given to the Apostles by Christ glorified, on the day of his resurrection: *As the Father has sent me, I also send you. When he had said this, he breathed upon them, and said to them, Receive the Holy Spirit, whose sins you shall forgive, they are forgiven them, and whose sins you shall retain, they are retained*.[^c06-9]

The institution of the other sacraments does not appear so clearly in the Gospel. But Sacred Scripture does speak about their being instituted by Jesus Christ. From the very beginning, the Apostles confirmed those who had been baptised, giving them the fullness of the Holy Spirit by the imposition of hands.[^c06-10] James the Less speaks clearly about the Anointing of the Sick as something perfectly well known.[^c06-11] Christ reaffirmed the unity and indissolubility of matrimony[^c06-12] and St Paul speaks highly of it as a something that can only be understood in the light of its having been raised to a supernatural level. For he says to the faithful at Ephesus, *Let wives be subject to their husbands, as to the Lord; because a husband is head of the wife just as Christ is head of the Church\... Husbands, love your wives, in the same way that Christ loved the Church and delivered himself up for her\... This is a great sacrament, moreover I say this in reference to Christ and to the Church*.[^c06-13]

The Council of Trent solemnly defined that all the sacraments were instituted by Christ: *If anyone says that the sacraments of the New Law were not all instituted by Jesus Christ our Lord\... let him be anathema*.[^c06-14]

*Structure of the sacraments*

While they are different from one another, all the Sacraments have something in common, *being a symbol of something sacred and a visible manifestation of invisible grace.*[^c06-15] They consist of a material and visible element which hides and at the same time manifests a spiritual and invisible reality. Catholic theologians, following the indications of the Magisterium, explain that these sensible signs are most suitable, for *divine Wisdom provided for each thing according to its condition\... and it is natural for man to understand things through his senses.*[^c06-16] Besides, the very structure of the Sacraments --- they are made up of things as the matter and words as the form[^c06-17] --- makes them similar to Jesus Christ, *for in the mystery of the Incarnation, the Word of God united himself to sensible flesh*[^c06-18] thus sanctifying all material realities. ***What are the sacraments, which people in early times described as the footprints of the Incarnate Word, if not the clearest expression of this way which God has chosen in order to sanctify us and to lead us to heaven? Don**'**t you see that each sacrament is the love of God, with all of its creative and redemptive power, given to us through the medium of material things?***[^c06-19]

The economy of salvation willed by God is therefore a sacramental economy. Already when he chose the Israelites as his own people, he established certain tangible rites which symbolised salvific grace: circumcision, the purifications, the paschal lamb\... But the sacraments of the New Law *differ greatly from the sacraments of the Old Law. The sacraments of the Old Law did not produce grace but were only a figure of the grace that was to be given through the Passion of Christ. But our sacraments both contain grace and confer it on those who receive them worthily.*[^c06-20] Moreover, the Council of Trent taught that they are not only outward signs to increase faith;[^c06-21] not as some reformers said, are they mere signs of divine justification,[^c06-22] but *by means of the sacraments of the New Law\... grace is conferred "ex opere operato",*[^c06-23] conferred, that is, by virtue of the sacramental rite itself, provided of course that the necessary conditions are fulfilled. *All the sacraments*, the Council of Florence affirms, *are composed of three elements: things as matter, words as form, and the person of the minister who confers the sacrament with the intention of doing what the Church does. And if anyone of these three is lacking, the sacrament is invalid.*[^c06-24]

*Furthermore, the same Holy Roman Church holds and teaches that there are seven sacraments of the Church. One is Baptism\... Another is the sacrament of Confirmation which bishops confer by the imposition of hands, anointing those who have been reborn. Then there is Penance, the Eucharist, the sacrament of Orders, Matrimony and Extreme Unction which, according to the teachings of St James, is administered to the sick.*[^c06-25] By means of analogy, the Magisterium has explained the function of each sacrament in the life of man and of the Church. Just as there is in natural life, a dual perfection, that of the individual and that of the community, so *the first five sacraments are ordered to the interior spiritual perfection of the individual; the last two are ordered to the government and to the spread of the whole Church. For by Baptism we are spiritually reborn and by Confirmation we grow in grace and are strengthened in faith, being reborn and strengthened, we are nourished with the divine food of the Eucharist. If, we become sick in soul, Penance heals us spiritually; Extreme Unction heals us in spirit as well as in body, according to the state of the soul. By Holy Orders the Church is governed and given spiritual growth; by Matrimony She is given bodily growth*.[^c06-26]

*Administration of the sacraments: Conditions for validity and licitude*

For the validity of the sacraments, besides the matter and form required by the Church, the presence of a minister is also necessary. In almost all of the sacraments, the priest is the minister who, *by Baptism brings men into the People of God. By the sacrament of Penance sinners are reconciled to God and to the Church. By the Anointing of the Sick the ailing find relief. And, especially by the celebration of Mass, men offer sacramentally the Sacrifice of Christ*.[^c06-27] In the case of Baptism, the sacrament is absolutely necessary for salvation, when there is an urgent need, *not only a priest or a deacon can baptise, but also a lay man or a woman, and even a pagan or heretic provided he observe the form established by the Church and has the intention of doing what the Church does*.[^c06-28] On the other hand, *bishops are the ordinary ministers of the sacrament of Confirmation; they confer Holy Orders and regulate the penitential discipline*,[^c06-29] while in Matrimony the ministers are the couple themselves.

The Church, making use of the power which Christ gave her, can stipulate conditions for the valid administration of the sacraments but always *salva illorum substantia*,[^c06-30] that is, provided it does not affect *those things which Christ our Lord established to be observed in the sacramental sign according to the testimony of the sources of revelation*.[^c06-31] The Church has done this, for example, in the case of Matrimony when she declared that *those who would attempt marriage without the presence of a parish priest --- or another priest with the permission of the parish priest or the Ordinary --- and two or three witnesses, are forbidden to do so by the Council, which further decrees all such contracts to be null and void.*[^c06-32]

The minister performs his ministry in the name of Christ and with his authority, for Christ is present by his operative power in the sacraments in such a manner that when someone baptises it is really Christ himself who baptises.[^c06-33] For this reason, a sacrament administered by an unworthy minister is valid provided he performs it with the intention of doing what the Church does and meets the other requirements. Throughout history, the Roman Pontiffs have explained this truth of faith most fully.[^c06-34] Nevertheless, to administer the sacrament licitly, it is ordinarily necessary to have a canonical mission which is conferred on the priest by ecclesiastical authority.[^c06-35] Christ gave this sacramental power solely to the Church, and she alone can administer it fruitfully.

Certain conditions are required on the part of the subject, however, in order to receive the sacraments validly. With regard to Baptism, any living person who has not yet received the Sacrament can be baptised. But if an adult is to be baptised, he must also have the intention of being baptised; and if it were done against his will, that is, if he were *absolutely unwilling, he would receive neither the grace nor the character of the sacrament.*[^c06-36] Those adults who are to be baptised ought to prepare themselves well, explicitly believing not only in the existence of a God who rewards,[^c06-37] but also in *the mysteries of the faith which are absolutely necessary means for salvation and these are principally the mysteries of the Trinity and of the Incarnation*.[^c06-38] They must put aside all affection for sin. If the person being baptised is an infant, the Church herself, through the lips of the godparents, provides the explicit act of faith which the child cannot profess because it lacks the use of reason.[^c06-39]

For the valid reception of the other sacraments, besides having the intention of receiving them, it is also necessary to have been baptised validly, since Baptism *is the door to the spiritual life*[^c06-40] and the foundation of the other sacraments. In order to receive them licitly and to one's advantage, one must have the proper dispositions, e.g., in the sacrament of Penance, an aversion to mortal sin with sorrow for having committed it and a firm purpose of amendment,[^c06-41] and in the other sacraments, the state of grace.[^c06-42]

*The need for, and the effects of\--,- the sacraments*

The Magisterium of the Church states that all these means of sanctification *are necessary for salvation\...; although not all the sacraments are necessary for each individual*.[^c06-43] Baptism is always necessary, both as an absolute necessity and as a necessary means. This is clear from our Lord's words to Nicodemus: *Unless a man be born again of water and the Spirit, he cannot enter the kingdom of God*.[^c06-44] Penance is also necessary if one has committed mortal sin.[^c06-45] Nevertheless, the mere desire to receive these sacraments, if it is effective --- that is, if it is accompanied by a *perfect* act of contrition and the firm resolution to go to Confession as soon as possible --- is already sufficient for God to infuse grace into the soul.[^c06-46] Adults ought also to receive the Eucharist, the food of immortality: *Unless you eat the flesh of the Son of Man and drink his Blood, you shall not have life in you. He who eats my flesh and drinks my Blood has life everlasting and I will raise him up on the last day*.[^c06-47] In accord with the precept of the Church this obligation is fulfilled *by receiving the sacrament of the Eucharist devoutly at least during the Easter season.*[^c06-48]

When the sacraments are received with the requisite proper dispositions, they always give abundant fruit. *It is true that the sacraments and the Sacrifice of the Altar have an intrinsic power insofar as they are acts of Christ himself who communicates and diffuses grace from the divine head into the members of the Mystical Body. Moreover, for them to be really effective, they demand the right disposition of our soul\... In the spiritual life, consequently, there should be no opposition or contradiction between the action of God, who pours forth his grace into men's hearts to perpetuate the work of redemption, and the effective collaboration of men, who must not nullify the gift of God*.[^c06-49] *Neither can there be a conflict between efficacy of the external rite, which arises "ex opere operato", and the worthiness of the one who administers or receives them whose act is usually called "opus operantis"*.[^c06-50]

The grace conferred by the sacraments is called "habitual" or "sanctifying" grace, the permanent principle of supernatural life.[^c06-51] It is an infused quality inhering in the soul *by which man is adopted as a son of God, is renewed interiorly and participates in the divine nature*.[^c06-52] Together with grace, God himself --- the Father, the Son and the Holy Spirit --- takes possession of the soul of the just man and makes it his dwelling place. *Do you not know* --- wrote St Paul to the Corinthians --- *that you are the temple of God and that the Spirit of God dwells within you?*[^c06-53] The divine presence in man is entirely supernatural. It is of an order which is far superior to that presence through which God maintains all creatures in existence,[^c06-54] inexpressibly greater also than that other, *natural, presence proper to a spiritual creature, created in the image and likeness of God*,[^c06-55] through which he is made *a sharer in the light of the divine intellect*;[^c06-56] and gifted with free will, *the lofty sign of the image of God in man*.[^c06-57]

***God is with us. In our soul in grace, there dwells the Blessed Trinity. For this reason, despite our miseries, we can and we ought to be conversing continually with our Lord.*** The indwelling of the Holy Trinity in the souls of the just is a truth of divine faith revealed by Jesus Christ to the Apostles during the Last Supper. *If anyone loves me, he will keep my word and my Father will love him, and We will come to him and make our abode in him*.[^c06-58] Even though the three divine Persons dwell in the just, Sacred Scripture attributes this presence, in a special way, to the Holy Spirit. He is the seal with which Christians are stamped,[^c06-59] the guarantee of the future glorification of our bodies,[^c06-60] help in our weakness.[^c06-61]

The Tradition of the Church has always considered this to be a truth of faith. According to the Fathers, the purpose of the acts of penance which precede the baptism of adults is to prepare a worthy dwelling place in the soul for the Holy Spirit.[^c06-62] On the other hand, when man commits a mortal sin he violates this temple of God[^c06-63] and forces the Spirit to leave him. But the Spirit returns when the sinner receives sacramental absolution.[^c06-64] *No one, therefore, reaches sanctity without the presence of the Holy Spirit*.[^c06-65] In order for the silent presence of the Spirit to bear more fruit we must want to know him and converse with him. St Augustine asks, *Why among the saints are some more saintly than others, if it is not because God dwells more intimately in some?*[^c06-66] For this reason, ***along with human virtues, my daughters and sons, we must foster the theological virtues* *---* *your life of faith, of hope and of charity* *---* *given to us in baptism through the ineffable action of the Holy Spirit. The Great Stranger* *---* *as I like to call him* *---* *lives, as I was saying, in the souls of those who are in God**'**s grace, transforming them into a dwelling of the Blessed Trinity.***[^c06-67]

The sacraments incorporate the Christian into Jesus Christ,[^c06-68] making the members of the Mystical Body like their head. *It is necessary that all the members should be made like him to the point where Christ is fully formed in them*.[^c06-69] *In this way, we share the mysteries of his life, made like to him in his death and resurrection, until we finally reign with him*.[^c06-70] This identification with our Lord, suffering and glorified, takes place *in a hidden but real way through the sacraments*.[^c06-71] Theology attributes these effects to the sacramental grace which is specific to each sacrament and which is directed toward strengthening the Christian in the different aspects of his supernatural life which are symbolised in the sacramental rite. This makes the Christian participate in different ways in the Passion, death and resurrection of Jesus Christ. *Thus, by Baptism, men are plunged into the Paschal Mystery of Christ: they die with him, are buried with him, and they rise with him\... As often as they eat the Supper of the Lord they proclaim the death of the Lord until he comes\...*[^c06-72]

Among the sacraments there *are three --- Baptism, Confirmation and Holy Orders --- which imprint a character on the soul; that is, a certain indelible mark which distinguishes it from other souls. From this it is deduced that these cannot be received more than once by the same person*.[^c06-73] The faithful *are destined by the character of Baptism for the worship of the Christian religion. Reborn as sons of God, they must confess before men the faith which they have received from God through the Church*.[^c06-74] The character of Confirmation unites them more closely with the Church, enriching those who receive it *with special strength from the Holy Spirit so that they are more strictly obliged to spread and defend the faith, both by word and by deed, as true witnesses of Christ*.[^c06-75] With the reception of these two sacraments the Christian participates in the priesthood of Christ[^c06-76] and thus is transformed into ***a guide, teacher and priest to his fellow men; he is for them, another Christ,*** **alter Christus,** ***or rather, as I usually say to you,*** **ipse Christus.**[^c06-77]

The character of the Sacrament of Holy Orders enables those who receive it to enter the hierarchy, destining them --- by means of the canonical mission *to feed the Church, in Christ's name, with the Word and the grace of God.*[^c06-78] *Though they differ from one another in essence and not only in degree*, the two priesthoods, *the common priesthood of the faithful and the ministerial or hierarchical priesthood are nonetheless interrelated. Each of them in its own special way is a participation in the one priesthood of Christ*.[^c06-79] *The ministerial priesthood, by the sacred power it enjoys, moulds and rules the priestly people, and performs the eucharistic sacrifice, offering it to God in the name of all the people. For their part, the faithful, in virtue of their royal priesthood, join in the offering of the Eucharist*,[^c06-80] *and exercise their priesthood by receiving the sacraments, by prayer and thanksgiving, by the witness of a holy life, and by self-denial and active charity*.[^c06-81]

***How good Christ was to leave the sacraments to his Church! They are the remedy for all our needs. Venerate them and be very grateful both to God and to his Church.***[^c06-82] The sanctification produced by the sacraments is an ontological sanctification which transforms man into Jesus Christ. It makes him a dwelling place of the Holy Trinity and a son of God. Of course, we will only reach the fullness of divine filiation in heaven.[^c06-83] And it is toward this goal that the saving work of the Church directs us. But even now, through the sacraments, *we are being transformed into his very image from glory to glory, through the Spirit of the Lord*,[^c06-84] until we become *conformed to the image of his Son, so that he should be the first-born among many brethren*.[^c06-85] In this way, what the Church states will be completely fulfilled: that *through Christ, the Word made Flesh, man has access to the Father in the Holy Spirit and comes to share in the divine nature*.[^c06-86]

## THE SACRIFICE OF THE ALTAR {#cuaderno.1.7}

Under the infallible guidance of Sacred Scripture and the Magisterium of the Church, we have considered the work of Redemption carried out by Jesus Christ, who *hanging on the Cross offered himself a Victim unblemished in God's sight that he might purify our conscience and set us free from lifeless observances in order to serve the living God*.[^c07-1]

Having completed his sacrifice on Calvary and risen by the power of God, he ascended into heaven. In order that there always be a memorial of his sacrifice on earth and that all men benefit from its fruits, *our Divine Redeemer willed that the priestly life, begun in his mortal body by his prayers and Sacrifice should continue unceasingly in his Mystical Body, which is the Church*.[^c07-2]

*The Holy Mass, a true and fitting sacrifice*

The Catholic Faith teaches that Christ, *a priest forever according to the order of Melchisedech*,[^c07-3] wished to perpetuate the work of redemption on earth. *And so, at the Last Supper, on the night on which he was betrayed, in order to leave for his beloved Spouse, the Church, a sacrifice that was visible, as the nature of man demands\... he offered his Body and Blood under the under the species of bread and wine to God the Father and he gave his Body and Blood under the same species to the Apostles to receive, making them priests of the New Testament at that time. This sacrifice was to represent the bloody Sacrifice which he accomplished on the Cross once and for all. It was to perpetuate his memory until the end of the world. Its salutary strength was to be applied for the remission of the sins that we daily commit. He ordered the Apostles and their successors in the priesthood to offer this Sacrifice.*[^c07-4]

The sacrificial nature of the Mass is directly deduced from the words Christ used in instituting the Eucharist. *This is my Body which shall be given up for you*,[^c07-5] he said to his disciples at the Last Supper. Afterwards, taking the chalice in his Hands, he affirmed: *This is my Blood of the new covenant, which is being shed for many unto the forgiveness of sins*.[^c07-6]

A few years after the institution of the sacrament, *the Apostle Paul, who has faithfully transmitted to us what he had received from the Lord*,[^c07-7] *clearly speaks of the eucharistic sacrifice when he points out that Christians, precisely because they have been made partakers of the table of the Lord, must not take part in pagan sacrifices. "The cup of blessing that we bless," he says, "is it not the sharing of the blood of Christ?\... You cannot drink the cup of the Lord and the cup of devils; you cannot be partakers of the table of the Lord and of the table of devils."*[^c07-8] [^c07-9]

The reality of the sacrifice of the Mass had already been prefigured in the Old Testament. When Abraham returned victorious from battle, *Melchisedech, the King of Salem, brought out bread and wine, for he was priest of the Most High God. He blessed Abraham*.[^c07-10] St Paul explicitly affirms that Melchisedech, *without father, without mother, without lineage, having neither beginning of days nor end of life*[^c07-11] was a "type" of Jesus Christ. And his sacrifice was a figure of the one which would be carried out in the New Covenant. Malachi had prophesied it as well: *From the rising of the sun even to its setting, my name is great among the nations; and everywhere they bring sacrifice to my name, and a pure offering*.[^c07-12] Thus the sacrifice of the altar is the new pasch, in which Christ, the Paschal Lamb, is *offered by the Church through her priests under visible signs, in memory of his departure from this world to the Father*.[^c07-13] Therefore the Council of Trent teaches: *If anyone says that in the Mass a true and proper sacrifice is not offered to God\... let him be anathema*.[^c07-14]

*The august sacrifice of the altar is, therefore, no mere simple commemoration of the Passion and Death of Jesus Christ; it is truly and properly the offering of a sacrifice, wherein by an unbloody immolation the High Priest does what he had done on the Cross, offering himself to the Eternal Father as a most acceptable victim*.[^c07-15] In the Holy Mass, just as on Calvary, Jesus Christ is at the same time Priest and Victim. *For it is one and the same Victim: He who now makes the Offering through the ministry of priests and He who then offered himself on the Cross; the only difference is in the manner of the offering*.[^c07-16] On the Cross, Jesus suffered death with the real shedding of his Blood. But on the altar, by reason of the glorious condition of his Humanity, "death will no longer have dominion over him",[^c07-17] and therefore the shedding of his Blood is not possible.[^c07-18] However, the sacrificial reality, although it be unbloody, is also shown forth *by external signs symbolic of his death. By the "transubstantiation" of bread into the Body of Christ and of the wine into his Blood, both his Body and his Blood are rendered really present; but the eucharistic species under which he is present symbolise the violent separation of his Body and his Blood, and so a commemorative showing forth of the death which took place in reality on Calvary is repeated in each Holy Mass, because, by distinct representations, Christ Jesus is signified and shown forth in the state of a victim*.[^c07-19]

Since the sacrifice of the Mass is identical to that of Calvary, it does not lessen in any way the dignity and infinite value of the unique redemptive sacrifice. *On the contrary, it is a clear proof\... of its greatness and necessity\... The daily immolation is a reminder to us that there is no salvation but the Cross of our Lord Jesus Christ*.[^c07-20]

The inner nature of the sacrifice of the altar does not consist in receiving in Communion the Victim being offered.[^c07-21] *It must be emphasised again and again* --- the Magisterium of the Church teaches - *that the eucharistic sacrifice is, by its very nature, the unbloody immolation of the divine victim, an immolation mystically manifested in the separation of the sacred species and the offering made of them to the Eternal Father. Holy Communion, undoubtedly, appertains to the integrity of the sacrifice; yet, though it is entirely necessary for the sacrificing minister, for the faithful, it is but something to be highly recommended*.[^c07-22] So, contrary to what Protestant reformers have asserted, for the Mass to be a true sacrifice, complete in its own right, Communion of the faithful is not necessary. *Should anyone say that Masses in which the priest alone communicates sacramentally are illicit and should be done away with: let him be anathema*.[^c07-23] Therefore, *it is false doctrine that would lead a priest to refuse to celebrate unless the faithful come to Holy Communion. And it is still worse to ground this view --- that the faithful must necessarily communicate with the priest --- on the sophistical contention that the Holy Mass, besides being a Sacrifice, is also a banquet of the community of brethren that the general Communion of the faithful is to be regarded as the culminating point of the whole celebration*.[^c07-24]

Although it is not necessary for the integrity of the Sacrifice, the Church has always desired *that in every Mass the faithful who are present communicate not only spiritually, but by a sacramental reception of the Eucharist, so that they might receive greater benefits from this most holy Sacrifice*.[^c07-25] She even recommends that they do so with *particles which have been consecrated at the same Holy Mass so as to ive real fulfilment to the words, "that as many of us who have received the Sacred Body and Blood of Thy Son by partaking of this altar, may be filled with every heavenly grace and blessing."*[^c07-26] [^c07-27]

*The Holy Mass, the sacrifice of Christ and of the Church*

If it is not necessary that the faithful receive Holy Communion for the sacrifice of the Mass to be complete, neither is it required that they be present at the eucharistic celebration or that they give their consent to the sacred action for the Holy Sacrifice to take place. The Magisterium puts us on guard against that error. *There are some who entirely disapprove of Masses that are offered privately and without a congregation, as though these were a departure from the original form of sacrifice. Some even say that priests cannot say Mass at several altars at the same time because this is to split up the community and jeopardise its unity. And there even are others who go so far as to claim that it is necessary for the people to confirm and ratify the Sacrifice in order that it may have its power and efficacy*.[^c07-28]

Catholic doctrine teaches that all Masses, even though celebrated privately, *are to be considered truly public*.[^c07-29] *Every time the priest re-enacts what the Divine Redeemer did at the Last Supper, the Sacrifice is really accomplished. And this sacrifice, always and everywhere, necessarily and of its very nature, has a public and social character. For he who offers it, acts in the name both of Christ and of the faithful, of whom the Divine Redeemer is the dead*.[^c07-30] Therefore *every Mass, even though a priest should offer it in private, is not something private; it is an act of Christ and of the Church*.[^c07-31]

***In every Mass the whole Christ, Head and Body, is present! The Mass is the Sacrifice of Christ and of his Holy Church. In celebrating a private Mass* *---* *which I have always loved very much* *---* *when I say,* Dominus vobiscum*, I say it to the whole Church.*** **Redemisti nos Deo sanguine tuo ex omni tribu, et lingua, et populo, et natione *(Rev 5:9), because Jesus Christ has redeemed us, for God, from all the tribes, and tongues, and peoples and nations.***

These false ideas are old heresies which are revived from time to time. **We must state that none of the faithful may think that private Masses, those in which only the priest receives the Eucharist, lose the value of the true, perfect and integral Sacrifice instituted by Christ our Lord, and therefore should be considered illicit. The faithful are not unaware, or at least they can easily be instructed in this matter, that the august Council of Trent, basing itself on the doctrine that has preserved the perpetual tradition of the Church, condemned the new and false doctrine of Luther which taught the contrary** ***(Benedict XIV)***.[^c07-32] [^c07-33]

*Every Mass which is celebrated* --- the Magisterium of the Church concludes --- *is offered not for the salvation of ourselves alone, but also for that of the whole world*.[^c07-34] *And this happens whether the faithful are present\... or whether they are absent, because it is in no way necessary that the people should ratify what has been done by the sacred minister*.[^c07-35] However, *the very nature of the action renders most appropriate the active participation of many of the faithful in the celebration of Mass*,[^c07-36] in order that the oblation whereby they offer the divine victim to the heavenly Father may produce its full effect.[^c07-37]

*The ministerial or hierarchical priesthood*

*In conformity to God's decree, sacrifice and priesthood are so related that both have existed in every law. Therefore in the New Testament, since ever the Catholic Church has received the holy and visible sacrifice of the Eucharist according to the institution of the Lord, it is also necessary to acknowledge that there is, in the Church a new, visible, and external priesthood, into which the old priesthood was changed*.[^c07-38]

Jesus Christ personally exercised this new priesthood while he lived on earth. *In the days of his earthly life, with a loud cry and tears, he offered up prayers and supplications to him who was able to save Him from death, and was heard because of his reverent submission\... And when perfected, he became the cause of eternal salvation, called by God a high priest according to the order of Melchisedech*.[^c07-39] Having risen from the dead and ascended into heaven, *because he continues forever, he has an everlasting priesthood. Therefore he is able at all times to save those who come to God through him, since he lives perpetually to make intercession for them*.[^c07-40] Nevertheless, in order to perpetuate the Sacrifice of the Cross on earth, during the Last Supper *the power of consecrating, offering, and administering his Body and Blood, and likewise the power of remitting or of retaining sins, was given to the Apostles and to their successors in the priesthood*.[^c07-41]

Only men who have been baptised can receive the Sacrament of Holy Orders.[^c07-42] And for the validity of their ordination, the minister consecrating them must be a Bishop.[^c07-43] The Church also teaches that *in the ordination of bishops, of priests, and of other grades, the consent, call or authority neither of the people nor of any secular power or authority is necessary to the extent that without it the ordination is invalid. Rather it decrees that all those who have been called and appointed merely by the people or by the secular power or ruler, and thus undertake to exercise these ministries, and that all those who arrogate these ministries to themselves on their own authority are not ministers of the Church but should be considered as thieves and robbers who have not entered through the door*.[^c07-44] [^c07-45]

For ordination to be licit, it is necessary that the consecrating bishop be in full communion with the Apostolic See,[^c07-46] that the candidate be free from any irregularity and be motivated by a right intention,[^c07-47] that he be morally upright and pious, that he have received Confirmation, that he be of the required age and have the necessary training in theology,[^c07-48] and that he have the necessary permission for ordination.[^c07-49] However, *in the Sacrifice of the Altar we believe that a good priest does nothing more, and a bad priest does nothing less; because it is not by the merit of the one consecrating that the Sacrifice is accomplished, but by the word of the Creator and by the power of the Holy Spirit*.[^c07-50]

A particular characteristic of this sacrament of Holy Orders is its distribution in hierarchical grades or orders.[^c07-51] Together with the office of the Roman Pontiff, Christ himself instituted the episcopate, the priesthood and the diaconate, so that *if anyone says that in the Catholic Church there is no divinely instituted hierarchy consisting of bishops, priests, and ministers: let him be anathema*.[^c07-52]

*Characteristics of the priestly mission*

Priests, having been made sharers in the priesthood of Christ, are ordained *in order to preach the Gospel and to shepherd the faithful, as well as to celebrate divine worship as true priests of the New Testament. On the level of their own ministry, sharing in the unique office of Christ the mediator*,[^c07-53] they announce to all the Word of God. However, it is in the eucharistic cult or in the eucharistic assembly of the faithful that they exercise, in a supreme degree, their sacred functions; there, acting in the name of Christ and proclaiming his Mystery, they unite the votive offerings of the faithful to the Sacrifice of Christ their head, and in the Sacrifice of the Mass they make present again and apply, until the coming of the Lord,[^c07-54] *the unique sacrifice of the New Testament*.[^c07-55]

In addition to the grace received, through sacred ordination *a character is imprinted on the soul of the priest which can neither be blotted out nor taken away*.[^c07-56] It is a seal which gives priests the capacity to carry out *the priestly office publicly on behalf of men in the name of Christ*.[^c07-57] By reason of this all-embracing dimension of the sacramental character, priests ought to devote themselves entirely to their priesthood. Ordained to be *servants of Christ and stewards of the Mysteries of God*,[^c07-58] they ought to attend to their ministry with all their energies and potential, and give themselves to all men without discrimination. ***A priest, living his vocation in this way, feeling about it in this way, cannot be anything but this: A priest! Just as gold is gold; and silver is silver. A priest-socialworker? A priest-missionary? No: a priest is a priest. And the more profoundly he is so, the more keenly will he perceive* *---* *without the need to add categories* *---* *what the demands of justice and charity are, and what the needs of the environment and people are.***

The priest is ordained forever; his priesthood, like that of Christ, is eternal. *The Lord has sworn, and he will not repent: You are priest forever, according to the order of Melchisedech.*[^c07-59] However, the Father warns us: ***Nowadays we hear a lot about the priesthood* ad tempus*, and this is madness* *---* *as if a husband were only to be a husband at intervals.*** The mission of spiritual service to all men demands from the priest a complete and permanent dedication to his ministry. ***To serve is the greatest joy a soul can have. And this is what we priests have to do: be at the service of everyone day and night. If one of us is not so, he is not a priest. He has to love the young and old, the poor and the rich, sick people and children. He has to receive souls, one by one, like a shepherd who knows his flock and calls each sheep by its name. So you see, there is no time for anything. In addition, he has to prepare the homily, and then not lose his theological grasp of doctrine; he has to keep abreast of this so as not to let himself be deceived by people who call themselves theologians and who know nothing.***

In the end, the priesthood finds its most sublime reason of being in love. By divine vocation the priest ***is a man of Love, the representative among men of Love made-man. He lives through Jesus Christ, for Jesus Christ, with Jesus Christ and in Jesus Christ. Therefore you can well understand that those who say that they do not have love are either sick or crazy or else they do not know what the priesthood is. In any case we should have compassion for them, and pray for them.***

Celibacy is an expression of this love and dedication. Following the advice of her divine Master,[^c07-60] the Church has imposed it as a sweet burden on her priests from the first centuries and has continually confirmed it throughout the course of history.[^c07-61] *In the heart of a priest, love has not been extinguished*[^c07-62] on account of celibacy. On the contrary, *grace, with a divine force, increases the longings of love. And love, when it is genuine, is total, exclusive, stable and lasting, an irresistible spur to all forms of heroism. And so, the free choice of sacred celibacy has always been considered in the Church "as something that signifies and fosters charity,"*[^c07-63]*^\ ^*it signifies a love without reservations; it fosters a charity open to all.[^c07-64]

*By preserving virginity or celibacy for the kingdom of heaven* --- the last Ecumenical Council explains --- *prompted by love for the Kingdom of heaven, priests consecrate themselves to Christ, in a new and excellent way. They more readily cling to him with undivided hearts*[^c07-65] and *dedicate themselves more freely, in him and through him, to the service of God and of men. They are less encumbered in their service to the Kingdom and in the task of heavenly regeneration. In this way they become better fitted for a broader acceptance of fatherhood in Christ*.[^c07-66] Far from isolating himself in an empty and sterile life, *by dying to himself completely each day, and giving up, for the love of Christ and of his Kingdom, the legitimate love of a family of his own, the priest will find the glory of an exceedingly rich and fruitful life in Christ, because, like him and in him, he loves and dedicates himself to all the children of God*.[^c07-67]

The priest does not need any earthly love. *Dominus pars hereditatis meae.*[^c07-68] The Lord is the portion of inheritance that has fallen to him. Always being with God and, for the sake of God, with all men, the priest --- if he is faithful to that Love --- will never be lonely. *Let priests remember that, in going about their work, they are never alone, but are supported by the almighty power of God\... Let them remember too that they have their brothers in the priesthood and indeed the faithful of the entire world as allies*.[^c07-69]

***They lie who say that we priests are alone,*** insists the Father. ***No one has more company than we, because we have the continuous companionship of our Lord, with whom we dealing all the while. To feel accompanied, it is enough to deal a lot with God and to fulfil our duties. If any priest were alone, it would be because he has withdrawn from God, because he has wilfully moved away from divine grace***.

*The participation of the faithful in the sacrifice of the Mass*

By the sacramental character of Baptism and of Confirmation, the Christian faithful are also made *a chosen race, a royal priesthood, a holy nation, a purchased people*,[^c07-70] destined *to offer spiritual sacrifices acceptable to God through Jesus Christ*.[^c07-71]

By virtue of this participation in the priesthood of Christ, the faithful take an active part in the celebration of the Sacrifice of the Altar. *Not only do the priests offer the Sacrifice, but also all the faithful, since what is carried out especially by the ministry of the priests is universally wrought by the desire of the faithful*.[^c07-72] The liturgy of the Mass itself clearly expresses this participation, since *the prayers by which the Divine Victim is offered to God are said for the most part in the plural, and they more than once indicate that the people have a part in the Sacrifice as being offerers of it; for example, "We, Thy servant, as also the whole people\... do offer unto Thy most excellent Majesty, of these Thy gifts bestowed upon us, a pure Victim, a holy Victim, a spotless Victim"*.[^c07-73]

*But the fact that the faithful take part in the Eucharistic Sacrifice does not mean that they also possess the power of the priesthood*.[^c07-74] The Church maternally warns us against those *who, holding a view not far removed from errors that have been already condemned*,[^c07-75] *teach that the New Testament knows of no priesthood other than that which is common to all the baptised; that the command which Jesus Christ gave his Apostles at the Last Supper to do what he himself had done, was addressed directly to the whole community of the faithful; and that only later did the hierarchical priesthood take its rise. They therefore maintain that the people possess the true priestly power, and that the priest acts only by virtue of a function delegated to him by the community*.[^c07-76] On the contrary, Catholic doctrine affirms *that the priest acts in the name of the people precisely and only because he represents the Person of our Lord Jesus Christ, considered as head of all the members and offering himself for them; that the priest, therefore, approaches the altar as Christ's minister lower than Christ but higher than the people; that the people, on the other hand, because it in no way represents the Person of the Divine Redeemer and is not mediator between itself and God, can in no way possess the priestly power*.[^c07-77]

The Church also teaches that *though they differ essentially and not only in degree, the common priesthood of the faithful and the ministerial or hierarchical priesthood are nonetheless ordered one to another; each in its own proper way shares in the one priesthood of Christ.*[^c07-78]

Under the impulse of the Holy Spirit, the Magisterium of the Church has defined the due manner in which the faithful, by virtue of the royal priesthood, offer the eucharistic sacrifice. *The unbloody immolation by which, after the words of Consecration have been pronounced, Christ is rendered present on the altar in the state of victim, is performed by the priest alone, and by the priest in so far as he acts in the name of Christ, not in so far as he represents the faithful, but precisely because the priest places the divine Victim on the altar, he offers it as an oblation to God the Father for the glory of the Blessed Trinity and for the benefit of the whole Church. Now, understood in this restricted sense, the oblation is shared by the faithful, and for two reasons: first because they offer the Sacrifice through the priest, and secondly because, in a certain sense, they offer it with him. And because they have this part in the Sacrifice, the people's offering also belongs to the liturgical worship\...*

*But, when the people are said to offer with the priest, this does not mean that all the members of the Church, like the priest himself, perform the visible liturgical rite --- this is done only by the minister divinely appointed for the purpose --- rather, they are said to offer with him inasmuch as they unite their sentiments of praise, entreaty, expiation, and thanksgiving with the sentiments or intention of the priest, indeed with those of the High Priest himself, in order that in the very oblation of the Victim, those sentiments may be presented to God the Father also by the priest's external rite.*[^c07-79]

The principal victim of the Sacrifice, then, is Jesus Christ. But the faithful, in order to exercise their common priesthood fully, should unite sacrifice to that of our Lord and offer themselves also to God the Father. *I exhort you\... to present your bodies as a sacrifice, living, holy, pleasing to God, your spiritual service*,[^c07-80] wrote St Paul to the Romans. And he said to the Philippians, *have this mind in you which was also in Christ Jesus*.[^c07-81] This *requires all Christians, so far as human power allows, to reproduce in themselves the sentiments that Christ had when he was offering himself in sacrifice: sentiments of humility, of adoration, praise and thanksgiving to the divine Majesty. It requires them also to become victims, as it were; cultivating a spirit of self-denial according to the precepts of the Gospel, willingly doing works of penance, detesting and expiating their sins. It requires us all, in a word, to die mystically with Jesus Christ on the Cross, so that we may say with the same Apostle: "With Jesus Christ I hang upon the Cross."*[^c07-82] [^c07-83]

In this way, the Mass will be for the faithful, too, the Sacrifice of Christ and of the Church. *Because, in the Sacrament of the Altar, as St Augustine tells us*,[^c07-84] *a sign is given to the Church that in the Sacrifice she offers, she herself is offered*.[^c07-85]

*The purposes of the sacrifice of the Mass*

Since the Sacrifice of the Mass is the same as the Sacrifice of Calvary, their purposes are also identical. So the Church sings in the liturgy: *As often as the commemoration of this Sacrifice is celebrated, the work of our Redemption is carried out*.[^c07-86]

The sacrifice of the Cross was, firstly, a sacrifice of adoration and praise of God. *In order that this hymn should never cease, the members are united, in the eucharistic sacrifice, to their Divine head; and with him, with the angels and archangels, they sing endless praises to God*,[^c07-87] *giving all honour and glory to the Almighty Father*.[^c07-88] Therefore, although the Holy Mass is sometimes offered *in honour and in memory of the saints, the Church teaches that Sacrifice is offered not to the saints, but to God alone who has given them their crown*.[^c07-89]

The second aim of the holy Sacrifice is that of thanksgiving, since *only the Divine Redeemer, because he is the most beloved Son of the Eternal Father and fully knows his unbounded Love, could offer him a worthy hymn of thanksgiving. This is what he intended; this is what he willed; when at the Last Supper he "gave thanks".*[^c07-90] *Hanging on the Cross, he continued to give thanks; he continues to do so in the august Sacrifice of the Altar*.[^c07-91]

The third purpose of the Mass is expiation and atonement for our sins, because in this sacrament *the same Christ "who offered himself once in a bloody manner of the Altar of the Cross is present and is offered in an unbloody manner"*[^c07-92] *so that, if we "draw near to God with an upright heart and true faith, with fear and reverence, with sorrow and repentance, through the Mass we may obtain mercy and find grace to help us in time of need."*[^c07-93] *For by this Oblation the Lord is appeased, he grants the Grace and the Gift of repentance, and he pardons wrongdoings and sins, even grave ones*.[^c07-94]

Moreover, the mercy of God has wanted that these fruits of expiation and atonement be offered *for the sins, punishments, satisfaction and the other needs of the faithful on earth, as well as for those who have died in Christ and are not yet wholly cleansed*.[^c07-95]

The fourth purpose of the Mass is that of petition. In going to the Sacrifice of the Altar, men may obtain the mediation of Jesus Christ who, on the Cross, *offering up prayers and supplication\... was heard because of his reverent submission*,[^c07-96] and who now in heaven *lives always to make intercession for us*.[^c07-97] These graces benefit both those who are present at the Holy Sacrifice and the person for whom it is applied.[^c07-98]

The Sacrifice of the Altar is an ever-present and living re-presentation of the Sacrifice of the Cross, from which it derives all its effectiveness. *The merits of this Sacrifice are utterly boundless; without limits, they extend to all men at all times and in all places. And the reason is because the Priest and Victim of it is God himself made man; because his immolation, like his obedience to the Will of his Eternal Father, was quite perfect; and because he went to meet death as the head of the human race*.[^c07-99]

***My child*** --- our Father invites us --- ***think now about the Holy Mass: about how we should celebrate it or attend it. Consider the fact that the angels are present. Remember that you are doing or taking part in something divine. See how on the altar Christ again offers himself for you and for me. And you will feel a great desire to imitate his humility, his self-abasement in the Host; and you will be filled with acts of thanksgiving, adoration, reparation, and petition. And you will offer yourself, with your arms outstretched, like another Christ,* ipse Christus, *ready to be nailed to the sweet wood, for the love of souls.***[^c07-100]
