## CHRIST IN THE CHURCH {#cuaderno.1.8}

When closing the Year of Faith in 1968, Pope Paul VI gave a solemn reminder that all the symbols of the Catholic faith profess that our Lord Jesus Christ, the only-begotten and eternal Son of the Father, was born, in time, of the Virgin Mary. *He dwelt among us, full of grace and truth. He proclaimed and established the Kingdom of God and made us know in himself the Father. He gave us his new commandment to love one another as he loved us. He taught us the way of the beatitudes of the Gospel: poverty in spirit, meekness, suffering borne with patience, thirst after justice, mercy, purity of heart, will for peace, persecution suffered for justice sake. Under Pontius Pilate he suffered --- the Lamb of God bearing on himself the sins of the world. And he died for us on the cross, saving us by his redeeming blood. He was buried, and, of his own power, rose on the third day, raising us by his resurrection to that sharing in the divine life which is the life of grace. He ascended to heaven, and he will come again, this time in glory, to judge the living and the dead: each according to his merits --- those who have responded to the love and piety of God going to eternal life, those who have refused them to the end going to the fire that is not extinguished. And his kingdom will have no end*.[^c08-1]

Throughout history, Christ remains present in his Church in a hidden way, visible only to the eyes of faith. This is what he promised his Apostles moments before ascending into heaven: *I am with you always, to the close of the age*.[^c08-2]

*Christ's presence in the Church*

Christ, glorified and triumphant, continues to assist the pilgrim Church. He makes himself present in many different ways, as a pledge of the complete and lasting union we hope for. *Christ is present in his Church when she prays, since it is he who "prays for us and prays in us and to whom we pray as to our God".*[^c08-3] *It is he who has promised: "Where two or three are gathered together in my name, I am there in the midst of them"*.[^c08-4] *He is present in the Church as she performs her works of mercy, not only because we do to Christ whatever good we do to one of his least brethren*,[^c08-5] *but also because it is Christ, performing these works through the Church, who continually assists men with his divine love. He is present in the Church on her pilgrimage of struggle to reach the harbour of eternal life, since it is he who through faith dwells in our hearts*,[^c08-6] *and, through the Holy Spirit whom he gives us, pours his love into those hearts*.[^c08-7] [^c08-8]

Christ is also present in the Church as she preaches the Word of God, *a stumbling block to Jews and folly to Gentiles*.[^c08-9] *When Sacred Scripture is read in the Church, it is he who speaks*.[^c08-10] *The Gospel which she proclaims is the Word of God, which is not preached except in the name of Christ, by the authority of Christ, and with the assistance of Christ, the Incarnate Word of God. In this way there is formed "one flock which trusts its only shepherd"*.[^c08-11] [^c08-12] When Paul was jailed and near martyrdom, he wrote Timothy that *the word of God is not fettered*.[^c08-13] He praised the Thessalonians' faith: *We also thank God constantly for this, that when you received the word of God which you heard from us, you accepted it not as the word of men but as what it really is, the word of God, which is at work in you believers*.[^c08-14]

Christ is also present *in his Church as she governs the People of God, since her sacred power comes from Christ, and since Christ, "The Shepherd of Shepherds"*,[^c08-15] is present in the pastors who exercise that power, according to his promise to the Apostles.

*Moreover, in a manner still more sublime, Christ is present in his Church as she offers in his name the Sacrifice of the Mass*.[^c08-16] He is present in the minister who celebrates Mass.[^c08-17] And Christ is present in the Church's liturgical cycle which commemorates the mysteries of his life. *Therefore the liturgical year, which is nourished by the Church's piety, is not a cold and distant replaying of past events, nor simply a memorial. Rather, it is Christ himself living on in his Church, continuing the way of great mercy he began in his mortal life when he went about doing good*.[^c08-18] Its admirable purpose is to help men come in contact with its mysteries and begin to live by them.[^c08-19]

Finally, our Lord *is also present by his power in the sacraments*.[^c08-20] *These are the actions of Christ, who administers them through men. Therefore, the sacraments are holy in themselves, and by the power of Christ they pour grace into the soul when they touch the body*.[^c08-21]

*Eucharistic presence*

Thus, our Lord is present in the faithful who are gathered together in his name, when the divine word is preached, in the liturgy and in the administration of the sacraments. *These different ways in which Christ is present are astonishing: they confront the Church with a mystery ever to be pondered. But there is yet another very distinct manner, truly sublime, in which Christ is present in his Church, it is the way in which he is present in the sacrament of the Eucharist, on account of which it is "a more consoling source of devotion, a more beautiful object of contemplation, a more effective means of sanctification than all the other sacraments"*.[^c08-22] *The reason is clear: it contains Christ himself and it is "a kind of perfection of the spiritual life; in a way, it is the goal of all the sacraments".*[^c08-23] *It is called the "real" presence, not in an exclusive sense, as though the other forms of presence were not "real", but by reason of its excellence. It is the substantial presence by which Christ is made present without doubt, whole and entire, God and man.*[^c08-24]

The truth of the real, bodily and substantial presence of Christ in the Eucharist was revealed by Christ himself at Capharnaum. After the great miracle of the multiplication of the loaves, he said: *I am the living bread which came down from heaven; if any one eats of this bread, he will live for ever; and the bread which I shall give for the life of the world is my flesh. The Jews then disputed among themselves, saying, How can this man give us his flesh to eat?*[^c08-25] They failed to understand our Lord's meaning. His clear words become incomprehensible for the person who does not allow himself to be penetrated by the light of Faith. *\`How can this be?', we seem to hear some of you murmur. \`How can something like this be true, which takes us far beyond our normal experience, our usual knowledge of the physical world, all possibility of sensible proof?' This typical modern mentality,* said Pope Paul VI, *at times deters modern man when faced with this message: Christ is with us. And they repeat the negative comments of those who listened to the eucharistic discourse at Capharnaum: "This is a hard saying; who can listen to it?"*[^c08-26] [^c08-27] This is the response of someone who does not want to accept Christ's words, whose supreme rule of truth is his own reason. *Some try to twist Christ's word, making simplistic comparisons of the Scripture passage with the mythical legends of ancient pagan mysteries. This is a vain show of erudition, serving only to obscure the Gospel's revelation.*

Others try to place limits to God's Word, claiming that it is only a ritual meal, a purely symbolic presence and not a real presence.[^c08-28] It is true that the Eucharist contains a symbolic meaning. St Paul wrote the Corinthians: *Because there is one bread, we who are many are one body, for we all partake of the one bread*.[^c08-29] *While the eucharistic symbolism brings us to an understanding of the effect proper to this sacrament, which is the unity of the mystical Body, it does not indicate or explain what it is that makes this Sacrament different from all others*.[^c08-30] Jesus' words are very clear: *My flesh is food indeed, and my blood is drink indeed. He who eats my flesh and drinks my blood abides in me, and I in him*.[^c08-31] They are a promise of eternal life, and *call for a response on our part; our intellect is not humiliated, rather it must be docile, vigilant and filled with love. For this faith is required*.[^c08-32] *The eucharist is "mysterium fidei", a mystery of faith. A light that is most bright, sweet and certain for the believer; a dark rite for the person without faith\... The one who accepts it, chooses. He chooses Peter's vigorous response: "Lord, to whom shall we go? You have the words of eternal life".*[^c08-33] [^c08-34]

Our Father has spoken to us of the ardent love for mankind that moved Christ. And it is love, along with faith, that enables us to fathom more deeply this mystery. ***I am reminded of the leave-taking of someone who has to go, but would like to stay. But duty, in one form or another, forces him to leave those he loves. So he leaves behind a photograph, with a dedication so ardent that it seems almost enough to burn the paper. He can do no more, because a creature**'**s power is not as great as its desire. What we cannot do, our Lord can. He goes and he stays behind, so that we can feed on him and become one with Him.***

Christ's promise became a reality at the Last Supper. *Now as they were eating, Jesus took bread, and blessed, and broke it, and gave it to the disciples and said, "Take, eat; this is my body." And he took a cup, and when he had given thanks he gave it to them, saying, "Drink of it, all of you; for this is my blood of the covenant, which is poured out for many for the forgiveness of sins"*.[^c08-35] Our Lord's words were so clear, the command he gave his disciples so forceful --- *Do this in remembrance of me*.[^c08-36] --- that the first Christians began to meet to celebrate the Lord's Supper after his Ascension into heaven. The *Acts* of the Apostles says that *they devoted themselves to the Apostles' teaching and to fellowship in the breaking of bread and to prayers*.[^c08-37] St Paul himself gives witness to the strong faith in the Real Presence of the early Christian community at Corinth: *The cup of blessing which we bless: Is* it not a participation in the Blood of Christ? The bread which we break: *Is* it not a participation in the Body of Christ?\... For as often as you eat this bread and drink the cup, you proclaim the Lord's death until he comes. Whoever, therefore, eats the bread or drinks the cup of the Lord in an unworthy manner will be guilty of profaning the Body and Blood of the Lord.[^c08-38]

*The constant teaching which the Catholic Church passes on to her catechumens,* Paul VI explained, *and the very words used by Christ when he instituted the most Holy Eucharist, compel us to acknowledge that "the Eucharist is the Flesh of our Saviour Jesus Christ who suffered for our sins and whom the Father in his loving- kindness raised again"*.[^c08-39] *To these words of St Ignatius of Antioch, we may add those which Theodore of Mopsuestia, a faithful witness to the faith of the Church on this point, addressed to the faithful: "The Lord did not say: This is a symbol of my Body, and this a symbol of my Blood, but, This is my Body and my Blood. He teaches us not to look to the nature of those things which lie before us and are perceived by the senses, for by the prayer of thanksgiving and the words spoken over them, they have been changed into Flesh and Blood"*.[^c08-40] [^c08-41]

For eleven centuries no one placed in doubt the Real Presence of Christ in the Eucharist. Berengarius, at the start of the second millennium, was the first to deny the conversion of the bread and wine into the Body and Blood of the Lord. This gave rise to a unanimous reaction in the Church, which forcefully rejected this position in thirteen councils. At the Council of Roman, in 1079, Pope St Gregory VII had him make the following Profession of Faith: *I, in my heart, believe and, with my lips, confess that through the mystery of the sacred prayer and the words of our Redeemer the bread and wine which are placed on the altar are substantially changed into the true and proper and living Flesh and Blood of Jesus Christ, our Lord, and that after consecration it is the true Body of Christ which was born of the Virgin and which, offered for the salvation of the world, was suspended on the Cross, and which sits at the right hand of the Father; and the true Blood of Christ, which was poured out from his side not only through the sign and power of the Sacrament, but also in the very reality and truth of their nature and substance*.[^c08-42]

In the following centuries, the Church developed, in an extraordinary way, the worship of latria to the Holy Eucharist, and the Magisterium clarified, even more fully, the nature of the mysterious change effected by the consecration of the bread and the wine. *Christ cannot be thus present in this Sacrament except by the change into his Body of the reality itself of the wine, leaving unchanged only the properties of the bread and wine which our senses perceive*.[^c08-43]

*Dogma of the Transubstantiation*

Many Fathers of the Church had taught that the Eucharistic conversion is total.[^c08-44] But only after the heresy fermented by Berengarius, did theology come to express the complete and supernatural change undergone by the matter of the Eucharist with a single, specific word --- "transubstantiation". Pope Innocent III was the first to use this term in explaining Catholic doctrine.[^c08-45] Soon afterwards, other Church documents began to employ it.[^c08-46] The Council of Trent set forth the true doctrine on the Eucharist on many points which had been rejected by Protestantism. After affirming once again the Real Presence of Jesus Christ in this Sacrament, it solemnly defined the way this is brought about: *If anyone says that in the sacred and holy Sacrament of the Eucharist there remains the substance of bread and wine together with the Body and Blood of our Lord Jesus Christ, and denies that wonderful and singular conversion of the whole substance of the bread into the Body, and of the entire substance of the wine into the Blood, only the species of the bread and wine remaining, a change which the Catholic Church most fittingly calls "transubstantiation": let him be anathema*.[^c08-47]

After Trent, the Church has reaffirmed on a number of occasions, the doctrine of transubstantiation, always teaching that it is an Article of Faith and should be preached as such.[^c08-48] *The Church, with the long labour of centuries, and not without the help of the Holy Spirit, has established a rule of language and confirmed it with the authority of the Councils. This rule, which has more than once been the watchword and banner of orthodox Faith, must be preserved religiously, and let no one presume to change it at his own pleasure or under the pretext of new knowledge. who would ever tolerate that the dogmatic formulas used by the ecumenical councils for the mysteries of the Holy Trinity and the Incarnation be judged as no longer appropriate for men of our times and therefore that others be rashly substituted for them? In the same way it cannot be tolerated that any individual should on his own authority modify the formulas which were used by the Council of Trent to express belief in the Eucharistic Mystery. For these formulas, like the others which the Church uses to propose the dogmas of Faith, express concepts which are not tied to a certain form of human culture, or to a specific phase of human culture, or to one or other theological school. No, these formulas present that part of reality, which necessary and universal experience permits the human mind to grasp, and manifest with apt and exact terms taken either from common or polished language. For this reason, these formulas are adapted to men of all times and all places*.[^c08-49]

These formulas can sometimes be *more clearly and accurately explained. In fact, the achievement of this goal is highly beneficial. But it would be wrong to give to these expression a meaning other than the original. Thus the understanding of the Faith should be advanced without threat to its unchangeable truth*.[^c08-50] Therefore it is *not allowable to exaggerate the element of sacramental sign as if the symbolism, which all certainly admit in the Eucharist, expresses and exhausts completely the mode of Christ's presence in this Sacrament. Nor is it allowable to discuss the mystery of transubstantiation without mentioning what the Council of Trent stated about the marvellous conversion of the whole substance of the bread into the Body and of the whole substance of the wine into the Blood of Christ, speaking rather only of what is called "transignification" and "transfiguration"*.[^c08-51]

It is true that, *as a result of transubstantiation, the species of bread and wine undoubtedly take on a new meaning and a new finality, for they no longer remain ordinary bread and ordinary wine, but become the sign of something sacred, the sign of a spiritual food. However, the reason they take on this new significance and this new finality is simply because they contain a new "reality" which we may justly term ontological. What lies under those species was not already there before, but something quite different; and that is so, not only because of the Faith of the Church, but in objective reality, since after the change of the substance or nature of the bread and the wine into the Body and Blood of Christ, nothing remains of the bread and the wine but the appearances alone*.[^c08-52] Therefore, *every theological explanation which seeks some understanding of this mystery must, in order to be in accord with Catholic Faith, maintain that in the reality itself, independently of our mind, the bread and wine have ceased to exist after the Consecration, so that it is the adorable Body and Blood of the Lord Jesus that, from then on, are really before us under the sacramental species of bread and wine, as the Lord willed it, in order to give himself to us as food and to associate us with the unity of his Mystical Body*.[^c08-53]

*Characteristics of the eucharistic presence*

The eucharist is *Christ himself, who suffered on the Cross and is now seated at the right hand of the Father*.[^c08-54] Our Father wrote: ***There he is: King of Kings and Lord of Lords, hidden in the Bread. To this extreme has he humbled himself through love for you****.*[^c08-55] Christ's sacred humanity is present only in heaven and in the Sacrament of the Altar. *These things are not mutually contradictory, that our Saviour himself is always seated at the right of the Father in heaven according to the natural mode of existing, and yet that in many other places sacramentally he is present to us in his own Substance by that manner of existence which, although we can scarcely express it in words, yet we can, however, by our understanding illuminated by Faith, conceive it to be possible to God, and which we ought most steadfastly to believe*.[^c08-56] Therefore, *the Body of Christ, after the words of the Consecration, is the same Body that was born of the Virgin and was immolated on the Cross*.[^c08-57] Although Christ is present under the eucharistic species in a different way --- in the manner of substance, in theological terms --- from the one in which he is present in heaven, *the unique and indivisible existence of the Lord glorious in heaven is not multiplied, but is rendered present by the Sacrament in the many places on earth where Holy Mass is celebrated*.[^c08-58] This has always been the Church's Faith, which affirms moreover the integral presence of Christ --- God and Man --- under each one of the sacramental species. After the Consecration *the Body (is present) under the species of bread, and the Blood under the species of wine, by the force of the words, but the Body itself under both by force of that natural connection and concomitance by which the parts of Christ the Lord, "Who has now risen from the dead to die no more"*,[^c08-59] *are mutually united, the divinity also, because of that admirable hypostatic union of his Body with his Soul. Therefore, it is very true that as much is contained under either species as under both*.[^c08-60] *For Christ, whole and entire, is present under the species of bread and under any part whatsoever of that species, likewise the whole (Christ) is present under the species of wine and under its parts*.[^c08-61] The Council reiterates that *the whole Christ is contained in the venerable sacrament of the Eucharist under each species and under every part of each species, when the separation has been made*.[^c08-62]

Transubstantiation is a miracle which only the Omnipotence of God can effect since, by it, the accidents (physical and chemical properties, quantity, atomic structure, etc.) of the bread and wine remain in the sacrament without a subject. These are maintained in being by God's special, direct intervention.[^c08-63] Since he is the Author of nature and the Creator of all things, God can suspend natural laws. The fact that the eucharistic species remain with all their properties means that any actions touching on the sacrament only affect the accidents, with no effect on the Body and Blood of Christ, impassible now that it has been glorified. This is what the Church sings in the *Corpus Christi* liturgy: *Not a single doubt remains, When they break the Host in twain, But that in each part remains, What was in the whole before; Since the simple sign alone, Suffers change in state or form, The signified remaining one, And the same for evermore*.[^c08-64]

*Eucharistic worship*

The continuing presence of the accidents, with the characteristic properties of bread and wine, is a sign that the Body and Blood of the Lord continue truly to be present under the sacramental veil. In opposition to the Protestant reformers, who admitted the presence of Christ exclusively in the use of the Sacrament, the Church has solemnly stated: *If anyone says that after the completion of the Consecration that the Body and Blood of our Lord Jesus Christ is present not in the marvellous Sacrament of the Eucharist, but only in use, while it is taken, not however before or after, and that in the Hosts or consecrated particles, which are reserved or remain after Communion, the true Body of the Lord does not remain: let him be anathema*.[^c08-65]

Our Father told us: ***My sons, when you contemplate the Sacred Host exposed in the monstrance on the altar, or hidden in the tabernacle, consider how great is the love, the tenderness of Christ. I can understand it because of my love for you: if I could be working far away, and at the same time next to each of you, how gladly I would do it. Well, Christ can! He loves us with a love infinitely greater than that found in all created hearts. He has remained so we may always unite ourselves to his Sacred Humanity, to help us, to console us, to strengthen us, so that we be faithful.*** The cult of worship and adoration that the Church has always rendered to the Eucharist is founded on this continued presence of Christ, as God and Man, in the Sacrament of the Altar, under the sacramental species.[^c08-66] *There can be no doubt,* *then, that* a*ll the faithful of Christ, in accordance with the perpetual custom of the Catholic Church, venerate this most Holy Sacrament with the worship of latria which is due to the true God. Nor is this Sacrament to be less adored because it was instituted by Christ the Lord to be received. For we really believe that same God to be there present, of whom the Eternal Father, when introducing him into the world said: "Let all the angels of God adore him"*,[^c08-67] *whom the Magi, "falling down, adored him"*,[^c08-68] *who, finally, as Scripture testifies, was adored by the Apostles in Galilee*.[^c08-69] [^c08-70]

The eucharist is the most precious jewel of the Mystical Body of Christ, its strength and its life, the centre of *the universal Church and of all humanity, since beneath the appearance of the species, Christ is contained, the invisible head of the Church, the Redeemer of the World, the centre of all hearts, "for whom all things exist and we in him"*.[^c08-71] *As long as the Eucharist is kept in our churches and oratories, Christ is truly the Emmanuel, that is, "God with us". Day and night he is in our midst, he dwells with us, full of grace and truth*.[^c08-72] *He restores morality, nourishes virtues, consoles the afflicted, strengthens the weak. He proposes his own example to those who come to him so that all may learn to be, like himself, meek and humble of heart and to seek not their own interests but those of God. Anyone who approaches this august Sacrament with special devotion and generously endeavours to return love for Christ's own infinite love, will experience and fully understand --- not without spiritual joy and fruit --- how precious is the life hidden with Christ in God*,[^c08-73] *and how great is the value of conversing with Christ, for there is nothing more consoling on earth, nothing more efficacious for advancing along the road of holiness*.[^c08-74]

Our Lord is always there waiting for us in the Tabernacle. ***When someone on this earth is important, when he has a bit of authority and position, whether he likes it or not he has to make those who want to see him wait, at least for a few moments. And what are all those who govern on earth, what are all the glories of earth, compared to God**'**s Majesty? God does not make us wait. It is he, in his infinite goodness, who waits for us.*** So, ***when you approach the tabernacle remember that he has been waiting for you for twenty centuries.***[^c08-75]

*Effects of the Eucharist*

The Holy Eucharist is the seed of eternal life: *He who eats my Flesh and drinks my Blood* --- Jesus promised in Capharnaum --- *has eternal life, and I will raise him up at the last day*.[^c08-76] *The Eucharist is medicine of immortality, antidote against death and food for living forever in Christ*.[^c08-77] And while the history of salvation is being played out and all creatures await anxiously the glorious coming of their Lord at the end of time, *it is the pledge of that hope and food for the way*.[^c08-78] In addition, the Eucharist presages, in an invisible but very efficacious way, the longed for unity of the faith that God so desires. The Council of Trent, at the end of its Decree on the Eucharist, said: *Finally, this holy Synod with paternal affection admonishes, exhorts, entreats, and beseeches, "through the tender Mercy of our God"*[^c08-79] *that each and all, who are classed under the name of Christian, will now finally agree and be of the same opinion in this Sign of Unity, in this Bond of Charity, in this symbol of concord, and that, mindful of so great a majesty and such boundless love of our Lord Jesus Christ, who gave his own precious Life as the price of our salvation, and gave us "his own Flesh to eat"*,[^c08-80] *they may believe and venerate these Sacred Mysteries of his Body and Blood with that constancy and firmness of faith, with that devotion of soul, that piety and worship, as to be able to receive frequently that "daily Bread"*,[^c08-81] *and that it may be to them truly the life of the soul and the perpetual health of mind, "so that being invigorated by the strength thereof"*,[^c08-82] *after the journey of this miserable pilgrimage, they may be able to arrive in their heavenly country to eat without any veil that "same Bread of angels"*,[^c08-83] *which they now eat under the sacred veils*.[^c08-84] This is what the liturgy of the Church asks for when adoring the Blessed Sacrament: *Jesus, whom now I see hidden, I ask you to fulfil what I so desire: That on seeing you face to face, I may be happy in seeing your glory.*[^c08-85]

*We know neither the moment of the consummation of the earth and of man,*[^c08-86] *nor the way the universe will be transformed. The form of this world, distorted by sin, is passing away*[^c08-87] *and we are taught that God is preparing a new dwelling and a new earth in which righteousness dwells*,[^c08-88] *the happiness of which will fill and surpass all the desires of peace arising in the hearts of men*.[^c08-89] *Then, with death conquered, the sons of God will be raised in Christ and what was sown in weakness and dishonour will put on imperishability*;[^c08-90] *charity and its works will remain*,[^c08-91] *and all of creation*,[^c08-92] *which God made for man, will be set free from its bondage to decay*.[^c08-93] Our Lord Jesus Christ will be present then in the community of his elect, not in mystery but in his true Being: *He will dwell with them, and they shall be his people, and God himself will be with them*.[^c08-94]

Until that time, the Holy Eucharist will be ***the most sacred and transcendant act which we, men and women, with God**'**s grace, can carry out in this life: receiving the Body and Blood of our Lord is, in a certain sense, like loosening our ties with earth and time, so as to be already with God in heaven, where Christ himself will wipe the tears from our eyes and where there will be no more death, nor mourning, nor cries of distress, because the old world will have passed away (cf. Rev 21:4).***[^c08-95]

## PERSONAL SIN {#cuaderno.1.9}

*God shows us his Love, first of all, in our vocation to the Faith. His Word is the expression of his Charity\... Faith in God is a summons of Love which demands of us an initial and fundamental response of love. Faith is our treasure, our happiness, the key to our destiny*.[^c09-1] Faith --- a gift from God --- teaches us that we have been created in the image and likeness of the Creator and have been called to an intimate union in his Love. The Catholic Church, the guardian of Revelation, enlightens our understanding with the truths contained in the deposit of Sacred Scripture and Tradition: the origin of the world and of man, original sin, the mystery of the Word Incarnate, the redemption wrought by Jesus Christ.

***My daughters and sons,*** the Father has written, ***have faith, a sturdy faith, a living faith, a faith that works through love.***[^c09-2] And Pope Paul VI, a few days prior to the Feast of SS. Peter and Paul, encouraged all Christians to *renew in a tangible and active fashion the message of the Salvation brought to the world by Christ*.[^c09-3] It is the fundamental duty of every member of the Church *to go to the root of our religious life, to its interior and original principle, which is to say, to the faith, in order to begin to make this life stronger with the knowledge of its component parts, with the appreciation of its divine origin, with the understanding of its interior workings, with the harmony of its exterior profession, and with the joy of possessing it personally and of affirming it in society. Wherefore, having completed the nineteenth centenary of the martyrdom of the Holy Apostles, Peter and Paul\... We have proposed to the Church the celebration of a "Year of Faith", as a post-conciliar consequence and necessity*.[^c09-4]

Putting these desires of the Roman Pontiff into practice, we will continue to consider, in the light of the infallible Magisterium of the Church, some of the basic truths of the Catholic faith. They are basic ideas of the Christian catechism, indispensable for carrying out a fruitful apostolate of doctrine.

*The reality of personal sins*

The Incarnation of Jesus Christ, his life, death and resurrection reconciled mankind, which had sinned in Adam, with its Creator. Man once again became a son of God, *and if children then heirs, heirs of God and co-heirs with Christ*.[^c09-5] This is a promise of eternal happiness which will only be completely fulfilled in heaven. Until then, the Christian is a wayfarer on earth and --- moved in an indescribable way by divine grace --- is free in what he does,[^c09-6] so that he is always open to the possibility of being a saint or a sinner; he can open his arms to divine Love or close them in a futile gesture of independence. Daily he stands at the crossroads: *The path of righteousness is life, but the way of error leads to death*.[^c09-7]

In this perspective of human life, the reality of personal sins stands out clearly. No one is free from them. *If we say we have no sin* --- St John writes --- *we deceive ourselves, and the truth is not in us*.[^c09-8] The Father reminds us that ***we cannot forget that we have within us a source of opposition and resistance to grace. We carry the wounds of original sin, inflamed perhaps by our personal sins.***[^c09-9] The Church states that, in spite of baptism, there remains in the soul that concupiscence, the *fomes peccati,* which *is from sin and inclines to sin*.[^c09-10] This tendency is not in itself sin, but *it has been left to provide a trial; it has no power to injure those who do not consent, those who, by the grace of Christ Jesus, manfully resist.*[^c09-11] But certainly, it makes the way to God difficult for souls. ***My child, your desires for sanctity will be opposed first by laziness, the first front on which you must fight. Then rebelliousness, the refusal to shoulder Christ**'**s sweet yoke, a mad longing, not for holy freedom but for licence. And sensuality, and, at all times (and more surreptitiously as the years go by), pride. Finally there is a whole string of evil inclinations, because our weaknesses never come alone.***[^c09-12]

*What divine Revelation makes known to us*, the Second Vatican Council states, *conforms with experience. When man examines his heart, he finds that he has inclinations towards evil, and is engulfed by many ills which cannot be attributed his good Creator. This is what explains the intimate division within man. As a result, all human life, whether individual or collective, shows itself to be a dramatic struggle between good and evil, between light and darkness. Indeed, man finds that by himself he is incapable of battling the assaults of evil successfully, to the point that everyone feels as though he is bound by chains*.[^c09-13]

*Mortal and venial sin and their punishment*

The experience of sin is obvious. The world, under the power of the devil,[^c09-14] and concupiscence are the immediate sources of sin, to which man can then consent, knowingly and willingly.[^c09-15] The teaching of the Church states that there are sins which are mortal by nature and which cause spiritual death and the loss of supernatural life. The others are called venial; that is, they are not radically opposed to the Creator, although they impede the practice of the infused virtues[^c09-16] and prepare the way for falling into grave sins.

Mortal sin is the greatest misfortune that can happen to a soul redeemed by Christ Jesus. ***Do not forget, my son, that for you on earth there is but one evil, which you must fear and avoid with the grace of God: sin.***[^c09-17] So serious is this evil that *all mortal sins, even those of thought, make men "children of wrath"*[^c09-18] and *enemies of God*.[^c09-19] Through them the grace of justification is lost, although faith is not lost.[^c09-20] Man becomes subject to the power of the devil,[^c09-21] and he becomes liable to eternal damnation.[^c09-22] Many pages of Scripture attest to the evil of sin, a most grave offence to the divine majesty. *Be appalled, O heavens, at this; be shocked, be utterly desolate, says Yahveh, for my people have committed two evils: they have forsaken Me, the fountain of living waters, and hewn out cisterns for themselves, broken cisterns, that can hold no water*.[^c09-23] The evil of sin consists in this: that *although men knew God, they did not honour him as God or give thanks to him, but became ineffectual in their thinking and their senseless minds were darkened\....and they worshipped and served the creature rather than the Creator*.[^c09-24] *And by refusing frequently to acknowledge God as his beginning, man has shattered his due submission to his own ultimate goal, as well as all his harmony, in as much as it affects himself and his relationships with others and with the rest of creation*.[^c09-25] Every sin, besides offending God, *causes a disturbance of the universal order established by God in his ineffable wisdom and infinite charity*,[^c09-26] a disorder that must be remedied with an adequate penalty.

Besides, sin is always *contempt for, or disregard of, the personal friendship between God and man, a real and unjustifiable offence against God, even more, an ungrateful rejection of the Love of God offered to us through Jesus Christ who called his disciples friends and not servants*.[^c09-27] [^c09-28] The corresponding penalty --- a penalty of loss --- is infinite: eternal separation from the Creator, loss of the beatific vision. But in addition, the sinner offers to a creature the love which belongs to God alone, and therefore, he merits a penalty of the senses, a mysterious and limited punishment which afflicts his body as well as the soul.

If a man dies in this state, his will remains eternally in rebellion against God and the providential order of the universe. His guilt is eternal and, therefore, so is his penalty. A man who dies in mortal sin loses forever the peace of God and the peace of the universe. This is the meaning of the dogma on the existence and nature of hell, which the Church has reaffirmed on numerous occasions: *the souls of those who die in actual mortal sin go down into hell immediately after their death and there suffer the pains of hell*.[^c09-29] There they suffer, under the power of Satan, the eternal penalty merited by their sins.

So that we will not forget the reality of its existence, at a time when few want to hear of suffering and punishment, our Father tells us, ***There is a hell. Not a very original statement, you think. I will repeat it to you, then: there is a hell! Echo it for me, at the right moment, in the ear of one friend, and of another, and another.***[^c09-30]

*Pardon for sins*

*No one by his own efforts can free himself of sin. No one is completely free from its weakness, loneliness and power; but rather, everyone needs Christ, our Model, Teacher, Liberator, Saviour and Vivifier*.[^c09-31] That is why it is absolutely necessary for us ***to abandon ourselves into God**'**s hands, God who is most wise, who is Wisdom itself, who is Almighty, who is, above all, our Father. In his hands we have the help we need to live a Christian life, because, through Baptism, he has made us his children, and in Confirmation he gives us a warrior**'**s strength. Then, since he knows our nature and weakness, he feeds us with his Body and Blood in the Eucharist. And since he is so good, he pardons our sins in the Sacrament of Penance, giving us the strength we need to return again to the fight, so as not to sin. And when the moment of death arrives, at the very portal of eternity, he strengthens us with the Anointing of the Sick.***

*As I live, says the Lord God, I have pleasure, not in the death of the wicked, but in that the wicked turns from his way and lives; turn back, turn back from your evil ways; why are you set on dying?*[^c09-32] God is always attentive to men, seeking again and again the salvation of their souls. He shows his infinite mercy especially in the sacrament of Penance where he offers the sinner the possibility of passing yet again *out of darkness into his marvellous light*.[^c09-33] If men were to preserve the justification received in their baptism, *there would have been no need to institute any other sacrament than Baptism for the remission of sins. But "God is rich in mercy"*[^c09-34]*^\ ^*and "*he knows our frame, he remembers that we are dust"*,[^c09-35] and therefore *he has also prepared a remedy of life for those who, after baptism, have given themselves over to the slavery of sin and to the power of the devil. This remedy is the sacrament of Penance and through it the benefits of the death of Christ are applied to those who have fallen after Baptism.*[^c09-36] *Jesus Christ himself, before ascending into heaven, instituted it when he said to his Apostles: Receive the Holy Spirit, if you forgive the sins of any, they are forgiven; if you retain the sins of any, they are retained*.[^c09-37]

The Second Vatican Council teaches that those who approach the sacrament of Penance obtain pardon through the mercy of God for the offences committed against him and are at the same time reconciled with the Church which they have wounded by their sins.[^c09-38] The love of God for his creatures is so great that ***just as a mother forgets about her son**'**s previous thoughtlessness when he greets her affectionately, so too does Jesus overlook the things we have done wrong when we finally go to him affectionately, repentant, purified by the sacrament of Penance.***[^c09-39]

The Magisterium has specified clearly the necessary conditions for having one's sins forgiven: *The elements of this sacrament are the acts of the penitent, namely, contrition, confession and satisfaction*. *Inasmuch as these acts are demanded of the penitent according to God's arrangement for the integrity of the sacrament and for the complete and perfect remission of sins, they are called "the parts of Penance"*.[^c09-40] First of all, there is contrition, which is *the deep sorrow and detestation for the sin committed, with a resolution of sinning no more*.[^c09-41] Scripture mentions its necessity many times: *wash yourselves; make yourselves clean; remove the evil of your doings from before my sight, cease to do evil\... Though your sins are like scarlet, they shall become white as snow; though they are red like crimson, they shall become as wool*.[^c09-42] If the contrition is perfect, *it reconciles man to God before the sacrament is actually received*;[^c09-43] nevertheless, the real desire of receiving the sacrament is always required. The Council of Trent teaches that *the reconciliation must not be attributed to contrition exclusively of the desire for the Sacrament, which is included in the contrition*.[^c09-44]

Imperfect contrition, on the other hand, is that which *is conceived either from a consideration of the malice of sin or from the fear of hell and its punishments. If it excludes the will to sin, and hopes for pardon\..., even though without the sacrament of Penance it cannot of itself lead the sinner to justification, it nevertheless disposes him for the attainment of God's grace in the sacrament of Penance*.[^c09-45]

The second act required of the penitent is the oral confession of his mortal sins, made in secret to a legitimate minister, at least once a year when he has fallen into mortal sin,[^c09-46] or is in danger of death. *Penitents must recount in Confession all their mortal sins\... even if they be most hidden sins\... As regards venial sins, which do not exclude us from God's grace and into which we fall rather frequently, it is proper and advantageous to mention them in Confession. Nevertheless, they can be left unsaid without any sin, and they can be atoned for in many other ways\... Moreover, it follows that those circumstances which change the species of the sin must also be mentioned in Confession. For if these circumstances be omitted, the penitents cannot confess their sins entirely, the judges do not know the sin, and it is impossible for them to judge properly the gravity of the sins and impose on the penitents a suitable penance*.[^c09-47]

***What depths of mercy there are in the God**'**s justice! For, in the judgements of men, he who confesses his fault is punished; and in the judgement of God, he is pardoned.***[^c09-48] Through contrition and confession of our sins, God erases the guilt from the soul and pardons the eternal penalty of loss which the sins have deserved. Grace returns to clothe the soul, and with charity *poured into our hearts through the Holy Spirit*,[^c09-49] the sinner once again enjoys God's friendship. But the penalty of the senses still remains; the original order, broken by sin, must be restored. This satisfaction is, in part, made with the acceptance of the penance imposed by the priest in Confession. The rest of the penalty must be expiated *either on this earth, through the sorrows, miseries and calamities of this life and above all through death*,[^c09-50] *or else in the life beyond, through the purifying punishments of fire and torment*.[^c09-51] [^c09-52]

Most important of all is love: the more one loves God, the more one restores the disorder. This is why we have ***to keep the sense of sin and of generous reparation alive in the face of the false optimism of those*** **enemies of the cross of Christ** ***(Phil 3:18), who judge everything in terms of progress and human endeavour. These people commit the serious sin of forgetting sin, which some think they have done away with already.***[^c09-53] Charity, as it grows more demanding, will lead us to receive, with a spirit of penance, the sufferings of life and death, and also to look for voluntary mortification which, in union with the sufferings of Jesus Christ on the Cross, make satisfaction for our sins. *Such is the mystery of man, and it is a great one, as seen by believers in the light of Christian Revelation. Through Christ and in Christ, the riddles of sorrow and death grow meaningful. Apart from his Gospel, they overwhelm us*.[^c09-54] With sacrifices which he has sought out personally, the Christian fights the three-fold concupiscence that his sins have constantly stirred up: *concupiscence of the eyes, concupiscence of the flesh, the pride of life*.[^c09-55] Alms,[^c09-56] fasting,[^c09-57] and prayer are especially directed to this end. And they are also means to obtain forgiveness for venial sins.[^c09-58]

*The communion of saints*

Man is not alone in the task of expiating his sins. *There reigns among men, through the hidden and benign mystery of the divine Will, a supernatural solidarity whereby the sin of one harms the others, just as the holiness of one also benefits the others. Thus the Christian faithful give each other mutual aid to attain their supernatural aim\... Of this supernatural solidarity the greatest and the most perfect principle, foundation and example is Christ himself\... Who, though he "committed no sin, suffered for us, was wounded for our iniquities, bruised for our sins\... and by his bruises we are healed"*.[^c09-59] Following in the footsteps of Christ,[^c09-60] the Christian faithful have always endeavoured to help one another on the path leading to their heavenly Father through prayer, the exchange of spiritual goods and penitential expiation\... This is the very ancient dogma of the communion of saints, whereby the life of each individual son of God is joined, in Christ and through Christ, with a wonderful link to the life of all his other Christian brothers in the supernatural unity of the Mystical Body of Christ till, as it were, a single mystical person is formed.[^c09-61] [^c09-62]

We have been called to this mission of co-redemption: ***This must be our task in the apostolate, a task which, with our own spirituality and our special asceticism, fits beautifully into the one and only mission of Christ and his Church. The Lord calls us to imitate him like well-beloved children of his* *---* **estote ergo imitatores Dei, sicut filii carissimi *(Eph 5:1), imitate God like his beloved children* *---* *cooperating humbly and with all your energies in the divine plan to join together again what is broken, to rescue what is lost, to put straight what man has left in disorder, to bring to its true goal what has gone off course: to restore harmony to all creation.***[^c09-63]

Through his own satisfaction and that of others, man prepares himself in this life to be received after death into heavenly glory. *Nothing unclean shall enter it, nor anyone who practises abomination or falsehood, but only those who are written in the book of life*.[^c09-64] If at death a man has not completely satisfied the penalty for sin, he must be purified in some way before he becomes capable of enjoying God. This is the meaning of the doctrine on the existence of purgatory, the destiny of *the souls of those who die in Grace but who have not yet made satisfaction for their sins by a complete penance*.[^c09-65]

***Purgatory is not a punishment but a necessity for the soul in sin. It is a proof of God**'**s love**,* *which I like to compare,*** the Father says, ***to the love of a mother who picks up her child**,* *puts it in the bath, lathers and perfumes it, and dresses it up until finally the creature looks heavenly!*** In this place of puri­fication, joy and sorrow are mysteriously mixed. There is sorrow because the soul wants to enjoy God, but is temporarily deprived of seeing Him. There is suffering, because the soul sees it is being *punished by fire for a time*.^6^[^c09-66]^6^ This is the punishment of sense, *a fire more painful than any torment in this world*.^6^[^c09-67]^7^ But on the other hand, there is joy because the soul is sure of its call to enjoy God. In fact, the souls of the just after they are puri­fied, *even before the Day of Judgement, attain true and eternal happiness which consists in the love of God and the vision of Him face‑to‑face.* ^6^[^c09-68]^8^ Purgatory is not a limited hell. It is the vestibule of heaven. After this purifying suffering *those who remain in Zion, the survivors of Jerusalem, will be called saints.* ^6^[^c09-69]^9^

*The Doctrine on Indulgences*

*Return, O faithless children, says the Lord, for I am your master: I will take you, one from a city and two from a family and I will bring you to Zion...*^7^[^c09-70]^0^ God's unlimited love for men has found yet another means to help them reach heaven as soon as possible. This is the way of indulgences.

Christ\'s death on the cross, according to the teaching of Tradition and the Magisterium, earned an infinite treasure of graces for the Church. *Christ has committed this treasure to the care of St. Peter, who holds the keys of heaven, and to his successors, His own vicars on earth, who are to distribute it for the good of the faithful. And they are to apply it with compassion for a proper and reasonable cause, for the benefit of those who are truly sorry and who have confessed, for the complete or partial remission of the temporal punishment due to sin.*^7^[^c09-71]^1^ This treasure is growing continuously because of *the prayers and good works of the Blessed Virgin Mary and all the saints, who, following in the footsteps of Christ the Lord, and through his grace, have sanctified their lives and fulfilled the mission entrusted to them by the Father. Thus while attaining their own salvation, they have also cooperated in the salvation of their brothers in the unity of the Mystical Body.*^7^[^c09-72]^2^

The Communion of the Saints, the bond of charity between the faithful who are rejoicing already with God, those who suffer in Purgatory, and those who are still wayfarers on earth, makes possible this mutual help which the Church is responsible for distributing.^7^[^c09-73]^3^ *In an indulgence, the Church, making use of its power as minister of the Redemption of Christ, not only prays, but also, by an authoritative intervention, dispenses to the suitably disposed faithful the treasury of satisfaction which Christ and the saints won for the remission of temporal punishment. The aim pursued by ecclesiastical authority in granting indulgences is not only that of helping the faithful to expiate the punishment due to sin, but also that of urging them to perform works of piety, penance and charity.* ^7^[^c09-74]^4^ *The indulgence, therefore, is not an easier way of substituting the penance required of sinners, but rather the support which all the faithful, completely and humbly aware of their own weakness, find in the Mystical Body of Christ, the whole of which works for their conversion with charity, example and prayer*.^7^[^c09-75]^5^ ^7^[^c09-76]^6^

The doctrine of indulgences, developed by the Church throughout the years, is based on those words of Jesus Christ to St Peter: *Whatever you bind on earth will be bound in heaven and whatever you loose on earth, will be loosed in heaven*.^7^[^c09-77]^7^ Through this supreme power of jurisdiction, Peter and his successors enjoy Christ's own power: *I have set you this day over nations and over kingdoms; to root up and to break down, to destroy and to overthrow, to build and to plant.*^7^[^c09-78]^8^

Many advantages derive from indulgences, if used with the right dispositions. In the first place, the faithful understand that *by their own powers they could not remedy the harm they have done to themselves and to the entire community by their sin, and they are therefore stirred to a salutary humility. Furthermore the use of indulgences shows us how closely we are united to each other in Christ, and how the supernatural life of each one can benefit others. Likewise, the religious practice of indulgences reawakens trust and hope in a full reconciliation with God the Father\.... In addition, it should not be forgotten that by acquiring indulgences the faith­ful submit with docility to the legitimate pastors of the Church and above all to the successor of Blessed Peter, the bearer of the keys of heaven, to whom the Saviour Himself entrusted the task of feeding His flock and govern­ing His Church*.^7^[^c09-79]^9^

Indulgences can be gained for oneself or for the deceased by means of suffrage.^8^[^c09-80]^0^ But *although indulgences are in fact free gifts, nevertheless they are granted for the living as well as for the dead only on determined conditions. To acquire them, it is required on the one hand that prescribed works be performed, and on the other that the faithful have the necessary dispositions, that is to say, that they love God, detest sin, place their trust in the merits of Christ and believe firmly in the great assistance they derive from the Communion of the Saints.*^8^[^c09-81]^1^

The discipline governing indulgences can be modified by the Church, using the power of the keys, to accord with historical circumstances. There now exist two types of indulgence: plenary, which pardons all temporal punishment due to sin, and partial, which only frees in part. *The faithful who*, *with a contrite heart, perform an action to which a partial indulgence is attached obtain, in addition to the remission of temporal punishment acquired by the action itself, an equal remission of punishment through the inter­vention of the Church. To acquire a plenary indulgence it is necessary to perform the work to which the indulgence is attached and to fulfil three conditions: Sacramental Confession, Eucharistic Communion, and prayer for the intentions of the Supreme Pontiff. It is further required that all attach­ment to sin, even to venial sin, be absent.* ^8^[^c09-82]^2^

*I will sing of thy mercies, O Lord, forever*,^8^[^c09-83]^3^ Scripture says. And the Church, the dispenser of those mercies, encourages us along the ways of sanctity and union with God. *Let us bear together the difficulties of our trip, on the seven seas of this world, with the hope of heaven. For us and for all souls who want to live, the aspiration is to reach God: the glory of heaven.*^8^[^c09-84]^4^ We are sure of victory because Jesus Christ fights at our side. Before He comes to *judge the living and the dead,*^8^[^c09-85]^5^ and the world ends,^8^[^c09-86]^6^ Scripture forewarns us of the appearance of *the man of lawlessness, the son of perdition, who opposes and exalts himself against every so‑called god or object of worship, so that he takes his seat in the temple of God, proclaiming himself to be God. And then the lawless one will be revealed, and the Lord Jesus will slay him with the breath of His mouth and destroy him by His appearing and His coming. The lawless one will come by the power of Satan, with pretended signs and wonders*.^8^[^c09-87]^7^ Until then, the number of the elect will grow day by day, *the shape of the world, de­formed by sin,*^8^[^c09-88]^8^ *will pass away; but we are taught that God is preparing a new dwelling place and a new earth where justice will abide,*^8^[^c09-89]^9^ *and whose blessedness will answer and surpass all the longings for peace which spring up in the human heart.*^9^[^c09-90]^0^ *Then, with death overcome, the sons of God will raise up Christ. and what was sown in weakness and corruption will be clothed with incorruptibility.^9^*[^c09-91]*^1^ Charity and its fruits will endure,*^9^[^c09-92]^2^ *and all creation which God made on man\'s account will be unchained from the bondage of vanity*.^9^[^c09-93]^3^ ^9^[^c09-94]^4^ The mercies of Our Lord will then have been completed, and an everlasting happiness will reunite all the sons of God.

The Father writes: ***Considering these things in the presence of God, I would wish your hearts to be filled with thanksgiving, and simultaneously, with apostolic zeal, with desires to bring all men the news of the charity of Christ**.* *Do not forget that our great mission is to give doctrine. This con**­**stitutes the great apostolate of Opus Dei***: ***to show th**e* *multitude that awaits us which is the path that leads straight to God. Therefore, my children**,* *you must realise that you are called to the divine task of proclaiming the mer**­**cies of Our Lord:*** **Misericordias Domini** **in aeternum cantabo *(Ps 83:2), I will sing of the mercies of the Lord forever***.^9^[^c09-95]^5^
