## THE SACRAMENT OF PENANCE (I) {#cuaderno.1.10}

Our Lord has called us to the Work to contribute in a positive manner in helping many souls to know Jesus Christ, to love his doctrine and his word, to form part of the only Church he founded on earth, and so attain their eternal salvation.

To try to act as good children of God involves, among other things, our working to sow doctrine by making use of our work, our family and social relationships, our friendships, in fact, our whole life. And this we must do untiringly, always drawing our strength from a deep love for God.

Our Father asks us to go over again the catechism which you must certainly have learned as little children; and explain it to others. We cannot call ourselves Christians if we do not act, if we do not spread the fire we have in our hearts to others. We have an obligation to do so, whether we feel like it or not. In these times when love is so lacking, you must have clear ideas and give people good doctrine.

This perennial necessity of giving doctrine is more urgent than ever now that the articles of the Catholic faith are being undermined through a persistent campaign. In certain sectors, truths of the Faith are each day more and more misrepresented, either through ignorance or distortion of the true doctrine. And among other vital points of Catholic dogma, this is true in the case of the sacraments.

The Gospel tells us that a large multitude was following Christ along the roads of Palestine and that all the crowd sought to touch him, for power came forth from him and healed them all.[^c10-1] The Son of God was always ready to show compassion for everyone who came to him with faith; for as long as He remained among men, his most sacred Humanity was like a canal through which all grace flowed. Furthermore, knowing that we would always be in need of this supernatural help, he provided all the means whereby the infinite merits of his redeeming Passion in all their fullness could be obtained and applied to us at any time or place. St Augustine, bearing witness to the Church’s faith, teaches that when the time of grace had come, the wisdom of God Incarnate, through whom we were called to freedom, established certain saving sacraments which would keep the community of the Christian people united.[^c10-2]

By Christ’s Will, the sacraments are symbols of something sacred and the visible form of invisible grace.[^c10-3] They are sensible realities, as is Christ’s humanity. But they signify something supernatural, namely, grace; and at the same time, they communicate the grace they signify. In many cases they consist of material realities, such as water, oil, bread and wine, incapable in themselves of producing any spiritual, much less supernatural, effect. However, God, the Creator of all things, by his sovereign will chose to attach certain supernatural effects to them. So they are not something that merely awakens our faith: they have a force of their own, “ex opere operato”, as the Church calls it, independent of the merits of the person administering them. The sacraments are actions of Christ who administers them through me. By the power of Christ they pour Grace into the soul as they touch the body.[^c10-4] By means of the sacraments God’s action reaches all men: the salvation sought by those who drew near to Jesus and wanted to touch his most sacred humanity. As Tertullian wrote in the second century: The flesh is washed so that the soul might be cleansed. The flesh is anointed so that the soul might be consecrated. The sign of the Cross is made on the flesh so that the soul might be strengthened. The flesh is covered in shadow by the imposition of the hands so that the soul might be illuminated by the Spirit. The flesh is nourished by the Body and Blood of Christ so that the soul might be nourished by God.[^c10-5]

All Christians must spread the Church’s doctrine on the sacraments, explaining their true nature, their effectiveness and their supernatural origin. We all have the right and the duty to teach Christ’s doctrine to all men exactly as the Church presents it to us: a holy and sanctifying doctrine that we should love, defend, practise and spread.

Need for the sacraments

The New Law has seven sacraments: Baptism, Confirmation, the Eucharist, Penance, Extreme Unction, Holy Orders and Matrimony. These differ greatly from the sacraments of the Old Law, which did not cause grace but were only a figure of the grace Christ’s Passion would bestow. Our sacraments both contain grace and confer it on those who receive them worthily.

The first five sacraments are ordered to the interior spiritual perfection of the person; the last two are ordered to the government and growth of the whole Church. For by Baptism we are spiritually reborn and by Confirmation we grow in Grace and are strengthened in the Faith. Being reborn and strengthened, we are nourished with the divine food of the Eucharist. If by sinning we become sick in soul, Penance heals us spiritually. Extreme Unction heals us in spirit and in body as well, insofar as it is good for the soul. By Holy Orders the Church is governed and given spiritual growth; by Matrimony she is given bodily growth.[^c10-6]

To be holy and lead an authentically Christian life, it is absolutely necessary to receive the sacraments frequently. There is no other viable way for anyone who aspires to live the life of Christ. As our Father teaches us: There has never been any new invention, nor will there ever be. The means have been, are, and will always be the same: the sacraments, prayer, mortification, life of piety... The Second Vatican Council reminds us that the mission of the Church, therefore, is fulfilled by that activity which makes her fully present to all men and nations. She undertakes this activity in obedience to Christ’s command and in response to the grace and love of the Holy Spirit. Thus, by the example of her life and by her preaching, by the sacraments and other means of grace, she can lead them to the faith, the freedom and the peace of Christ.[^c10-7]

Among the means of salvation, the sacrament of Penance occupies a particularly important place. In freeing man from the slavery of sin, it reconciles him with God and enables him, as often as necessary, to find again the way to sanctity. It is a basic weapon for conquering in the ascetical struggle, for maintaining the fervour of one’s dedication, and for avoiding obstacles that impede intimate union with our Lord. Our apostolic zeal should lead us to teach many people the relevant doctrine and the way to make use of the sacrament of Penance. Our Father tells us: Get hold of those friends of yours who perhaps are not going very well, because they do not know, they have not met our Lord. Through your personal friendship, place the truth in their hearts. Lead them to make a good confession: a good resolution, and to go forward! They can rest assured that the priest will not be scandalised by anything, because we priests are capable of the same evil as others.

Institution of the sacrament of Penance

Penance is a sacrament of the New Law, and thus did not exist before the birth of Christ.[^c10-8] Only Jesus Christ, true God and true man, could forgive sins, and he did so many times in the course of his life. Moreover he promised to give this same power to his apostles, according to the words: Truly, I say to you, whatever you bind on earth shall be bound in heaven, and whatever you loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven.[^c10-9] The Church has always understood these words as a clear manifestation of her Founder’s will to give to the apostles, and in them to their successors, the power to bind and loose, to forgive or retain sins.

Catholic doctrine holds that this promise was fulfilled shortly before Christ ascended into heaven. The Council of Trent, stating clearly the Church’s faith, declares that our Lord instituted the sacrament of Penance notably on the occasion when, after his Resurrection, he breathed upon his disciples, saying: “Receive the Holy Spirit; whose sins you shall forgive, they are forgiven them; and whose sins you shall retain, they are retained.”[^c10-10] The universal agreement of the Fathers has always understood that by such a striking action and by such clear words the power of remitting and of retaining sins, and of reconciling the faithful who have fallen after Baptism, was communicated to the apostles and to their legitimate successors.

Therefore, this holy Council accepts and approves the true meaning of those words of our Lord. And it condemns the false interpretation of those who attack the institution of this sacrament by distorting those words and referring them to the preaching of the word of God and to the announcing of the gospel of Christ.[^c10-11]

The power to forgive sins is proper only to God. Who can forgive sins but God alone?[^c10-12] But since the time of her divine foundation the Church has taught that this same power was entrusted by Christ to his apostles and to their legitimate successors in the priesthood. The help of divine goodness was so ordered that the indulgence of God cannot be obtained except by the supplications of the priests. For “the one Mediator of God and men, the man Christ Jesus”,[^c10-13] has entrusted this power to the leaders of the Church, that they might both minister the action of penance to those confessing, and admit the same persons, cleansed by salutary satisfaction, to the communion of the sacraments through the gate of reconciliation.[^c10-14]

Minister of Penance

By divine institution, only a person who has been ordained to the priesthood can administer this sacrament. No one else has the power to forgive sins. As St Basil wrote in the fourth century: The same rule applies to the confession of sins as in the cure of the sick. The illnesses of the body are not shown to everyone, or to just anyone, but only to those who are proficient in treating illnesses. So, in an analogous way, the confession of sins should only be made to those who can cure them, as it is written: “You who are strong, ought to bear with the failings of the weak”,[^c10-15] that is, take them into your care and love.[^c10-16] It should be noted, however, that the priest, in forgiving sins in the Sacrament of Penance, is somewhat different from the doctor who cures the sick. The priest’s power does not come from his knowledge, study, or technical skill; it comes immediately and gratuitously from God, without any personal merit. The priest does not forgive sins because he is an ‘expert’, but by virtue of the sacrament of Holy Orders.

In a solemn confirmation of the teaching of Scripture and the Fathers, the Council of Trent declared that the teachings which dangerously extend the office of the Keys to all men without exception, even to those who are not bishops and priests, are false and completely foreign to the truth of the Gospel. Those who teach such doctrines think that Christ’s words, “Whatever you bind on earth shall be bound in heaven, and whatever you loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven”[^c10-17] and “Whose sins you shall forgive, they are forgiven them; whose sins you shall retain, they are retained”[^c10-18] are in contradiction to the institution of this sacrament, addressed to all the faithful without distinction or discrimination, with the result that each individual has the power to forgive sins, public sins by a reprimand, if the person reprimanded acquiesces, and secret sins by voluntary confession to anyone.[^c10-19]

To be able to administer penance validly, however, it is not sufficient to have been ordained. Since this sacrament is a true judgement in which the penitent confesses his faults as a guilty party and the priest absolves them as a judge by virtue of the power received from God, the very nature and idea of a trial demands that the sentence be pronounced over one’s own subjects.[^c10-20]

Together with the power of Orders then, the power of jurisdiction is also required. To administer penance validly, the confessor must therefore obtain the proper faculties from the corresponding ordinary.

Because they have received this power of jurisdiction immediately from Jesus Christ, the Roman Pontiff for the universal Church, as well as the bishops in their dioceses, can reserve the absolution of certain especially serious sins and penalties.[^c10-21] However, in order to see that no one would be lost because of this, the Church of God has always seen to it that no reservation exist in the moment of death, so that all priests can absolve any penitent from any sins and censures.[^c10-22] In these cases, since the eternal salvation of souls lies in the balance, the Church proceeds solicitously to heal the wounds of her children by means of any one of her ministers, even though they do not have faculties or have been suspended from their ministerial functions. Church law requires, however, that if the penitent should recover and no longer be in danger of death, he must have recourse to the legitimate authority to be absolved from these reserved censures,[^c10-23] or go to confession again to a priest who has the necessary jurisdiction.

By God’s express will, the Sacrament of Penance is a judgement which the priest presides over and exercises in the name, and with the authority, of Jesus Christ. It is a judgement in which the sentence is always one of pardon, as long as the sinner is repentant and humbly confesses his faults. In the first century of the life of the Church, St Polycarp, a disciple of St John the Apostle, exhorted those who had the power to bind and to loose: The presbyters should be inclined to compassion, being merciful to all..., not too severe in their judgement, knowing that we are all debtors to sin.[^c10-24] When the priest pronounces the words of absolution, he does not do so as if he here merely declaring that the sins are forgiven, but rather through a judicial act by which he, as judge, pronounces the sentence.[^c10-25] Because of this, the words of absolution are said in the indicative mood: I absolve you from your sins, in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit. What depths of mercy there are in the God’s justice! For, in the judgements of men, he who confesses his fault is punished; and in the judgement of God, he is pardoned.

Blessed be the holy Sacrament of Penance![^c10-26]

This role of judge can be exercised by any priest who has the proper jurisdiction, even though he be a sinner or unworthy. And here lies the great mystery of divine love. The Church teaches that even priests who are in mortal sin exercise the office of remitting sins as the ministers of Christ by the power of the Holy Spirit conferred on them when they were ordained. Those who say that sinful priests do not have this power judge falsely.[^c10-27] It is Christ himself who, making use of the minister as an instrument, absolves and pronounces the words of forgiveness. Thus grace, whose ordinary channel is the sacraments, is guaranteed to reach all souls.

Need for the sacrament of Penance

All the baptised who have committed a mortal sin must have recourse to the sacrament of Penance to obtain God’s forgiveness, or at least then must repent with an act of perfect contrition and with the resolution to go to confession as soon as possible. Without this sincere and genuine desire to make a good confession, united to an act of perfect contrition, it is impossible for a sinner to be saved. For the sacrament of Penance is the way Christ has provided for Christians to obtain God’s pardon. Refusal to receive it as such would indicate the absence of true repentance.

The Catholic Church has always held this with absolute certainty. Origen, an authorised witness to oriental tradition, expounded on the different kinds of forgiveness of sins that are contained in the Gospel. After mentioning Baptism (which forgives all sins) and the different acts of piety, prayer, alms, etc., which only forgive venial sins, he states that there is another way to win remission of sins which is very harsh and costly: Penance. The sinner bathes his bed with tears, and his tears are his consolation day and night.[^c10-28] And he is not ashamed to confess his sins to the priest of the Lord and to ask him for medicine.[^c10-29]

This constant teaching of Christ’s Church on the sacrament of Penance has, in the course of the centuries, spread throughout the whole world. The Council of Trent, interpreting tradition, explained the need for penance thus: If all the regenerated had enough gratitude to God to keep forever the justice received in Baptism by his grace and goodness, there would have been no need to institute any other sacrament besides Baptism for the remission of sins. But since God “Who is rich in mercy”[^c10-30] “knows our frail structure”,[^c10-31] he has also prepared a remedy of life for those who, after baptism, have given themselves over to the slavery of sin and to the power of the devil. This remedy is the sacrament of Penance. Through it the benefit of Christ’s death is applied to those who have fallen after baptism.[^c10-32] It is a necessity of divine law, decreed by God himself,[^c10-33] which no authority on earth can revoke. Nevertheless, God’s mercy has provided that in the extreme case of a true and irremediable emergency in which it is impossible to go to a confessor, one can obtain the forgiveness of sins by making a act of perfect contrition, coupled with the effective desire to receive the sacrament as soon as possible.[^c10-34]

To help her children reconcile themselves to God and periodically rectify their lives, our Mother the Church has determined, to the great benefit of souls,[^c10-35] that mortal sins should be confessed at least once a year.[^c10-36] This also facilitates the fulfilment of the precept to receive Communion during Easter time. This obligation refers only to mortal sins and is not fulfilled by a sacrilegious confession or one that is voluntary bad.[^c10-37] It also obliges baptised children once they reach the use of reason and are able to distinguish between good and evil and hence able to commit sin.[^c10-38]

Spreading the doctrine of the need for frequent confession is a fundamental part of our apostolate; without it we could not possibly encourage our friends to live a truly Christian life. We must try to get the persons we know to receive this sacrament regularly. Anyone who aspires to progress along the way of sanctity cannot be content with merely fulfilling the precept of yearly confession; for this would deprive him of a divine help that strengthens us all against temptation, and it would be very hard for him to avoid offending God. Frequent confession, even when there are no mortal sins, is an indispensable means to purify the soul and fill it with grace and fortitude.

Those who do not understand the need for frequent confession do not understand either that one must go to the doctor frequently for a checkup. I need to go to confession every week, and sometimes two or three times a week; and I am not at all scrupulous, but I know what my soul needs.

These people who do not understand... could it not be because they do not want to show the poison they are carrying within? Could it not be that they are like the people who go to the doctor but do not want to tell him how long they have been sick, what their symptoms are, or where it hurts? These people, my children, need to go to a veterinarian, not to a doctor, because they are like animals... that do not talk.

It is very comfortable to ignore sin. It is very comfortable... for the moment. But as the years pass, those who have lived that way find they are like a dirty rag; they know they have led a useless and miserable life. Struggle, my children! And teach your friends to struggle, with optimism.

We must realise that confession was instituted by Jesus Christ as an effective remedy for our weakness. Consequently we must go to confession as soon as we notice the first symptoms of our illness, no matter how slight they should be. Otherwise it would mean that we are unaware of our condition and are acting rashly. Moreover it would be a sure sign of our unwillingness to live the life of Christ with our whole soul. Our Father insisted: For me, confession is a necessity. When I see something in me that is not right, I feel the absolute need of going to confession. I am not at all ashamed to say so.

First you have to say what you would not want to be known. Once this is said, the matter is straightened out. If we do not do this, what worries us keeps growing larger, like a snowball. It grows until it becomes gigantic. Then we go through some terrible days of senseless suffering, all because of foolishness.

This way of acting is a consequence of a living faith in the grace of the sacrament of Penance. We go to confession therefore as often as necessary to ask our Lord for forgiveness for our offences. And we go too when it would be good for our soul, that is, when we feel sorry for our lukewarmness and want to confess this to God, seeking in his Grace strength for our weakness and a love that is renewed and purified.

Effects of the sacrament of Penance

An integral and contrite confession, with the resolution to sin no more and to fulfil the penance, is a well-made confession. Its principal and primary effect is reconciliation with God, which in devout men who receive this sacrament with devotion, is sometimes followed by peace and serenity of conscience, joined to a great consolation of soul.[^c10-39] All the sins committed after baptism are forgiven; and if any of them were mortal, the soul once again becomes the temple of God and dwelling place of the Blessed Trinity. The Church teaches that the justification received both in Baptism and in Penance is not only the remission of sins, but also the sanctification and renewal of the inner man through the voluntary reception of grace and the gifts, whereby a man becomes just instead of unjust, and a friend instead of an enemy, that he may be an “heir in hope of eternal life”.[^c10-40] [^c10-41]

The divine life newly infused or fortified by the sacrament of Penance is of a special nature: besides conferring sanctifying grace, this sacrament also confers a specific Grace, causing a new likeness to Christ to be impressed on the soul. This sacramental grace, at the same time, is intimately and essentially aimed at warding off the sins that have been blotted out by the sacrament. Furthermore it is a guarantee that the penitent has been granted the strength to overcome the temptation to commit again the sins already forgiven.

On the other hand, it is easier for the darkness and difficulties threatening a person’s soul to smother, or exceedingly weaken, even the light of Faith when he neglects this saving remedy of Penance, where the soul can always obtain God’s forgiveness and mercy. The person who does not try to get out of sin and to put every effort into recovering grace will easily end up, sooner or later, denying the very basis of the moral law, thus trying, more or less consciously, to justify his way of acting.

Faced with this apparent loss of supernatural faith in so many people young and old, our Father has made us realise that very often they still have faith, even though weakened and hidden by their faults. Deep down inside they are good people, but they have forgotten what the life of a Christian is like. This is true, above all, if they have let themselves be carried away by sensuality and have formed a hardened skin which prevents them enjoying spiritual things.

Have you ever seen the hand of a small child? If a mosquito lands on it, the child immediately reacts. If the same mosquito lands on the weather-beaten hand of a farm-worker, he does not notice it. This is what happens to these friends: they do not realise, they do not notice what God is asking of them.

Since being ordained I have dealt with many young people. And whenever a Catholic has told me that he did not have Faith, I answered, “Get down on your knees and make a good Confession.” I helped him make a deep examination of conscience and afterwards he got up with faith. It is not that he did not have it, but just that he was carrying such a load of filth on him. How, in that state, was he going to see with faith? First he had to get rid of that rotten load.

When one makes a good confession, all those doubts disappear immediately. Nevertheless, as happens with the sick, these poor souls afterwards need a time of convalescence. Often it is not a question of not having faith, but rather of not living a clean life, our Father has told us.

Induimini Dominum Iesum Christum. Put on the Lord Jesus Christ, says St Paul to the Romans. It is in the sacrament of Penance that you and I put on Jesus Christ and his merits.[^c10-42] Here the infinite merits of the Passion and Death of our Lord are applied to us. Consequently, as the Council of Trent teaches with regard to justification in general, we are renewed in the spirit of our mind and are not only considered just but we are called that and such we are, as each one of us receives his own justice according to the measure in which “the Holy Spirit gives to each according as he wills,”[^c10-43] and according to the proper dispositions and the co-operation of each.[^c10-44]

The sacrament of Penance, besides pardoning the guilt, also completely pardons the eternal punishment due in justice for the sins.[^c10-45] The temporal punishment, on the other hand, as Sacred Scripture teaches, is not always entirely done away with.[^c10-46] It depends on the intensity of the contrition and the personal dispositions of the penitent. Because of this, it is very advisable to prepare well for confession, to arouse in the soul a true sorrow for sins and to receive the sacrament devoutly. The rest of the temporal punishment is pardoned through the satisfaction imposed by the priest on the penitent, through the sacrifices, prayers, works of mercy, indulgences, etc. that can be gained during one’s lifetime, and finally through the suffering borne after death in purgatory.

We, as God’s children, should remind Christians about the whole doctrine of faith on the sacrament of Penance, nowadays so much attacked in certain sectors and so little practised in many others. At the same time, we must teach people the theory and practice of this sacrament, by making them see the absolute necessity of renewing their friendship with God when it has been broken off through mortal sin, and making them see its great usefulness for growing in sanctity. Thus we will counteract, with an abundant sowing of sound doctrine, the spread of weeds and error. St Paul, writing to Timothy about the true Christian doctrine he was to teach, told him: If you put these instructions before the brethren, you will be a good minister of Christ Jesus, nourished on the words of the faith and of the good doctrine which you have followed. Have nothing to do with godless and silly myths. Train yourself in godliness.[^c10-47] They are myths which are sometimes disguised with the attractive garments of science and speculation, gifts of God that many people insist on abusing. These are waterless springs and mists driven by a storm; for them the nether gloom of darkness has been reserved. For, uttering loud boasts of folly, they entice with licentious passions of the flesh men who have barely escaped from those who live in error. They promise them freedom, but they themselves are slaves of corruption.[^c10-48]

Our Father reminded us: You must be firm in your criteria, my children, especially today when errors are cropping up which seem new and are really old, since they have been condemned many times by the Church. And without being unbearably dogmatic (for the only dogmas are those of the Church), you must feel responsible before God and history for shaping the present day in a Christian manner, without yielding in principles or making compromises, which seem to me nothing more than roguery.

Haec est victoria quae vincit mundum, fides nostra (1 John 5:4). Faith in Christ engenders certainty and we have the mission to bring clarity to men’s minds, and peace to their hearts, rescuing them from an atmosphere of doubt, of relativism, of uncertainty, which it is not hard to fall into. We must teach them that a Catholic can have clear doctrine, that he can have faith... and still be fragile. It is a diabolical temptation to justify our passions by trying to adapt the faith to them. The unjustifiable can not be justified. While being understanding with them, we must keep people from throwing everything overboard and show them what they must go on practising, in spite of their frailty.[^c10-49]

## THE SACRAMENT OF PENANCE (II) {#cuaderno.1.11}

The sacrament of Penance is a source of grace and forgiveness. Christ himself instituted it before his glorious Ascension into heaven. He entrusted it to his Apostles and their successors to administer in his name. It is a splendid manifestation of divine mercy: *As I live, says the Lord God, I have no pleasure in the death of the wicked, but that the wicked man turn from his way and live; turn back, turn back from your evil ways for why will you die, O house of Israel?*[^c11-1] *Come now, let us reason together, says the Lord, though your sins are like scarlet they shall be white as snow; though they are red like crimson, they shall become like wool*.[^c11-2]

Part of the work of catechesis, which we must promote through our apostolate of doctrine, refers to the use of this sacrament in accord with the Faith that the Church teaches us. As our Father has told us: ***Confession is very necessary. You already know this from the catechism. One cannot live without Confession because we would be like walking corpses. We need to go to our Lord, making a good examination of conscience, to acknowledge our mistakes, to be sorry for our faults, to make good resolutions, to have faith in the word of God, to receive absolution, to fulfil the penance and to begin to struggle again. Confession is a wonderful thing. I go to Confession as often as I need to.***

Each of the Sacraments have matter and form. In most of them, the matter is some material thing: water bread and wine, oil\... In the sacrament of Penance the matter is not something material, and it is therefore called *quasi-matter*. The Church teaches that *the quasi-matter of this Sacrament is the acts of the penitent, namely, contrition, confession and satisfaction. Inasmuch as these acts are demanded of the penitent according to God's arrangement for the integrity of the sacrament and for the complete and perfect remission of sins, they are, for that reason, called the parts of penance*.[^c11-3] It is necessary to approach this Sacrament with true sorrow for sins and with the firm purpose of amendment, and to accept the penance which the priest, as God's minister, imposes. One must also have the intention of confessing each and every mortal sin and make a diligent examination of conscience. This disposition is indispensable for receiving the grace which God our Father gives us in this sacrament.

*The first act of the penitent: Contrition*

The first act of the penitent is contrition: *A deep sorrow and detestation for sin committed, with a resolution of sinning no more. Moreover, this spirit of contrition has always been necessary to obtain forgiveness of sins, and thus, in the case of a man who has fallen after baptism, it only prepares for the remission of sins if it is accompanied by trust in divine mercy and a firm desire of fulfilling the other conditions necessary to receive the sacrament properly*.[^c11-4] Sorrow for having offended God is indispensable, although it is *not sufficient* to obtain forgiveness. The resolve not to sin again and voluntary rejection of the sins committed are also required. *Therefore, this Holy Council declares that this contrition implies not only abandoning sin and determining to lead a new life and beginning to do so, but also hating one's past life, according to the words: "Cast away from you all your transgressions by which you have transgressed, and make to yourselves a new heart and a new spirit"*.[^c11-5] [^c11-6]

The Church holds that there are two types of sorrow and hatred for sin: *Perfect contrition*, when the sorrow comes from a pure love for God, and *imperfect contrition or attrition*, which stems from fear of the punishment which the fault deserves, though implicitly this fear also includes the love of God. Perfect contrition, resulting from an ardent love of God whom we have offended, is so pleasing in his eyes that when it is impossible to go to Confession, *it reconciles man to God before this sacrament is actually received.* Nevertheless, the Council of Trent goes on to affirm, *the reconciliation must not be attributed to a contrition which excludes the desire for the Sacrament which must be included in the contrition*.[^c11-7] Therefore it does not mean that the oral confession of sins is superfluous: on the contrary, perfect contrition presupposes a desire for oral confession and is by nature ordained to it.[^c11-8] It would be contradictory to have perfect sorrow for sins united to rejection of the divine precept to confess them to a priest.

Furthermore every sin is in some way an offence against the Church. For this reason, the forgiveness is, by God's will, forgiveness granted by the Church: God's forgiveness follows that of the Church.[^c11-9] And the only authorised and true representative of the Church in this judgement is a priest with jurisdiction, who makes Christ present among the faithful and absolves with authority received from him.

Actual confession of sins is necessary also because no one can be absolutely certain that his contrition is sufficiently perfect. For this reason the Church requires that those who desire to receive Communion not be conscious of being in mortal sin. In order to partake of the Eucharistic Sacrifice it is not sufficient to make an act of perfect contrition with the intention of going to confession later, except in very rare and special cases, for example, when there is a grave and immediate danger of scandal. Since no one ordinarily can be certain of the sufficiency of his contrition, it would show great disregard for Christ to go and receive him without the necessary dispositions. As St Paul warns: *Whoever, therefore, eats the Bread or drinks the Cup of the Lord in an unworthy manner is guilty of profaning the Body and Blood of the Lord*.[^c11-10]

Imperfect contrition is sufficient to receive the sacrament of Penance, though certainly with less supernatural benefit. The Church teaches that *imperfect contrition, which is called attrition because it is usually conceived either from a consideration of the malice of sin or from the fear of hell and its punishments, if it excludes the will to sin and is accompanied by hope for pardon, does not make a man a hypocrite or a greater sinner; but it is a gift from God and an inspiration of the Holy Spirit, not, indeed, as already dwelling in the soul, but as only giving an impulse that helps the penitent make his way towards justice. And although attrition without the sacrament of Penance cannot by itself lead the sinner to justification, still it disposes him for the attainment of God's grace in the sacrament of Penance*.[^c11-11]

In receiving this sacrament we ought to have the disposition of being truly and profoundly sorry for our sins, of detesting them and reflecting that Christ died on the Cross in order to free us from them. If men could only realise the malice of sin and God's infinite goodness and holiness, if they had even an inkling of this *mystery of iniquity*[^c11-12] which is the only force capable of breaking the supernatural bond that unites Christians with their Father of heaven, they would weep bitterly when faced with the possibility of offending our Lord, and they would always try to remain in his grace.

Our personal dealings with God, our life of piety, make us more sensitive to sins, our own and those of others. They teach us to react immediately. We must ask our Lord to give us and everyone the grace of a refined conscience which always provokes immediate reaction to sin, because ***pride does violence to the memory and darkens it. What has been done is clouded over or is embellished, and one finds a justification to cloak with goodness the evil which one has committed and is not prepared to rectify. Arguments and reasons build up, tending to drown the voice of conscience, making it ever weaker and more confused.***[^c11-13]

On the other hand, recognising the reality of our sins is a starting point for repentance and contrition. Our Father wrote: ***We must help those who fall to get up again right away, to go to Confession as often as necessary: every day, if it is advisable. We must instil in them the conviction that falls are not inevitable (if they were they would not be sinful), that it is possible to sanctify oneself and that one must begin again and use the means, that grace will not be lacking. Without lessening the importance of one**'**s failures, one must avoid discouragement and, with supernatural outlook, one must increase one**'**s confidence in God.***[^c11-14] We must help those who fall to use all the human and supernatural means to avoid sin in the future: to flee the occasions, to frequent the Sacraments, to go to Our Lady and to their guardian angel\...

We could also remind our friends that this life is the period of God's Mercy, a time we all should take advantage of, going to the sacrament of Penance with a contrite heart. After death there is no longer any chance either for repentance or for forgiveness. St Cyprian wrote: *I beg of you, my brethren, let each one of you confess his sins while he still lives in this world, whilst his confession can still be heard, while he still can perform satisfaction and while it pleases the Lord that pardon be granted by the priests*.[^c11-15]

Each one of us should see how he can foster this disposition of repentance, of contrition, so necessary to obtain mercy. As the Father has taught us: ***When faced with our blunders and mistakes, we should react immediately by making an act of contrition, which comes to our heart and lips as quickly as blood rushes to a wound, successfully resisting the foreign body, the germ of infection,***[^c11-16] and if necessary, going to the Sacrament of Penance.

If there is contrition, if the soul turns to God sincerely, ready to begin again, if it humbly admits that it has sinned and weeps for its offences against God, all mistakes are corrected, no matter how grave. ***In the affairs of the soul nothing is ever hopelessly lost. The humble and contrite effort to retain what remains will ensure the recovery of even more than what was lost.*** **Now the intensity of the penitent's repentance may sometimes be proportionate to a greater grace than that from which he fell by sinning\... wherefore the penitent sometimes rises to a greater grace than that which he possessed before** ***(St Thomas, S. Th., III, q.89, a.2 c).***[^c11-17] There is no room for discouragement, which only helps the enemy. God our Father is always watching out for us: He is a Father who awaits but the slightest sign of sorrow in order to forgive us compassionately and to fill us with his grace. He urges us to be on our way again, to begin the return trip step by step. No fall, no matter how serious, definitively separates us from God if we place our confidence in him and seek forgiveness in the sacrament of Penance.

*The second act of the penitent: Confession*

The second act which the penitent must perform is the oral confession of his sins. This confession must necessarily include each and every one of the mortal sins committed after baptism and not yet directly remitted in a previous Confession. Consequently we speak about *necessary* matter, because to omit this culpably would invalidate the sacrament. Since Penance is a true judgement, *it is clear that priests can not exercise this judgement unless they know the case, nor can they observe equity in imposing penances if the faithful declare their sins only in a general way, and not specifying them one by one. This leads to the conclusion that penitents must mention in Confession all the mortal sins of which they are aware after a thorough examination, even if they be most hidden sins and committed against only the last two of the ten commandments; for these sins sometimes wound the soul more grievously and are more dangerous than those which are committed out in the open.*[^c11-18]

The doctrine of the Church, solemnly declared by the Council of Trent, has always been that mortal sins must be confessed according to their number and species. At the beginning of the third century, Origen wrote: *Hear what the law ordains: "if a man is guilty of any of these sins, he shall confess the sin he has committed."*[^c11-19] *There is a profound mystery in the fact that it is ordained that we declare our sins. Hence, all the things that we have done must be declared and made public according to their kind*.[^c11-20]

The moral content of human actions, even though determined principally by the objective end of the act, can be modified according to the different circumstances that concur in it: *the place where it happens; the person who does it; the subjective end that the person intends; the manner, the duration or time when it was done: all these can decisively modify its goodness or malice and even radically change its morality. It follows that those circumstances which change the species of the sin must all be mentioned in confession because if these circumstances are omitted, unless the penitents confess their sins entirely, the judges do not know their sins, and it is impossible for them to judge rightly as to the gravity of the sins and to impose on the penitents the penalty which they ought to impose*.[^c11-21] Without doubt, in exceptional cases, the mercy of God and that of the Church has determined for the good of souls that this grave obligation does not oblige, even though it must be fulfilled as soon as those special grave circumstances that waived the integrity have disappeared.[^c11-22]

A person should not worry if, after Confession and after having made due examination of his conscience, he realises that he has forgotten some grave sin. *For what the Church demands from each penitent is that he examine himself quite carefully and look into the innermost recesses of his conscience and that he confess those sins by which he remembers having mortally offended his Lord and God. But the other sins which he does not remember after a thorough examination are considered as included in a general way in the same confession. It is for these sins that we pray sincerely with the Prophet: "From my hidden sins cleanse me, O Lord."*[^c11-23] However, if these sins are remembered later they must be told in the following confession.[^c11-24]

Confession of mortal sins according to their number and species, together with the circumstances that could change their morality, is a normally indispensable means for the continual better formation of the consciences of the faithful, so that they may act more freely.[^c11-25] In this way scruples are avoided, for the soul relies on the help of the priest to distinguish, in specific cases and not only in general, what is sinful from what is not. This divine precept does not imply any interference in the intimacy of conscience. It is rather a vivid reflection of the intimate union of the penitent with Jesus Christ himself who acts through his minister in the sacrament of Penance.

Through the mercy of God, when a person who has real interior life puts all his effort into fulfilling the Will of God, it is rare that he commits mortal sins. This is no reason for such a person to be deprived of the benefits of the sacrament of Penance. *Venial sins, those which do not exclude us from God's grace and into which we fall rather frequently, are properly and advantageously mentioned, and this implies no presumption, as is clear from the practice of devout men. Nevertheless\... they can be atoned for in many other ways*.[^c11-26]

The purpose of the sacrament of Penance is not only to forgive mortal sins and return the life of grace to the soul but is also to forgive all sins, including venial ones, and to communicate the specific graces needed precisely to overcome these sins or evil inclinations of which the penitent accuses himself, even though they are not grave faults. It also strengthens the virtues which these faults oppose. Besides the role of a judge, the priest is also carrying out the role of a teacher and shepherd, and his help is very useful for progressing in the spiritual life. Even though it can be presumed from previous experience that we will commit these venial sins again, it is not irreverent to confess them, since there is true repentance and the Confession will give us grace not to commit them again, at least not as easily.

Precisely because the sacraments are indispensable for the growth of Christian life, one of the goals we set for ourselves in apostolate is that our friends and acquaintances, and all the people with whom we are actually dealing, receive the sacraments frequently. As Pius XII said: *To hasten daily progress along the path of virtue we advocate earnestly the pious practice of frequent Confession. Not without the inspiration of the Holy Spirit was this practice introduced into the Church. By it genuine self-knowledge is increased, Christian humility grows, bad habits are corrected, spiritual neglect and tepidity are countered, the conscience is purified, the will is strengthened, a salutary self-control is attained and grace is increased by virtue of the Sacrament itself. Let those, therefore\... who make light of or weaken esteem for frequent Confession realise that what they are doing is foreign to the Spirit of Christ and disastrous for the Mystical Body of our Saviour*.[^c11-27]

***Show your gratitude to our Lord***, our Father told us, ***for the institution of the holy sacrament of Penance by going frequently to receive it. You know well that even though there be nothing, or almost nothing, to confess, this sacrament strengthens us by drawing closer to Christ our Lord.*** In this case it is advisable to accuse oneself of some mortal sin from one's past life already forgiven in a previous confession, or of faults committed against a particular virtue or commandment. And so, by the intensity of our repentance, as well as the actual confession of these sins which have already been forgiven, there will certainly be sufficient matter to receive the absolution, and the virtues most weakened by these sins will be strengthened with the grace of the Sacrament.

*The need for oral confession*

***You must prepare yourselves well***, our Father advised us, ***to make your oral confession, as the old catechism says. All those comments to the contrary, which you hear around, are lies!***

When Christ instituted each and every one of the sacraments, he gave the Church the power to determine the specific way to administer them without changing anything essential.[^c11-28] Thus, even though the Gospel does not explicitly state that Confession must be private and secret, the Church, by the power received from Christ, can make this a necessary requirement. We must keep in mind, moreover that Sacred Scripture is not the only source of Revelation. The Word of God is also contained in the non-written Tradition which has come down to us from Jesus Christ himself.[^c11-29]

The oral and secret confession of mortal sins is an almost immemorial practice in the Church. As St Ephraem taught in the fourth century, *First tell your sins to be justified. Penance begins with words because the confession that is made with the lips is already the beginning of penance*.[^c11-30] Furthermore, it is appropriate for the confession of sins to be made secretly because in this way souls can come more easily to this sacrament, without the fear and shame of having others know their sins. *God does not ask you to reveal your sins before witnesses in the midst of the theatre. He tells you: confess your faults to Me alone, privately, so that I may cure your wounds and take this burden from you*.[^c11-31]

In its consideration of this age-old practice in the Church, the Council of Trent states that *although Christ has not prohibited that one confess sins publicly in expiation for his crimes and for his own humiliation, and as an example to others, as well as for the edification of the Church offended, yet this is not commanded by divine precept nor would it be advisedly enjoined by any human law that offences, especially secret ones, be disclosed in a public confession*.[^c11-32] The Church has always observed this norm. In the first centuries, when it was a case of especially grave and public sins, the Church, however did allow sacramental satisfaction to be made publicly but not Confession, which was ordinarily made privately before the minister.[^c11-33]

Collective penitential acts and also the general confession recited by the priest and faithful at the beginning of Mass serve only to foster contrition, to forgive venial sins and to prepare the soul to assist with greater benefit at the Eucharistic Sacrifice; but they have no value in so far as the remission of mortal sins is concerned. The Church allows general absolution, without previous oral confession of sins, only in cases of particular necessity or of imminent danger of death, when it is physically or morally impossible for all the people in a crowd to go to Confession one by one, as happens in time of war, in a shipwreck, etc. And even in these cases, once the extraordinary circumstances no longer exist, these sins should be confessed in the ordinary way.[^c11-34]

After confession has been made with true sorrow and all the mortal sins weighing on one's conscience have been made known, and after absolution has been received, God once again resides in the soul which has yielded to Him and he fills it with peace, serenity and joy. ***We all need interior peace, and this peace comes when we try to take away whatever obstacles there are between this poor heart of ours, which after all is of flesh, and the Heart of Jesus and the Most Sweet Heart of Mary. For this reason,*** our Father told us, ***I advise everyone (and I insist that it is only advice) to have the devotion of making many acts of contrition. And one practical external manifestation of this devotion is to have a special affection for the holy sacrament of Penance.***

***If it is always a wonderful means to be united to God, it is all the more so in these times in which we are living, when this sacrament is attacked so much. I advise you never to miss the weekly Confession that our plan of life offers us. And go even more frequently during the week, if you have something bothering you: Get it out! The sooner, the better: as soon as possible, quickly!***

***To be cheerful always, we must be normal people, not neurotics. And if there is any reason for sadness, you ought to go to Confession, which is a great sacrament. Confession must be done well, with the tongue, saying first that which makes us most ashamed, that which we would not want to be known****.*

*The third act of the penitent: Satisfaction*

The sacramental act of Penance does not terminate with the oral confession of sins. Another part of the substance of the Sacrament is for the penitent to accept the satisfaction that is imposed by the priest in order to make amends to divine justice as far as is humanly possible.[^c11-35]

In the common opinion of the Fathers of the Church, Penance is like *a second life-saving plank following a shipwreck* and that it demands, of its very nature, a special effort from the soul to obtain pardon of the whole punishment due to its sins. *The plan of divine justice would seem to demand that those who have fallen in ignorance before their baptism be restored to grace in a way different from those who, once freed from servitude to the devil and from sin, having received the gift of the Holy Spirit, have not shrunk from knowingly violating the temple of God*[^c11-36] *and grieving the Holy Spirit*.[^c11-37]^-^[^c11-38] For the Sacrament of Penance really to take place, it is necessary that the penitent accept the satisfaction imposed by the priest before absolution is given and have a sincere intention of fulfilling it. In the event of failure to do or complete the penance, the Confession is valid (it already was) although this non-fulfilment is a sin, grave or light according to the particular case.

Many reasons affirm the necessity of sacramental satisfaction. In the first place, it is in accord *with divine mercy that sins should not be forgiven us without any satisfaction, lest we find an opportunity*[^c11-39] *to regard sins lightly, and, offending and insulting the Holy Spirit, fall into more serious sins, "storing up retribution to ourselves on the day of wrath"*.^\ ^[^c11-40] *There is no doubt that satisfaction deters from sin, and acts as a bridle on penitents, making them more cautious and watchful in the future. It heals the effects of sins by acts of the contrary virtues and does away with evil habits acquired in a bad life*. [^c11-41]

With respect to satisfaction, the Father has given his sons the priests this advice: ***In general, impose penances that are easy to fulfil. If necessary, the priest himself should make up for the rest of the penance, even through corporal mortification. The penances, besides being easy to fulfil, should be very precise. Souls should see the depth of Christ**'**s mercy and should rectify out of love. At the same time, demand with all firmness what in conscience must be demanded, for the holiness of the sacrament and for the good of the souls themselves.***[^c11-42]

With these acts of satisfaction, the repentant sinner separates himself from *the threatened punishments of God*[^c11-43] as Sacred Scripture so often testifies. *Again, in making satisfaction for our sins, we are made like to Christ Jesus, who has satisfied for our sins,*[^c11-44]*and "from whom is all our capability".*^\ ^[^c11-45] *We also have the firm assurance that "if we suffer with him we shall also be glorified with him"*.^\ ^[^c11-46] [^c11-47]

This solidarity with the suffering Christ gives all our acts of satisfaction a divine value, even though the infinite merits of Christ[^c11-48] are in no way diminished because of them. *A man has nothing in which he can take pride; but all our pride is in Christ, in whom we live, in whom we move,*[^c11-49] *in whom we make satisfaction, bringing forth fruits worthy of repentance*,[^c11-50] all of which have their value from Christ, and are offered to the Father by Christ.[^c11-51]

The satisfaction we make for sins is a real manifestation of love of God and of desire to make reparation and atonement. We must teach this to everyone by encouraging them to fulfil with devotion the penance imposed on them and to be generous in accepting cheerfully those penances that God sends them. These also show his fatherly love, for *the generosity of the divine bounty is so great that we can make satisfaction in God's sight through Jesus Christ, not only by the penances that we voluntarily undertake to make up for sin, or by those that the priest decides to impose proportionately to the gravity of the sin, but also (and this is a greater proof of love) by bearing patiently the temporal punishments sent by God*. [^c11-52]

The sacrament of Penance is a divine gift, an infallible medicine for any infirmity of the soul. We must receive it with devotion and spread its practice among Christians. It brings about a personal encounter with Jesus Christ who is always ready to heal us, to restore our spiritual life and to make us grow in sanctity. Our human weakness is in constant need of this divine remedy, *because there is no just man on the earth who does good and never sins*. ^5^[^c11-53]^3^

[^c11-33]: *The secret sacramental Confession which the Holy Church used in the beginning and still uses has always been unanimously recommended by the ancient and holy Fathers. Hence we clearly reject the vain calumny of those who dare to teach that this is foreign to the divine command and a human invention of the Fathers of the Fourth Lateran Council. In that Council, the Church did not determine that the faithful were to go to Confession, for this was already known to be necessary by divine institution, but that each and every person that reached the age of reason was to fulfil the precept of Confession at least once a year* (Council of Trent, Session 14, ch. 5, (Dz 1683)

St Leo I the Great, Letter, *Sollicitudinis quidem*, 11 June 452, (Dz 308))

But by the sacrament of Penance we are by no means able to arrive at that new and spotless life without much weeping and labour, as divine justice demands. Thus the Holy Father rightly spoke of penance as \`\`a kind of difficult baptism\'\' (Session 14, ch. 2, (Dz 1672))
