## MAGISTERIUM AND JURISDICTION IN THE CHURCH {#cuaderno.1.12}

In the Mystical Body of Christ, which is the Church, all Christians share *a common dignity as members which stems from their regeneration in Christ; from the same grace of filiation and the same call to perfection: one salvation, one hope and one undivided charity*.[^c12-1] However, *for the nurturing and constant growth of the People of God, Christ the Lord instituted in his Church a variety of ministries, which work for the good of the whole Body*.[^c12-2] And having been appointed by God the Father *heir of all things*,[^c12-3] *that he might be Teacher, King and Priest of all, the head of the new and universal people of the sons of God*,[^c12-4] he willed to give to his Church the power which he himself had received. He announced this to the Apostles shortly before ascending into heaven: *All power in heaven and on earth has been given to Me. Go, therefore, and make disciples of all nations, baptising them in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, teaching them to observe all that I have commanded you*.[^c12-5]

It is because of the underlying unity of the People of God that all the members of the Church participate in this power of Christ, although in different ways, according to the diversity of functions entrusted to each.[^c12-6] But only *the Apostles were enriched by Christ with a special outpouring of the Holy Spirit coming upon them*,[^c12-7] *and they passed on this spiritual gift to their helpers by the imposition of hands*,[^c12-8] *and it has been transmitted down to us in episcopal consecration. But episcopal consecration, together with the office of sanctifying, also confers the office of teaching and of governing, which, however, of its very nature, can be exercised only in hierarchical communion with the head and members of the college*.[^c12-9]

The tasks of sanctifying, governing and teaching do not depend on the personal holiness of those entrusted with them, although the need for the personal holiness of such persons is urgent. These tasks are carried out in the name of Christ,[^c12-10] and will remain until the end of time, because our Lord, *just as he sent the Apostles, whom he had chosen for himself out of the world, as he himself was sent by the Father, so also he wished shepherds and teachers to be in his Church \`\`until the consummation of the world\'\'*.[^c12-11] [^c12-12]

*The power of Magisterium*

*The heavenly doctrine of Christ, although for the most part committed to writing under divine inspiration, would not have been able to unite the minds of men had it been entrusted to human intellects alone; it would very easily have become subject to differing interpretations. This would happen not only because of its depth and of its mysteries, but also because of the variety of man\'s interpretations and because of disputes that flare up when there are conflicting passions. From differences in interpretations a variety of beliefs is necessarily generated; whence come controversies, dissentions, and wranglings\... To unite minds, to create and preserve unity of interpretation, there had to be another principle --- in addition to the existence of Sacred Scripture. This, the wisdom of God requires: for he could not have willed that the faith should be one if he did not provide means sufficient for the preservation of this unity*.[^c12-13] The history of the Church from its very beginning gives witness to the existence of a function of magisterium, exercised in the first place by the Apostles[^c12-14] and transmitted afterwards to their successors.[^c12-15] *Therefore, as is clear from what has been said, Christ instituted in the Church a living, authoritative, and permanent magisterium, which by his own power he strengthened, by the Spirit of Truth he taught, and by miracles he confirmed. He willed and ordered, under the gravest penalties, that its teachings should be received as if they were his own*.[^c12-16]

As Revelation ended with the death of the last Apostle, the mission of the Magisterium is not to manifest any new doctrine, but to defend, guard and interpret the deposit of faith that she has received,[^c12-17] making certain *to dispel and eliminate all that is opposed to the faith or that would in any way endanger the salvation of souls*.[^c12-18] Thus, the Church cannot allow the revealed truths to become obscured,[^c12-19] or the meaning of a defined dogma to vary, *because, and this is something which must be repeated often, what deserved to be cut off by apostolic hands with the blade of the gospel and with the consent of the universal Church cannot recover strength to live again. Nor can that which has clearly been destined for the eternal fire become again a fertile shoot in the Lord\'s vineyard*.[^c12-20]

Although the magisterium of the Church is exercised through human instruments, it is not a human magisterium. Christ himself declared this to the disciples: *The Advocate, the Holy Spirit, whom the Father will send in my name, he will teach you all things, and bring to your mind whatever I have said to you*.[^c12-21] This is the mission of the Holy Spirit, the soul of the Church: *As he proceeds both from the Father who is the eternal Truth, and from the Son who is the substantial Truth, he receives from each his Essence and the fullness of all Truth. This Truth he communicates to his Church, by his all-powerful help, making sure that she never falls into error and that the seeds of divine doctrine are able to grow in her throughout time and be fruitful for the welfare of the nations*.[^c12-22] Consequently, infallibility is one of the characteristic notes of the universal ecclesiastical magisterium taken as a whole.[^c12-23] *As often, therefore, as it is declared on the authority of this Teaching that this or that truth is contained in the Deposit of divine revelation, it must be believed by everyone as true. If it could in any way be false, an evident contradiction follows: for then God himself would be the author of error in man*.[^c12-24]

This truth has always been accepted in the Church without discussion. It was only in the sixteenth century that Protestantism dared to put it in doubt. Later on there were other heretical movements, almost all of Protestant inspiration, that did the same. Against these false assertions the Church continued to affirm that She is the sole authorised interpreter of the Faith, explaining the object of her infallible magisterium and its exercise by the competent authority.[^c12-25]

*The object of the Church\'s Magisterium*

The object of the magisterium is everything which in some way refers to faith and morals**.** *The Church through the power given to her by her divine Founder has not only the right, but principally the duty, not to tolerate but rather to proscribe and condemn all errors if this is demanded by the integrity of the Faith and the good of souls***.**[^c12-26] She also has *****the right and duty to judge social and economic matters with supreme authority\... Even though economic science and moral discipline employ their own principles in their own spheres, it is, nevertheless, an error to say that the economic and moral orders are so distinct from, and alien to, each other that in no way does the former depend on the latter*.[^c12-27] Moreover, by divine right, the Church *is also competent to interpret the natural moral law. It is in fact indisputable, as our predecessors have many times declared*,[^c12-28] *that Jesus Christ, when communicating to Peter and to the Apostles his divine authority and sending them to teach all nations his commandments*,[^c12-29] *constituted them as guardians and authentic interpreters of all the moral law, not only, that is, of the law of the Gospel, but also of the natural law, which is also an expression of the will of God, the faithful fulfilment of which is equally necessary for salvation*.[^c12-30] [^c12-31]

Another proper field for the exercise of the Church\'s magisterium is the interpretation of Sacred Scripture. Thus the Council of Trent solemnly declared: *No one should dare to rely on his own judgement in matters of faith and morals affecting the structure of Christian doctrine and to distort Sacred Scripture to fit meanings of his own that are contrary to the meaning that Holy Mother Church has held and now holds: for it is her office to judge about the true sense and interpretation of Sacred Scripture*.[^c12-32]

*Infallibility of the Magisterium*

The functions of the infallible Magisterium can only be exercised by the Roman Pontiff, the successor to the Prince of the Apostles, and by the bishops gathered together in communion with the Holy See. *They are authentic teachers, that is, teachers endowed with the authority of Christ, who preach to the people committed to them the faith they must believe and put into practice, and by the light of the Holy Spirit illustrate that faith*.^3^[^c12-33]^3^ The totality of the faithful, united in obedience to their pastors, enjoy the prerogative of making no mistake when *they show universal agreement in matters of faith and morals*.^3^[^c12-34]^4^ This sense of the faith, proper to the Christian people, must be distinguished from the prerogative of infallibility in proposing faith and morals, for this belongs solely to the Roman Pontiff and to the bishops when, gathered under his authority, they remain in communion with the Roman See.

The Magisterium of the Roman Pontiff enjoys the prerogative of infallibility when *as the supreme teacher of the universal Church, in whom the charism of infallibility of the Church itself is individually present, he is expounding or defending a doctrine of Catholic faith*.^3^[^c12-35]^5^ This was solemnly defined by the First Vatican Council: *we teach and define that it is a divinely revealed dogma: that the Roman Pontiff, when he speaks "ex cathedra", that is, when, acting in the office of shepherd and teacher of all Christians, he defines, by virtue of his supreme apostolic authority, doctrine concerning faith or morals to be held by the universal Church, possesses through the divine assistance promised to him in the person of St.Peter, the infallibility with which the divine Redeemer willed His Church to be endowed in defining doctrine concerning faith or morals*.^3^[^c12-36]^6^ And *such definitions of the Roman Pontiff are therefore irreformable because of their nature, but not because of the agreement of the Church*,^\ 3^[^c12-37]^7^ a*nd therefore they need no approval of others, nor do they allow an appeal to any other judgment,^3^*[^c12-38]*^8^* not even to an Ecumenical Council.^3^[^c12-39]^9^

While the Pope is personally infallible when he speaks *ex cathedra*, the bishops *do not individually enjoy the prerogative of infallibility*.^4^[^c12-40]^0^ For Christ did not entrust the power of magisterium to the Apostles taken individually, but rather as members of an organized College presided over by the Prince of the Apostles. For this same reason, the bishops *nevertheless proclaim Christ\'s doctrine infallibly whenever, though dispersed throughout the world, but still maintaining the bond of communion among themselves and with the successor of Peter, and authentically teaching matters of faith and morals, they are in agreement on one position as definitely to be held.^4^*[^c12-41]*^1^ This is even more clearly verified when, gathered together in an ecumenical council, they are teachers and judges of faith and morals for the universal Church.^4^*[^c12-42]*^2^* The prerogative of infallibility of an Ecumenical Council depends on the Roman Pontiff because a Council is ecumenical not just by the gathering of bishops from all over the world but when it is convoked or accepted by the Pope and presided over by him or his delegates. And even then, its decisions are not infallible until they are *personally* confirmed as such by the Roman Pontiff.^\ 4^[^c12-43]^3^

According to Catholic doctrine it is nonsense to speak of one infallible Magisterium and another non-infallible Magisterium, because all the teachings of the Church enjoy the guarantee of infallibility, although in different ways. Every isolated act of the solemn Magisterium (of the Pope when he speaks *ex* *cathedra* and of the Ecumenical Councils) is of itself infallible if it includes the essential characteristics: that it be pronounced by the Roman Pontiff in his capacity as Teacher and Shepherd of all Christians, with the fullness of his apostolic authority and with the clear intention, deduced from the tone of the words or from the form employed, of giving a definitive judgment which is irreformable and obligatory for the whole Church on doctrine concerning faith or morals.^4^[^c12-44]^4^ But the Ordinary, too, possesses an overall infallibility, and it is always authentic because it too is carried out in the name and with the authority of Christ. *Nor must it be thought that what is expounded in Encyclical Letters does not of itself demand consent, since in writing such letters the Popes do not exercise the supreme power of their Teaching Authority. For these matters are taught with the ordinary teaching authority, of which it is true to say: "he who hears you, hears me," ^4^*[^c12-45]*^5^ and generally what is expounded and inculcated in Encyclical Letters already for other reasons pertains to Catholic doctrine. But if the Supreme Pontiffs in their official documents purposely pass judgment on a matter up to that time under dispute, it is obvious that the matter, according to the mind and will of the same Pontiffs, cannot be any longer considered a question open to discussion among theologians*.^\ 4^[^c12-46]^6^

*The assent due to the Magisterium of the Church*

The assent owed to the different declarations of the Magisterium differs according to the nature of the documents in which they are contained. As the

First Vatican Council taught, *by divine and Catholic faith everything must be believed that is contained in the written word of God or in tradition, and that is proposed by the Church as a divinely revealed object of belief either in a solemn decree or in her ordinary, universal teaching*. ^4^[^c12-47]^7^ Consequently whoever pertinaciously refused to give this assent of divine faith would be a heretic. ***Our faith is divine; it is one, as God is One; and this fact entails, as a consequence, that either a person defends all its tenets, firmly and consistently, or else he will sooner or later come to renounce it. For it is certain that once a breach has been made in the walls, the whole city is in danger of being surrendered. So you will defend what the Church indicates,*** the Father goes on to tell us, ***because She is the only Teacher on these divine truths. And you will defend it with your example, with your word, with your writings, with all the upright means that are within your reach.**^4^***[^c12-48]***^8^***

The doctrinal and moral decisions of the ordinary Magisterium of the Roman Pontiff or of the Roman Congregations must be accepted not merely with an external silence, but with a *religious submission of mind and will*.^4^[^c12-49]^9^ *By this present constitution of ours which must be valid forever, with the same apostolic authority we decree, declare, establish and ordain that silent submission in no way satisfies the obedience due.^5^*[^c12-50]*^0^* So spoke Pope Clement Xl against Jansenism. The obedience required is not limited *to those matters which have been expressly defined by decrees of the Ecumenical Councils, the Roman Pontiffs and this Holy See\..., but it is also necessary to submit to the decisions concerning the doctrine of faith that come from the pontifical congregations, the same as those doctrinal points which, through the common and constant opinion of Catholics, are considered to be theological truths and conclusions which are so certain that the opinions contrary to such points of doctrine ‑ even though they may not be called heretical ‑ deserve a theological censure of another type.^5^*[^c12-51]*^1^ This religious submission of mind and will must be shown in a special way to the authentic magisterium of the Roman Pontiff, even when he is not speaking ex cathedra; that is, it must be shown in such a way that his supreme Magisterium is acknowledged with reverence, the judgments made by him are sincerely adhered to, according to his manifest mind and will. His mind and will in the matter may be known either from the character of the documents, from his frequent repetition of the same doctrine, or from his manner of speaking*.^5^[^c12-52]^2^

Together with the authentic Magisterium expressed in the encyclicals, the Church has declared in an explicit way that *absolutely everyone is obliged in conscience to submit to the dictates of the Pontifical Biblical Commission\..., and also to the Decrees of the Sacred Congregations referring to doctrinal questions and approved by the Supreme Pontiff. And those who refute these teachings in word or in writing cannot escape the note of disobedience and temerity and hence are not free from serious fault*.^5^[^c12-53]^3^ On the contrary, ***obedience to the Church and the Roman Pontiff will give us certainty and firmness on the untouchable principles of faith and morals. This docility of ours,*** the Father wrote***, is part of our common denominator. On the other hand, when it is a question of doctrine open to opinion, each one of my children can and ought to form his personal opinion. But it must be an opinion without stubbornness that can be modified when new reasons appear which change the way of posing the problem in question.* *^5^***[^c12-54]***^4^* ****The Father has also asked us to be able to distinguish ***between truth and opinion, between the firmness that must be used to defend the central truths on which the whole of human existence depends and the firmness with which it is prudent to hold judgments on more peripheral and sometimes entirely changeable matters. The authority of a dogma defined by the Church\'s Magisterium is not the same as that of an opinion defended by some theologian, or theologians. Nor can a person confuse the orthodox attitude that leads one to protect the tradition of the Church with the closed attitude of the one who refuses to accept all progress.**^5^***[^c12-55]***^5^***

In order to guard the purity of her doctrine and to regulate the exercise of her saving mission, the Church possesses a power of governing which is manifested in a juridical structure. Indeed, *law assures and provides for the ecclesial community\...the basic framework of relationships within which Christian life can flourish and develop\... It is on this foundation that charity, that is, the love of the Church, sprouts and flowers in the Church, penetrating everything like the leaven of the Gospel and gathers everything into the oneness of Christ. Finally, it is on this foundation of juridical statutes that the pastoral office acquires its force and effectiveness. Not that the pastoral office is to be regarded as a juridical relationship, nor can it be reduced to such. But pastoral activity will be carried out effectively and received with respect only to the extent that it is bolstered and supported by a sound and solid framework of law.^5^*[^c12-56]*^6^* Thus*, the Church, by divine institution, not only has the power of magisterium to teach and define matters of faith and morals and to interpret Sacred Scripture without danger of error, but she also possesses the power to govern so that she may retain in her bosom those children she has received, confirm them in the doctrine which she gave them, and promulgate laws concerning everything that refers to the salvation of souls and to the exercise of the sacred ministry and the worship of God*.^5^[^c12-57]^7^

The office of governing the faithful is of divine origin. The Magisterium has declared this on several occasions, and in an especially solemn manner in the Dogmatic Bull "Unam Sanctam", dealing with the supreme power of the Roman Pontiff: *This authority, moreover, although given to man and exercised by man, is not human, but divine\... Whoever, therefore, resists this authority thus ordered by God, resists the command of God*.^5^[^c12-58]^8^ ^5^[^c12-59]^9^ And in opposition to the errors of the Synod of Pistoia, the Church also teaches that her power to govern was not received from the community of the faithful: *The proposition stating that God has given the Church a power to be communicated to the shepherds who are her ministers for the salvation of souls, understood in the sense that the power of ministry and ecclesiastical rule is given to the shepherds by the community of the faithful, is heretical*.^6^[^c12-60]^0^

*The aim of Law in the life of the Church*

The juridical structure of the Church *which rests on the power of jurisdiction granted by Christ to the Hierarchy, tends in its entirety toward the care of souls, so that men, under the protection and the guidance of laws, may be partakers of Christ\'s truth and grace and may live, grow and die in sanctity, devotion and fidelity.^6^*[^c12-61]*^1^* *In other words, Canon Law aims at attaining that lofty purpose through the Church, whose structure and guidance, by means of proper institutions and norms, is primarily the competence of that same Canon Law*.^6^[^c12-62]^2^ *In the society of the Church, too, law cannot survive and be effective unless it is spelled out and codified in an organized way. The very nature of law calls for codified statutes; hence they must not be regarded as something alien to the Church, as some foreign plant grafted forcibly onto the Church organism. On the contrary, canon law is designed to foster an important aspect of the Church\'s mission; it is meant to preserve, protect and encourage all those common undertakings designed to make us more faithful and more persevering in the practice of Christian living.^6^*[^c12-63]*^3^* The law is so necessary that *those who are subject to the Hierarchy are obliged in conscience to submit to the law, according to those words: "he who hears you, hears me, he who rejects you, rejects me." ^6^*[^c12-64]*^4^* *The juridical precepts therefore do manifest with certainty the will of Christ, to whom, as Lord, we are subject. There is no reason for some people, in their excessive praise of liberty "to which we have been called",^6^*[^c12-65]*^5^ to say that it is opposed to law and thus offer a wrong interpretation of the Apostle Paul\'s teaching*.^6^[^c12-66]^6^ *Canon Law does not impede, restrict or diminish growth in the Christian life. With the never-failing grace of the Holy Spirit to guide it, canon law protects, fosters and promotes authentic Christian living and its future progress*.^6^[^c12-67]^7^ ***In the life of the Church, the law is something very holy, my children. It is not an empty form, nor a weapon to keep consciences in her grasp. It is a reasonable and supernatural ordering of things according to justice. It is not simply an instrument to command, but a light at the service of the entire Church to illuminate for everyone the path of fulfillment of the great commandment of Love***.^6^[^c12-68]^8^ This is why the Father has taught us to be ***faithful to the decisions of the Hierarchy of the Church, down to the smallest details, acting not just as subjects of an authority, but with the piety of children and with the affection of those who feel and who are members of the Body of Christ***.^6^[^c12-69]^9^

*The ecclesiastical hierarchy*

Not all the ministries of the Mystical Body of Christ have been created by the ecclesiastical authority*. Although it is well known, it is fitting to recall that some of the elements which constitute the Church, which is not an egalitarian society, are of divine right. Such is the case of the Primacy of the Roman Pontiff, the episcopacy and finally of the priesthood and the diaconate. And also the laity must be numbered among these elements, even though they do not possess the power to govern. On the other hand, other constituent elements of the Church are a result of human law. Such for example, are the metropolitans, the pastors and the religious.^7^*[^c12-70]*^0^* This distinction is important because *the primary things, namely those of constitutive divine law, cannot be changed in any way*.^7^[^c12-71]^1^ Moreover, this radical continuity in the structure of the Church is the best guarantee of progress, because, as our Father pointed out, **aggiornamento** ***in the Church, today as in any other period, is fundamentally a joyful reaffirmation of the People of God\'s faithfulness to the mission received, to the Gospel. This faithfulness should be alive and active in every circumstance of men\'s lives. It therefore requires opportune doctrinal developments in the exposition of the riches of the* Depositum fidei*, as can clearly be seen in the two thousand years of the Church\'s history and recently in the Second Vatican Council. It may also require suitable changes and efforts to improve, in their human and perfectible element, the organizational structures and the missionary and apostolic methods of the Church. But it would be, to say the least, superficial to think that* aggiornamento *consists primarily in change, or that all change produces* aggiornamento*. One need only consider that there are people who seek changes which go outside and against the Council\'s doctrine and would put the progressive movement of the People of God back several centuries in history, back at least to feudal times***.^7^[^c12-72]^2^

The Pope enjoys supreme and full power over the universal Church. *This has always been the common and firm opinion of all Catholics; and it is a dogma of faith that the Roman Pontiff, successor to Blessed Peter, Prince of the Apostles, has a Primacy in the whole Church, not only of honor, but also of authority and jurisdiction, and hence the bishops themselves are subject to him*.^7^[^c12-73]^3^ His authority extends in an immediate way to each and every diocese, to each and every Christian, without in any way being opposed *to the power of ordinary and immediate episcopal jurisdiction by which the bishops who, under appointment of the Holy Spirit^7^*[^c12-74]*^4^ succeeded in the place of the Apostles, feed and rule individually the particular flock assigned to them as true shepherds*.^7^[^c12-75]^5^ Therefore the power that the bishops exercise in their diocese, *personally in Christ's name, is proper, ordinary and immediate, although its exercise is ultimately regulated by the supreme authority of the Church and can be circumscribed by certain limits, for the advantage of the Church or of the faithful.^7^*[^c12-76]*^6^*

With the Pope and under his supreme authority, the College of Bishops also exercise the universal power of government. *Just as in the Gospel, the Lord so disposing, St. Peter and the other apostles constitute one apostolic college, so in a similar way the Roman Pontiff, the successor of Peter, and the bishops, the successors of the apostles, are joined together\... But the college or body of bishops has no authority unless it is understood together with the Roman Pontiff, the successor of Peter, as its head. The Pope\'s power of primacy over all, both pastors and faithful, remains whole and intact\... This power can be exercised only with the consent of the Roman Pontiff*.^7^[^c12-77]^7^

*Scope of the power of jurisdiction*

This power of government, independent of the civil power,^7^[^c12-78]^8^ is exercised absolutely and directly over strictly religious and spiritual matters. *Whatever, therefore, in things human is of a sacred character, whatever belongs either of its own nature or by reason of the end to which it is referred, to the salvation of souls, or to the worship of God, is subject to the power and judgement of the Church.^7^*[^c12-79]*^9^ Christ, to be sure, gave his Church no proper mission in the political, economic or social order. The purpose which He set before her is a religious one. But out of this religious mission itself come a function, a light and an energy which can serve to structure and consolidate the human community according to the divine law,^8^*[^c12-80]*^0^* without the Church thereby becoming involved in purely temporal affairs, left by the Creator to the free decision of men. Therefore, as long as the essential principles of the natural and divine orders are respected, the Church does not intervene or concern herself at all *to decide which is the best among many diverse forms of government and the civil institutions of Christian States; and amid the various kinds of State rule she does not disapprove of any, provided the respect due to religion and the observance of good morals be upheld\... There is no doubt but that in the sphere of politics ample matter may exist for legitimate difference of opinion, and that, the single reserve being made of the rights of justice and truth, all may strive to bring into actual working the ideas believed likely to be more conducive than others to the general welfare. But to attempt to involve the Church in party strife, and seek to bring her support to bear against those who take opposite views, is only worthy of partisans*.^8^[^c12-81]^1^ If necessary, as our Father says, ***only the ordinary ecclesiastical hierarchy has the right and the duty to give Catholics political orientations, and make them see the necessity* *‑* *where they judge that there indeed is such a necessity* *‑* *of taking a particular position in problems of public life. And when the Hierarchy intervenes in this way, it is by no means clericalism. Every well-formed Catholic ought to know that it belongs to the pastoral mission of the bishops to give criteria in public affairs when the good of the Church requires it. And well-formed Catholics also know that such intervention pertains by divine right solely to the bishops; for only they, being in union with the Roman Pontiff, have the public function of governing in the Church***.^8^[^c12-82]^2^

The field which the Church has left open to the free discussion of men is broad; and broad, too, is the freedom to act in one way or another in the fabric of Christ\'s Mystical Body. *But there are two matters beyond agreement: the truth of the Faith, authoritatively sanctioned by Tradition and the ecclesiastical Magisterium, and the constitutional law of the Church. Obedience must be given to the ministry of pastoral government that Christ established and that the wisdom of the Church has developed and extended in the various members of the mystical and visible body of the Church, to guide and strengthen the many component parts that make up the People of God*.^8^[^c12-83]^3^

The tasks of magisterium and government in the Church *are intended to lead men to that true heavenly and eternal happiness for which we were created*.^8^[^c12-84]^4^ This is why they are exercised with a spirit of service in imitation of Jesus Christ who *did not come to be served but to serve*. ^8^[^c12-85]^5^ In the unity of fraternal love everyone in the Church ‑ subjects and rulers, laymen, religious and clergy ‑ in fulfilling the mission entrusted to each of them, help to build up the Body of Christ, *so that the entire Church, strengthened by all her members, may with greater efficacy fulfill her mission for the life of the world*.^8^[^c12-86]^6^ The Father reminds us that ***in any case, you must not forget that the mission of service falls especially on those who have authority in the Church. And the higher up they are, the greater is the scope of their responsibility and the greater their obligation of sacrifice. It is hard to bear on earth the weight of the Church and the burden of seeing to her continual growth. I repeat this to you so that the love which the spirit of the Work makes you feel for those who bear this mission on their shoulders will increase every day; and so that you act in such a way that they can always count on the help of your unity and the support of your apostolic work among your fellow citizens in the middle of the world***.^8^[^c12-87]^7^

## THE ROMAN PONTIFF {#cuaderno.1.13}

Christ the Lord established his Holy Church on earth as *a sign and instrument of communion with God and of unity among all men*.[^c13-1] To maintain the unity of this People whom he had acquired with his Blood, he decided to give his Church *a supreme authority to which all Christians must render obedience*.[^c13-2] While living on earth, he himself presided over the Church. After ascending into glory, *though invisible he continues, until the end of time, to govern and guard his Church from heaven. But since he willed that his kingdom should be visible, he was obliged to designate a Vicar on earth*.[^c13-3]

*The Primacy of Peter*

From the initial pages of the Gospel, the divine intention to establish a primacy in the Church becomes clear. St John narrates that when He met Simon Peter, *Jesus looked at him and said, So you are Simon, the son of John*. You shall be called Cephas (which means Peter).[^c13-4] This was the first announcement of the primacy. Christ would confirm it months later on the road to Caesarea. The divine grace of the Holy Spirit had moved Peter profoundly to make a crystal clear confession of Christ's divinity: *You are the Christ, the Son of the living God. And Jesus answered him, Blessed are you, Simon Bar-Jona: for flesh and blood has not revealed this to you, but My Father who is in heaven. And I tell you, you are Peter (Cephas), and on this rock I will build my Church, and the gates of hell shall not prevail against it. I will give you the keys of the kingdom of heaven and whatever you bind on earth shall be bound in heaven, and whatever you loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven.*[^c13-5]

Under the veil of a triple metaphor expressed in these words, our Lord promised Peter supreme authority over the Church. As his new name, *Cephas*, indicated, Simon was destined to be a *rock* --- the rock that would support the edifice of the Church. *What did Jesus mean by this word? What does the changing of his name mean? Christ referred to the firmness, the stability, the permanence\... which the faith of Simon Peter was supposed to have, not only in relation to his own personal destiny, but also in relation to the whole Church, which Christ adds that he wished to build upon this rock. And so Peter's position in the Church is that of the rock upon which a building is constructed.*[^c13-6] *And the rain fell, and the floods came, and the winds blew and beat upon that house, but it did not fall, because it had been built on rock*.[^c13-7]

*The promise is carried out when Christ the Lord, after his Resurrection, having thrice asked Peter whether he loved him more than the rest, gave him this injunction: "Feed my lambs\... feed my sheep"*.[^c13-8] [^c13-9] *In this manner Christ proclaimed Peter the continuation of himself, his vicar in the pastoral mission which he himself had indicated to be his most characteristic and beloved mission: "I am the Good Shepherd"*.[^c13-10] *Peter's primacy of leading and serving the Christian people was to be a pastoral primacy, a primacy of love. The nature and the authority of the pastoral function of the apostolic primacy was to have its foundations in the undying love of Peter for Jesus*.[^c13-11]

The Church accepted this primacy from the beginning as willed by God. The Prince of the Apostles is always listed first among them,[^c13-12] and he makes frequent use of a special authority over the other Apostles. It is he who proposes the election of a new apostle to take the place of Judas.[^c13-13] He takes the initiative on Pentecost and converts the first Christians.[^c13-14] He replies before the Sanhedrin in the name of everyone.[^c13-15] He chastises Ananias and Sapphira with full authority.[^c13-16] He admits Cornelius, the first gentile, into the Church.[^c13-17] He presides over the Council of Jerusalem and rejects the aims of the Jewish proselytes regarding circumcision, thereby re-affirming the principle that salvation can only be reached through Jesus Christ.[^c13-18] *In open opposition to this very clear teaching of Sacred Scripture, as it has always been understood by the Catholic Church, are the perverse opinions of those who wrongly explain the form of government established by Christ in his Church, either by denying that Peter alone, in preference to the other apostles, whether singly or as a group, was endowed by Christ with true and proper primacy of jurisdiction, or by claiming that this same primacy was not given immediately and directly to blessed Peter, but to the Church and through the Church to Peter as to an agent of the Church*.[^c13-19]

Down the centuries this doctrine has been expressed by the Magisterium in different ways. The Church affirms that Peter alone is the Prince of the Apostles to whom all are to be subject.[^c13-20] He is the only Vicar of Jesus Christ on earth,[^c13-21] the rock upon which God willed to build the Church,[^c13-22] the Head and Principle of unity,[^c13-23] and the source *from whom have come this episcopate and all the authority belonging to this name*.[^c13-24] The First Vatican Council formulated the prerogatives of the Apostle Simon Peter, and solemnly stated them as revealed truth: *If anyone says that the blessed Apostle was not constituted by Christ, the Lord, as the Prince of all the Apostles and the visible head of the whole Church militant, or that he received immediately and directly from Jesus Christ our Lord only a primacy of honour and not a true and proper primacy of jurisdiction: let him be anathema*.[^c13-25]

*The Roman Pontiff, successor to St Peter*

The Apostle Peter, like all men, would have to die. Jesus himself told him how he would die when He bestowed on him primacy over all of the Church. *Truly, truly, I say to you, when you were young, you girded yourself and walked where you would, but when you are old you will stretch out your hands and another will gird you and lead you where you would not wish to go*. This he said, St John comments, *to show by what death he was to glorify God*.[^c13-26] Yet his role as supreme shepherd, instituted by Christ *for the perpetual safety and everlasting good of the Church, must, by the Will of Christ, endure without interruption in the Church which was founded on the rock and will remain firm until the end of time*.[^c13-27] For Christ's words to come true, the Prince of the Apostles would have to have successors to exercise, in his name and with his authority, the supreme ecclesiastical ministry until the end of time. In 431 it was affirmed by the Papal Legate to the Council of Ephesus, as doctrine already well known, with approval of all those attending the great assembly: *No one doubts, in fact it is obvious to all ages, that the holy and most blessed Peter, head and Prince of the Apostles, the Pillar of Faith, and the foundation of the Catholic Church, received the keys of the Kingdom from our Lord Jesus Christ, the Saviour and the Redeemer of the human race. Nor does anyone doubt that the power of forgiving and retaining sins was also given to this same Peter who, in his successors, lives and exercises judgement even to this time and forever*.[^c13-28] Tradition in the Church is unanimous in affirming that this succession is found only in *the bishops of the holy Roman See, which he established and consecrated with his blood. Therefore, whoever succeeds Peter in his Chair holds Peter's primacy over the whole Church according to the plan of Christ himself*.[^c13-29]

This has always been the Catholic faith, manifested on numerous occasions through the Church's history. In the Council of Chalcedon in 451 the oriental bishops unanimously embraced the dogmatic declaration of Pope St Leo I regarding the two natures of Christ, which acknowledges that the Apostle Peter spoke as Pope. The same testimony is given in the Third Council of Constantinople in 681 when Pope Agathonus intervened to explain the correct doctrine against Monotheism. And in the Fourth Ecumenical Council held in the same city, the Fathers of the East and West unanimously professed, with the same words used three centuries before by Pope St Hormisdas,[^c13-30] that the Apostolic See had preserved intact the deposit of Revelation and they affirmed their will to remain in communion with her. Later on, the Second Council of Lyon, the Council of Florence, and the First Vatican Council solemnly defined this truth: *All the faithful of Christ must believe that "the holy Apostolic See and the Roman Pontiff have the primacy over the whole world, and that the same Roman Pontiff is the successor of St Peter, the Prince of the Apostles, and the true Vicar of Christ, the head of the whole Church, the father and teacher of all Christians; and that to him, in the person of St Peter, was given by our Lord Jesus Christ the full power of feeding, ruling, and governing the whole Church; as is also contained in the proceedings of the ecumenical councils and in the sacred canons"*.[^c13-31] For this reason, ***our greatest love, our highest esteem, our deepest veneration, our fullest obedience, and our greatest affection must also be for the Vice-God on earth, the Pope. Always remember that in the hierarchy of love and authority, after God, and our Mother the Most Holy Virgin, comes the Pope. That is why I often say:*** **I thank you, my God, for the love of the Pope which you have put in my heart.**[^c13-32]

As a rebuttal of the errors of those who deny that the Roman Primacy was of divine institution, Catholic Faith teaches that it was not instituted because of merely political circumstances,[^c13-33] nor does it derive its authority from the Councils or by concession on the part of the Church,[^c13-34] but rather that Christ instituted it himself. This is the way the Church of Rome has understood this supremacy over all other episcopal sees and she has exercised this supremacy on innumerable occasions from the very beginning.[^c13-35]^\ ^As the First Vatican Council put it, *We teach and declare that, by the Lord's will, the Roman Church holds the pre-eminence of ordinary power over all the other churches. This power of jurisdiction of the Roman Pontiff is truly episcopal. It is immediate. Regarding this jurisdiction, the shepherds of whatever rite and dignity, and also the faithful, individually and collectively, are bound by a duty of hierarchical subjection and of sincere obedience. This not only in matters that pertain to faith and morals, but also in matters that pertain to the discipline and government of the Church throughout the whole world. Whilst ever this bond of unity with the Roman Pontiff is guarded both in government as well as in the profession of the same faith, then the Church of Christ will be one flock under one Supreme Shepherd. This is the doctrine of Catholic truth; and no one can deviate from this without losing his faith and his salvation*.[^c13-36]^\ ^

The power of the Roman Pontiff is superior to that of Councils, Patriarchs,[^c13-37] and bishops, both individually and collectively in the episcopal college, of which the Successor of Peter is the head;[^c13-38] and it extends over all the baptised. ***My sons and daughters,*** our Father insisted, ***do not tire of preaching the love and obedience owed to the Holy Father. Even if his office had not been instituted by Jesus Christ, reason tells me that a strong central authority, the Holy See, is necessary to bring to their senses those people who, within the Church, cannot reconcile themselves with her and who do absurd things. But what is more important and takes precedence over these logical reasons is the will of God, who wants a Vicar on earth and infallibly assists him with his Spirit.***[^c13-39]

*The power of the Magisterium*

In addition to the Supreme Pontificate, the office of the Primate encompasses two more functions that were given personally by Jesus Christ to Peter: the supreme magisterium and supreme government of the Church.

On the road to Caesarea our Lord had promised to build the Church upon Simon and thus make it indestructible: *But, since all Christians must be united in sharing one unchanging Faith, our Lord Jesus Christ, by the power of his prayer obtained for Peter that in the fulfilment of his office he should never stray from the faith*.[^c13-40] And so during the Last Supper Jesus, foreseeing the weakness of his disciples who were present and of those who were to come, promised indefectibility in matters of Faith to Peter and, in him, to his successors. He would have the express task of being Master and mainstay for the others. *Simon, Simon, behold, Satan demanded to have you, that he might sift you like wheat, but I have prayed for you that your faith may not fail; and when you have turned again, that you strengthen your brethren*.[^c13-41] By virtue of this divine promise, the Roman Pontiff enjoys the privilege of infallibility when he speaks *ex cathedra* that is, *when, acting in the office of Shepherd and Teacher of all Christians, he defines, by virtue of his Supreme Apostolic Authority, doctrine concerning faith or morals to be held by the Universal Church*.[^c13-42] It is a duty of all the faithful to accept this doctrine in all its purity, integrity, and authentic meaning. Otherwise, ***it would not be a treasure of divine truths,*** our Father wrote, ***but rather something human, that neither saves nor redeems: salt that has become insipid. This catastrophe results from the insanity of giving in on principles, the readiness to diminish doctrinal differences, and make concessions in what belongs to the untouchable deposit of the faith***.[^c13-43]

The assent owed to the teaching authority of the Roman Pontiff is not limited to truths solemnly defined *ex cathedra*, but also extends to all acts of his ordinary magisterium. *Nor must it be thought that what is expounded in Encyclical Letters does not of itself demand consent since in writing such Letters the Popes do not exercise the supreme power of their teaching authority. On the contrary, these matters are taught with the ordinary teaching authority, of which it is true to say: "He who hears you, hears me."*[^c13-44] [^c13-45] Our Father always instilled in us this filial disposition of obedience to the Roman Pontiff: ***We accept everything the Church accepts in faith, in morals, and in discipline. And we reject whatever the Church rejects, without any limitations.***[^c13-46]

Therefore, although each Catholic is very free to form his personal criteria in matters left to the free discussion of theologians, when the Holy Father expresses his will on a certain point, his word should be received, respected, and adhered to, internally and externally. ***In theology we accept everything the Church proposes as being of faith,*** our Father wrote. ***And with the same submission we are ready to accept, as well, everything else that the Roman Pontiffs decide, even if people who are ignorant of, or scorn the Magisterium of the Church do not understand our faithfulness. For there are some points of doctrine which, even if they have not been solemnly defined by the Church, have been fixed by the ordinary magisterium of the Roman Pontiff. So these matters are no longer open to discussion. To refuse to accept them, furthermore, would be foolhardy, as the Holy Father Pius XII says:*** **It is true that popes generally leave theologians free in those matters which are disputed in various ways by men of very high authority in this field; but history teaches that many matters that formerly were open to discussion, now no longer admit of discussion\... And if the Supreme Pontiffs in their official documents purposely pass judgement on a matter, up to that time under dispute, it is obvious that the matter according to the mind and will of the same Pontiffs, cannot be any longer considered a question open to discussion among theologians.** ***(Pius XII, Encyclical Letter,*** **Humani generis*, 8 December 1950, AAS 42 (1950), p. 568)***[^c13-47]

*The power of jurisdiction*

Our Lord's words to Peter on the way to Caesarea, along with the promise to make him the firm rock of the Church, also include the promise to invest Supreme Authority in him. *Everyone knows that keys are the usual sign of governing authority. Hence when Christ promised to give Peter the keys of the kingdom of heaven, he promised to give him power and authority over the Church\... In this same sense he said: "Whatever you bind on earth shall be bound in heaven, and whatever you shall loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven." This metaphorical expression of binding and loosing indicates the power of making laws, or judging and of punishing\... of such extent and force that God will ratify whatever is decreed by Peter. Therefore it is supreme and absolutely independent, so that, having no other power on earth as its superior, it embraces the whole Church and all things entrusted to the Church*.[^c13-48] By virtue of this power, the Roman Pontiff has at his command the spiritual treasure of the Church, earned by the merits of Christ, the most holy Virgin Mary and the saints;[^c13-49]^,\ 1\ January\ 1967\ ^he convokes, he presides, and he dissolves ecumenical councils;[^c13-50] he institutes, he changes, and he removes bishops for just reasons,[^c13-51] without putting in jeopardy his own episcopal authority, received directly from God in his consecration. The Second Vatican Council teaches that *episcopal consecration confers, together with the office of sanctifying, also the duties of teaching and ruling which, however, of their very nature can be exercised only in hierarchical communion with the head and members of the college*.[^c13-52] For the bishops to be able to exercise their ministry as Teachers and Shepherds, they need a canonical mission which *can be made by legitimate customs that have not been revoked by the supreme and universal authority of the Church, or by laws made or acknowledged by the same authority, or directly by Peter's successor himself. Should he object or refuse apostolic communion, then a bishop cannot be admitted to office*.[^c13-53]

In as much as he is supreme legislator, *only the Roman Pontiff can establish general laws*.[^c13-54] The Pope is also the supreme Judge in the universal Church, as Pope Julius I already brought to mind in the fourth century: *Did you not know that the custom is this: to write to Us first, and thus from here justice would be determined?*[^c13-55] His judgement, moreover, is not subject to appeal, as the First Vatican Council teaches in recalling the unanimous tradition of the Church: *We also teach and declare that he is the supreme judge of the faithful; and that one can have recourse to his judgement in all cases pertaining to ecclesiastical jurisdiction. We declare that the judgement of the Apostolic See, whose authority is unsurpassed, is not subject to review by anyone; nor is anyone allowed to pass judgement on its decision*.[^c13-56] As of right, the Roman Pontiff can only be judged by God.[^c13-57]

Ordinarily, the judicial decisions of the Church in the sacramental and extra-sacramental fora are made by bishops invested with the legitimate canonical mission and in communion with the Holy See. Nevertheless, *by reason of the supreme power that was entrusted to them in the Church, Sovereign Pontiffs could reserve to their own special judgement some of the more serious cases of sins\... It is in accord with divine authority that this reservation of sins is valid not only in external discipline but also before God. However, this same Church of God has always devoutly upheld that there is no reservation at the hour of death, lest this reservation be the occasion of anyone's damnation; and therefore in those circumstances any priest can absolve any penitent from all sins and censures*.[^c13-58]

*Faithfulness to the Roman Pontiff*

***Remain very close to the Roman Pontiff,*** **il dolce Cristo in terra. *Follow his teachings as they come out, meditate on them in your prayer, and defend them with your word and your pen.***[^c13-59] Our Father asks all his children, especially those working in theology, to ***defend the authority of the Roman Pontiff against every possible attack, for no one but God can limit his authority. In their research these children of mine should always be ready to accept from the very first moment, devoutly, promptly and joyfully, whatever the Church establishes in the future with the intention of defining some doctrine.***[^c13-60]

This filial and complete adherence to the person of the Pope is perhaps more necessary today than ever. ***My sons, since our Lord Jesus Christ founded the Church, this Mother of ours has been under continuous persecution. I dare say that in other times the persecutions were carried out openly; today they are often perpetrated under cover: but today, like yesterday, the Church is under attack. We are living in times of great bewilderment for souls. The voice of confusion is heard on all sides, and with it is revived every heresy that has ever existed in the Church**'**s history. This is a time similar to Luther**'**s. My children, you must not forget***, our Father pointed out, ***that an infamous propaganda campaign is being carried out against the holiness of matrimony, against the sanctity of the priesthood, against chastity and virginity, against the Mother of God and our Mother, against the Roman Pontiff, and against the Holy Eucharist***.[^c13-61] In these circumstances loyalty to the Pope is needed more urgently than ever. He has, on earth, the keys of the kingdom of heaven: *He opens and no one shall shut, he shuts and no one opens*.[^c13-62] The doctrine of Faith regarding the primacy and infallibility of the Roman Pontiff therefore acquires a fundamental importance which is decisive and for the good of the whole Church.

*Consequences of this doctrine*

Firstly, this doctrine is the root of unity in the Church and forms its strongest bulwark. The Second Vatican Council states that *the Roman Pontiff, as the Successor of Peter, is the perpetual and visible source and foundation of the unity both of Bishops and of the whole company of the faithful*.[^c13-63] To cut oneself off from the Roman See consciously is to cut oneself off from Christ, with the very same consequences. ***Do you not see that those who separate themselves from the Church, even if before they had borne much fruit, quickly dry up and their fruit turns into a seething pile of worms. Love the Holy Church, Apostolic, Roman, One!***[^c13-64] *If a man is baptised in the Catholic faith and later leaves the Church --- the Roman Church, the one Catholic Church --- and remains stubbornly separated from the Faith, he is a schismatic or a heretic*.[^c13-65] *No one can reach his final salvation outside of the faith of the Church and obedience to the Roman Pontiffs*.[^c13-66]

One more consequence of the doctrine regarding the Primacy is the right of the Roman Pontiff *to communicate freely with the shepherds and flocks of the whole Church in the exercise of his office, so that they can be instructed and guided by him in the way of salvation. Hence, We condemn and disapprove the opinions of those who say that it can be licit to hinder the communication of the Supreme Head with the shepherds and flocks; or whose who make this communication subject to the secular power in such a way that they claim whatever is decreed for the government of the Church by the Apostolic See or on its authority is not binding unless it has been confirmed by the "placet" of the secular power*.[^c13-67]

The Catholic faith in the primacy and infallibility of the Roman Pontiff, so clearly affirmed by Sacred Scripture, so ardently defended by the Church for twenty centuries, and so solemnly expressed in dogmatic formulas by the First Vatican Council, has been reaffirmed once more in the Second Vatican Council: *This teaching concerning the institution, the permanence, the nature and import of the sacred primacy of the Roman Pontiff and his infallible teaching office, the sacred synod proposes once again to the firm belief of all the faithful*.[^c13-68] Upon it rests the structure of the entire Church, against which the forces of the devil and of death can do nothing. Confusion may reign at times in some circles, but this should not sadden us. ***Rather it should stimulate us to be more faithful each day in order to be more effective and to love the Church and its visible Head more and more.*** **Ubi Petrus, ibi Ecclesia, ibi Deus. *We want to be with Peter, because with him is the Church; with him is God; and without him God is not present. That is why I have wanted to make the Work* Roman*. Love the Holy Father very much. Pray a lot for the Pope. Love him very much. Love him very much! Because he needs all the love of his children. And this I understand very well: I know it from experience, because I am not like a wall. I am a man of flesh. Therefore I like the Pope to know that we love him, that we will always love him, and for only one reason: he is the sweet Christ on earth.***

***When you are old, and I have rendered an account to God, you will tell your brothers how the Father loved the Pope with his whole soul, with all his strength.***[^c13-69]

## THE BLESSED VIRGIN (I) {#cuaderno.1.14}

The history of salvation tells of God’s encounter with men, foretold by prophets, achieved in Christ and made present throughout all time by the Church. This long journey of humanity, which will reach its goal when Christ depicts the entire creation in himself and God may be all things to all men,[^c14-1] has been traversed in its fullness by but one creature: Mary Most Holy, Mother of God and our Mother. The Second Vatican Council teaches: But while, in the Blessed Virgin, the Church has already reached that perfection whereby she exists without spot or wrinkle,[^c14-2] the faithful still strive to increase in holiness, vanquishing sin completely, and for that reason they raise their eyes to Mary who shines forth to the whole community of the elect as the model of virtues.[^c14-3] Therefore, it is good to call to mind the Church’s teaching on Mary, who unites in her person and re-echoes the most important doctrines of the Faith.[^c14-4]

The divine Maternity of Mary

The Lord created me at the beginning of his work, the first of his acts of old. Ages ago I was set up, at the first, before the beginning of the earth. When there were no depths I was brought forth.[^c14-5] Although the sacred writer is speaking here of God’s uncreated Wisdom, the Church’s liturgy has wanted to apply these inspired words to Mary, chosen from all eternity to be the Mother of God. Having foreseen from all eternity the lamentable fall of the entire human race which would result from the sin of Adam, the Lord decreed by a plan, hidden since before time, to complete the first work of his Mercy; with a mystery ever more inestimable... He chose and prepared a Mother for his only-begotten Son to take flesh from her and from whom, in the blessed fullness of time, he would be born; and so much did he love her above all creatures that only in her was the Father pleased with singular delight.[^c14-6]

The divine maternity is the central event that fills Mary’s life with light, and it explains the countless privileges with which God wished to adorn her.[^c14-7] It is a truth that has been professed by Christians since the Church’s beginnings. When Nestorius, at the beginning of the fifth century, denied Mary the title of theotokos – Mother of God – the entire Christian world rose in defence of the Catholic truth. And the Third Ecumenical Council, meeting in Ephesus, solemnly proclaimed: If anyone denies that God is truly the Emmanuel, and therefore that the holy Virgin is the Mother of God, since she gave birth to the Word of God made flesh, let him be anathema.[^c14-8]

The dogma of Mary’s divine maternity follows from the dogma of the hypostatic union. The Church has always professed, and solemnly defined in several ecumenical councils, that Jesus Christ was not an ordinary man, who was first born of the holy Virgin, and upon whom the Word later descended.[^c14-9] Nor did he take on a heavenly body that passed through the Virgin’s womb like water through an aqueduct,[^c14-10] as the Gnostics thought. Rather, the Virgin gave birth to the Sacred Body, informed by a rational Soul, which was united hypostatically to the Word of God.[^c14-11] And, indeed, if the Son of the Blessed Virgin Mary is God, assuredly She who bore him is rightly and deservedly to be called the Mother of God. If the Person of Christ is one only and Divine, without any doubt, Mary ought to be called by all, not the only Mother of Christ Man, but “Deipara” or “Theotokos”, that is, Mother of God... This truth, handed down from the earliest times of the Church, cannot be rejected on the pretext that the Blessed Virgin Mary did, indeed, supply the body of Jesus Christ, but did not produce the Word of the Heavenly Father; since, as Cyril already rightly and lucidly answered in his time, in the same way as those mothers in whose womb our earthly body, but not our rational soul, is engendered, are rightly and truly called our mothers; even so She, by reason of the unity of the Person of her Son, is truly the Mother of God.[^c14-12]

The Immaculate Conception

To prepare a worthy dwelling for his Son, God wanted his Mother to be immaculate, most holy, free of all guilt and sin. She received this singular privilege from God, never granted to anyone else, because She had been raised to the dignity of his Mother. Well then, this sublime privilege, declared and approved solemnly at the Council of Ephesus against the heresy of Nestorius, greater than which no other can possibly exist, demands fullness of divine grace and complete immunity from sin since it required the greatest dignity and holiness, after that of Christ.[^c14-13]

Christians have, since ancient times, held to the truth of Mary’s Immaculate Conception. And the Church considers that, in Sacred Scripture, one finds the foundation of this doctrine: when, after the sad fall of Adam, God, Creator of all things, addressed the serpent, the tempter and corrupter, in these words, which not a few Holy Fathers and Doctors have applied to the most holy Virgin, as have very many approved interpreters: “I will put enmity between thee and the woman, and thy seed and her seed”.[^c14-14] Now, if the most Holy Virgin Mary, by virtue of being stained by original sin, had been deprived of divine grace for even a moment, in that space of time, however minute, there would not have prevailed between her and the serpent that everlasting enmity spoken of by the earliest tradition up to the solemn definition of the Immaculate Conception, but rather a certain subjection. Moreover, by greeting the most Holy Virgin as “full of grace”[^c14-15] and “blessed amongst women”,[^c14-16] by these words, as Catholic Tradition understands them, it is indicated that “the Virgin was the abode of all divine graces and adorned with the gifts of the Holy Spirit, to such a degree that she never had been subject to the accursed one”.[^c14-17] [^c14-18]

Right from the first centuries, the liturgy took up this truth, common patrimony of the Christian people, and began to celebrate the feast of the Immaculate Conception of Mary. Theologians only began to discuss this topic in the age of scholasticism, until Pope Sixtus IV intervened and approved the solemn and public celebration of the feast.[^c14-19] Soon afterwards, he once again spoke out against those who claimed it was heretical and sinful to celebrate the office of the Immaculate Conception and attend sermons where it is affirmed that she was conceived without stain.[^c14-20] A century later, the Council of Trent, when explaining Catholic doctrine on original sin, declared: this Holy Synod declares that it is not its intention to include the Blessed and Immaculate Virgin Mary, Mother of God, in this decree, which deals with original sin.[^c14-21] Afterwards, the supreme Magisterium of the Church continued to encourage the solemn celebration of the feast of Mary Immaculate, and prohibited any attack, whether in public or private, on this doctrine.[^c14-22] It is repugnant to the mind of the Christian people even to think that the holy, undefiled, and innocent flesh of Christ could have been assumed in the womb of the Virgin from flesh which even for a slightest moment had been in any way stained.[^c14-23]

God, Omnipotent, All-powerful, Most Wise, had to choose his Mother. What would you have done if you had to choose her? I think you and I would have chosen the mother we have, adorning her with every grace. That is what God did. Therefore, after the Blessed Trinity, comes Mary. Theologians have frequently come up with logical reasoning to explain the abundant graces showered upon Mary, and the fact of her not being subject to satan: It was fitting; God could do so; therefore he did. It is a great proof. This is the clearest proof that the Lord granted his Mother, from the very moment of her Immaculate Conception, all possible privileges. And thus she is beautiful, spotless and pure in soul and body. This is what Pius IX declared when he solemnly proclaimed: The doctrine which holds that the most Blessed Virgin Mary at the first instant of her conception, by a singular privilege of Almighty God, in virtue of the merits of Christ Jesus, the Saviour of the human race, was preserved free from all stain of original sin, has been revealed by God, and on this account must be firmly and constantly believed by all the faithful.[^c14-24]

This truth of faith does not detract from the universality of Christ’s Redemption, since Christ the Lord in a certain most perfect manner really redeemed his Mother, since it was by virtue of his merits that She was preserved by God immune from all stain of original sin. Wherefore the infinite dignity of Jesus Christ and the universality of his Redemption are not diminished or lowered by this doctrine; rather they are greatly augmented.[^c14-25]

Along with the Blessed Virgin’s immunity from original sin, the Church teaches that Mary Most Holy was always free from concupiscence[^c14-26] and any personal sin,[^c14-27] and that She possessed a special privilege to avoid all venial sin.[^c14-28] Only of Mary can it truly be said: You are all fair, My Love; in You no flaw.[^c14-29] All goodness, all beauty, all majesty, all loveliness, all grace adorn our Mother. Do you not fall in love when you contemplate a Mother like her?

Mary’s perpetual virginity

The Incarnation of the Word, on which man’s salvation depended, came about with the free consent of Mary most holy, who on hearing the message of the angel, exclaimed: be it done unto me according to thy word.[^c14-30] She, who was to be Mother of God, asked Gabriel but one question: How can this be, since I know not man?[^c14-31] The Archangel’s reply expressed clearly that Mary’s virginity would be respected by God.[^c14-32] Right from the beginning of the Church, all the Creeds of faith are unanimous in acknowledging this truth.[^c14-33]

The dogma of Mary’s virginity comprises three aspects intimately united amongst themselves: for the Mother of God always preserved her virginity, before giving birth, while giving birth, and perpetually thereafter.[^c14-34] Catholic faith holds that our Lady conceived Christ by the power of the Holy Spirit, and not through man’s action. In this way the prophecy of Isaiah was fulfilled: a Virgin shall conceive and bear a Son, and shall call his name Emmanuel.[^c14-35] The Church has always taught this truth clearly. An early council affirmed that Mary, preserving intact her virginity, and without knowing man, provided the Word with flesh, impregnated by the Holy Spirit.[^c14-36]

The second aspect of this dogma is the virginity of our Lady while giving birth, which should be understood in the sense that, through a special divine intervention, she preserved intact her bodily virginity when giving birth to Jesus. The Son of God was born opening his mother’s womb, but without diminishing her virginity, thanks to the power of his Divinity.[^c14-37] The Fathers of the Church unanimously uphold this truth, and already St Ignatius of Antioch, a direct disciple of St John, affirmed that the prince of this world was kept in ignorance regarding Mary’s virginity and her giving birth,[^c14-38] because it was a mystery that could only be contemplated by God and his angels. Pope St Leo explained: such a birth was the most fitting for the power of God and his Wisdom, who is Christ, so that he might become similar to ourselves in his Humanity, and we might gain through his Divinity.[^c14-39]

The third aspect of the dogma of Mary’s virginity involves believing that She always remained a virgin after giving birth, so that Jesus was her only child. Christians have always held in horror that the same virginal womb from which Christ was born in his flesh could have given birth again.[^c14-40] Thus the Fathers applied mystically to Mary the scriptural expressions enclosed garden and sealed fountain.[^c14-41] They also applied the vision of the temple narrated by the prophet Ezekiel: Then he brought me back to the outer gate of the sanctuary, which faces east; and it was shut. And he said to me, “This gate shall remain shut; it shall not be opened, and no one shall enter by it; for the Lord, the God of Israel, has entered by it.”[^c14-42]

Mary Corredemptrix

The ordinary and universal Magisterium of the Church sees the Virgin Mary as associated with Christ’s redemption of the human race: so that just as a woman had a share in bringing about death, so also a woman should contribute to life... For, as St Ireneus says, She “being obedient, became a cause of salvation for herself and for the whole human race.”[^c14-43] Hence not a few of the early Fathers gladly assert with him in their preaching: “the knot of Eve’s disobedience was untied by Mary’s obedience; what the virgin Eve bound through her disbelief, Mary loosened by her faith”.[^c14-44] Comparing Mary with Eve, they call her “Mother of the living,”[^c14-45] and frequently claim: “death through Eve, life through Mary”.[^c14-46] [^c14-47] Thus in the divine plan, Mary is mediatrix between men and God.

In the words of the Apostle, there is but one mediator: for there is but one God and one mediator between God and men, the Man Christ Jesus, who gave himself as ransom for all.[^c14-48] But Mary’s function as mother of men in no way obscures or diminishes this unique mediation of Christ, but rather shows its power. The Blessed Virgin’s salutary influence on men arises not from any inescapable necessity but from divine approval and the superabundance of the merits of Christ. It is founded on his mediation, depends entirely on it and draws all its power from it. It does not hinder in any way the immediate union of the faithful with Christ but on the contrary fosters it.[^c14-49]

If, as the Angelic Doctor teaches, “there is no reason why others should not be called mediators between God and man, in as much as they cooperate by predisposing and by being instruments of the union of man with God”.[^c14-50] Such are the angels and saints, the prophets and priests of both Testaments; but the Blessed Virgin has a special claim to the glory of this title. For no single individual can even be imagined who has ever contributed or ever will contribute so much toward reconciling man with God... She is from whom Jesus is born; She is therefore truly his Mother and for this reason a worthy and acceptable Mediatrix to the Mediator.[^c14-51]

Mary’s co-redemptive mission began when, in response to the archangel Gabriel’s message, as representative of all human nature, she gave her consent to a spiritual matrimony between the Son of God and human nature.[^c14-52] From that moment on, she devoted herself totally, as a handmaid of the Lord, to the person and work of her Son, under and with him, serving the mystery of the redemption, by the grace of Almighty God.[^c14-53] All of her action took on a co-redemptive value: She conceived, brought forth, and nourished Christ. She presented him to the Father in the temple, shared her Son’s sufferings as he died on the Cross. Thus, in a wholly singular way, she cooperated by her obedience, faith, hope and burning charity in the work of the Saviour in restoring supernatural life to souls.[^c14-54]

But it was iuxta crucem Iesu, next to the Cross of Jesus, that Mary principally carried out her co-redemptive mission. There she stood,[^c14-55] in keeping with the divine plan, enduring with her only-begotten Son the intensity of his suffering, associating herself with his sacrifice in her mother’s heart, and lovingly consenting to the immolation of this Victim born of her.[^c14-56] The Church’s Magisterium states: Mary suffered and, as it were, nearly died with her suffering Son; for the salvation of mankind she renounced her mother’s rights and, as far as it depended on her, offered her Son to placate divine justice; so we may well say that she with Christ redeemed mankind.[^c14-57]

Our Father reflected on this truth for us many times: I want to tell you few things, but very clearly. One is that, to reach the Blessed Trinity, I go through Mary, and through Mary I reach Jesus. Who better can teach us to love God than this Queen, this Lady, this Mother, who is more intimately related to the Trinity: Daughter of God the Father, Mother of God the Son, Spouse of God the Holy Spirit? Greater than her, only God!
